### **Preface** 

ATP 6-02.53 is the primary doctrine for tactical radios and tactical radio networks. This publication describes the non-prescriptive methods to perform missions, functions, and tasks for the employment of tactical radios and tactical radio networks to support every warfighting function and enable command and control of forces. This publication includes doctrine on new communications and networking capabilities. It addresses the employment of interdependent and interoperable tactical radios. 

The principal audience for ATP 6-02.53 is leaders, staffs, supervisors, planners, and radio operators responsible for operating tactical radios or employing tactical radio networks at echelons corps and below. This ATP is a standard reference for system planners. It provides network planning guidance, interoperability considerations, and equipment capabilities. Commanders and staffs of Army headquarters serving as joint task force or multinational headquarters should also refer to applicable joint or multinational doctrine concerning the range of military operations and joint or multinational forces. Trainers and educators throughout the Army will also use this publication. 

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates ensure that their decisions and actions comply with applicable United States, international, and in some cases host-nation laws and regulations. Commanders at all levels ensure that their Soldiers operate in accordance with the law of armed conflict and the rules of engagement (see FM 6-27). They also adhere to Army Ethic as described in ADP 6-22. 

ATP 6-02.53 implements the following interoperability agreements: 

STANAG 4204, _Technical Standards for Single Channel VHF Radio Equipment_ (Edition 3). 

STANAG 4372, SATURN – _A Fast Frequency Hopping EPM Mode for UHF Radio_ (Edition 4). 

STANAG 4538, _Technical Standards for an Automated Radio Control System (ARCS) for HF communication Links_ (Edition 1). 

This publication uses joint terms where applicable. Selected joint and Army terms and definitions appear in both the glossary and the text. Terms for which ATP 6-02.53 is the proponent publication (the authority) are italicized in the text and are marked with an asterisk (*) in the glossary. Terms and definitions for which ATP 6-02.53 is the proponent publication are boldfaced in the text. For other definitions shown in the text, the term is italicized, and the number of the proponent publication follows the definition. 

This publication applies to the Active Army, Army National Guard/Army National Guard of the United States, and United States Army Reserve unless otherwise stated. 

The proponent of ATP 6-02.53 is the United States Army Cyber Center of Excellence. The preparing agency is Doctrine Division, United States Army Cyber Center of Excellence. Send comments and recommendations on DA Form 2028 ( _Recommended Changes to Publications and Blank Forms_ ) to Commander, United States Army Cyber Center of Excellence, ATZH-OPD (ATP 6-02.53), 419 B Street, Fort Gordon, GA 30905-5735, or e-mail to <u>usarmy.gordon.cyber-coe.mbx.gord-fg-doctrine@army.mil.</u>

### **Introduction** 

ATP 6-02.53 expands on the foundational information about tactical radios and tactical radio networks in FM 6-02. Tactical radios are critical command and control enablers. Tactical radio networks empower subordinate decision making and allow integration and synchronization across all domains. Tactical radios and networks support the Army’s mission across the competition continuum. 

Fluid tactical situations in sustained operations require versatile and adaptable tactical radio networks. Tactical radios allow forces to move and communicate from higher echelons down to the squad level. Squadlevel radios enable communication and situational awareness at the lowest level possible. 

This publication contains important developments regarding tactical radio operations with greater emphasis on the employment of tactical radio retransmission at echelons corps and below. 

ATP 6-02.53 has three chapters and nine appendixes: 

- **Chapter 1** discusses the operational environment and examines challenges in a contested and congested environment. It includes an explanation of how tactical networks and radios enable command and control and situational awareness during Army operations. 

- **Chapter 2** addresses the employment of tactical radios and signal support by Army echelon. It also addresses the importance of integrating tactical radios into command posts. 

- **Chapter 3** introduces network planning and explains planning requirements and considerations for secure tactical radio operations. This chapter provides an overview of retransmission planning and employment and includes retransmission pre-combat checks and pre-combat inspections. This chapter also describes communications staff elements—planners, spectrum managers, and communications security account managers—and the importance of coordinated activities among staff elements during the planning process. 

- **Appendix A** includes information on how to identify and respond to electromagnetic attack. It addresses techniques to overcome threat capabilities including information on how to report and prevent the effects of suspected electromagnetic attack. This appendix also describes types of electromagnetic interference and appropriate actions to minimize the impact to operations or friendly communications. 

- **Appendix B** presents training and certification standards for retransmission teams. This appendix also contains signal assessment tables, battle drills, and team drills to include assigned tasks supporting the unit’s mission. 

- **Appendix C** addresses the combatant command communications team and their responsibility in providing the combatant commander and theater army commander secure, reliable communications support. 

- **Appendix D** discusses aspects of waveform and waveform application functional components within the tactical networking environment. It also describes the properties of radio waves and wave modulation. 

- **Appendix E** describes high frequency radio networks and how they support command and control. It also discusses establishing high frequency radio networks and how propagation, antenna choice, and site selection affect signal transmission. Included in this appendix is information on how each echelon uses high frequency radio networks to enable communications and fulfill mission objectives during operations. 

- **Appendix F** addresses the Julian day, synchronization time, and ZULU time. It also includes a sample time zone conversion chart and world time zone map. 

- **Appendix G** addresses radio operations in adverse weather conditions. Included in this appendix is information on techniques for better communication including how radio system maintenance based on environmental factors enhances radio capabilities and ensures successful communication. 

- **Appendix H** provides information on antenna selection and techniques with examples of field repair of antennas and antenna support equipment. This appendix also contains important concepts and 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**vii** 

**Introduction** 

   - terms beneficial to understanding tactical radio operations and implementing effective tactical radio communications. 

- **Appendix I** discusses tactical radios and radio platforms. Also addressed are enhanced capabilities and interoperability features for handheld, manpack, vehicle-mounted, and airborne radios. This appendix includes information on the integrated tactical network, a key enabler for robust communication for units facing disconnected, intermittent, or limited communication.

**viii** 

##### **Chapter 1** 

### **Overview** 

_A piece of paper makes you an officer, a radio makes you a commander._ 

General of the Army Omar N. Bradley 

This chapter discusses the operational environment and challenges encountered in a congested and contested environment. It includes information on tactical radio fundamentals covering the topic of radios in Army operations, common tactical radio frequency bands, and capabilities. The chapter concludes with a brief overview of Army and joint networks. 

##### **SECTION I – OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT AND THREAT** 

1-1. Commanders and their staffs seek to develop and maintain an understanding of their operational environment and its effects across the competition continuum. A thorough understanding of the operational environment helps identify opportunities to coordinate with mission partners and converge effects when operating throughout a multidomain battlespace. 

1-2. Threats are a fundamental part of an overall operational environment. For mission success, commanders and staffs must identify potential threats—both state and non-state actors—within the context of an operational environment that might disrupt, destroy, or intercept the flow of information to command and control systems and Army communications networks. 

#### **THE OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT** 

1-3. Army forces conduct operations in specific operational environments. An _operational environment_ is the aggregate of the conditions, circumstances, and influences that affect the employment of capabilities and bear on the decisions of the commander (JP 3-0). The operational environment encompasses the physical areas of the air, land, maritime, and space domains, as well as cyberspace and the electromagnetic spectrum (EMS). Signal leaders must understand the available communications capabilities and interoperability challenges of communications support in each domain. 

1-4. Signal formations’ delivery of communications services varies based on the mission. As commanders operate across the competition continuum, signal capabilities must also be tailorable to meet the commander’s requirements. Commanders rely on signal leaders to clearly articulate available communications capabilities to support operations and accomplish the mission. 

##### **CONTESTED ENVIRONMENT** 

1-5. Communications and information networks are continually targeted by threats. Threats understand the extent of U.S. forces’ reliance on communications and automated information systems. Consequently, threats will contest the operational environment and seek to diminish the effectiveness of U.S. and allied forces’ communications networks. Threats will deliberately attempt to deny, degrade, compromise, neutralize, and destroy friendly use of the EMS, space, and cyberspace capabilities, including terrestrial communications systems. 

1-6. Successfully integrating signal support with electromagnetic warfare and intelligence capabilities is essential to obtaining and maintaining freedom of action in the operating environment while denying the same to adversaries. Synchronizing capabilities across multiple domains and warfighting functions enables command and control and mitigates the threat’s ability to disrupt tactical radio networks. 

##### **CONGESTED ENVIRONMENT** 

1-7. Competition for available bandwidth results in a congested EMS Army elements and supporting agencies depend on the EMS for communications and information collection, and electromagnetic warfare capabilities to support operations in the air, land, maritime, space, and cyberspace domains. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**1** 

**Chapter 1** 

1-8. Signal systems rely on the EMS for network transport. Gaining and maintaining access to the EMS is critical for signal support to operations. Signal elements plan communications and network capabilities to support all anticipated requirements in their assigned areas. In a congested operational environment, however, there may not be adequate spectrum availability to support all missions. Signal leaders must clearly articulate the limitations and expected level of degradation, so commanders can make appropriate risk decisions and align the available capabilities with their priorities. In addition, signal leaders should incorporate a primary, alternate, contingency, and emergency (PACE) communication plan to establish redundant communications means if the primary capability is unavailable. See chapter 3 for additional information on PACE communication plans and planning considerations. 

1-9. Units that use the full extent of their communications capabilities during training improve their ability to understand conditions, circumstances, and influences impacting the electromagnetic operational environment. Signal planners should leverage resources within their support channels during unit training and exercises to include capabilities, expertise, and tools to further maximize performance effectiveness during mission execution. Implementing realistic training scenarios fosters proficiency enabling team members to achieve greater accuracy in characterizing congested environments. 

#### **FREEDOM OF MANEUVER** 

1-10. Maintaining dominance is increasingly dependent upon the Army’s ability to establish and maintain freedom of maneuver within cyberspace and the EMS. Advances in technology and a rise in the number of spectrum-dependent devices used by nonmilitary and military entities have resulted in greater competition for usable bandwidth and an exponential increase in electromagnetic emitters within the electromagnetic operational environment. The _electromagnetic operational environment_ is a composite of the actual and potential electromagnetic energy radiation, conditions, circumstances, and influences that affect the employment of capabilities and the decisions of the commander (JP 3-85). 

1-11. The EMS spans every domain in the operational environment; threats attempt to deny friendly ability to use it successfully. Disruptions present significant challenges that threaten the Army’s ability to successfully accomplish objectives and mission success. For forces to effectively operate in an increasingly congested and contested environment, signal soldiers must identify malicious activity that compromises network integrity and implement techniques to secure critical communications networks and data. 

1-12. Through surveillance and target acquisition, threats attempt to deny U.S. forces the use of EMS, thus compromising tactical network access, disrupting radio networks, and preventing electromagnetic signature characterization efforts of the U.S. and its mission partners. Threats with intent to defeat U.S. forces target key national assets within the United States and abroad using technical ingenuity and electromagnetic warfare equipment with enhanced detection, interception, and geolocation capabilities. 

1-13. Signal soldiers and planners must maintain network trust, reliability, and resilience across the EMS. Techniques offering identity and location protection prevent attacks based on distinct signature, enhances force protection, and enables network planners and spectrum managers to develop actionable mission planning strategies to improve Soldier emission awareness and enable leader decision making. 

1-14. Threat capabilities specific to tactical radio operations include systems designed to physically destroy U.S communications equipment. To increase their understanding of threat capabilities used against spectrumdependent devices and electrical equipment, U.S. forces align cyberspace electromagnetic activities (CEMA) and spectrum management operations to enhance spectrum efficiency, maximize spectrum compatibility, and ensure EMS superiority against current and potential threats. U.S. forces also focus on integration, interoperability, and collaboration with Federal departments and agencies, allies, and mission partners to further synchronize coalition activities, increase EMS capability and capacity, and deter strategic competition. The three distinct divisions of electromagnetic warfare—electromagnetic attack, electromagnetic protection, and electromagnetic support—aid in creating force dominance while spectrum management operations prevent unacceptable interference and enable freedom of action in and through the EMS. Refer to ATP 3-12.3 for more information about electromagnetic warfare techniques. 

1-15. Every proactive measure taken by U.S. forces to prevent signal interception and communications systems discovery results in increased Soldiers situational understanding and awareness of their own radio emissions when operating in an increasingly complex, densely populated electromagnetic operational 

**2** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Overview** 

environment. To mitigate activities that place the mission at risk and deter threats directly impacting tactical radio communications and user operations, signal personnel apply electromagnetic protection measures to protect, detect, characterize, counter, and mitigate specific effects. By taking measures to prevent signal detection, location, and emanation, command post survivability is enhanced and the efforts by attackers to discover and target key infrastructure resource are minimized or defeated. Implementing offensive and defensive actions, active and passive countermeasures, and decoying techniques and capabilities also offsets EMS operating environment constraints and allows sufficient freedom of action to carry out mission objectives successfully. See appendix A for signal threats and how to recognize and respond to spectrum interference. 

#### **THREAT** 

1-16. A _threat_ is any combination of actors, entities, or forces that have the capability and intent to harm United States forces, United States national interests, or the homeland (ADP 3-0). Threats can be categorized as adversaries, enemies, or insider threats. An _adversary_ is a party acknowledged as potentially hostile to a friendly party and against which the use of force may be envisaged (JP 3-0). An _enemy_ is a party identified as hostile against which the use of force is authorized (ADP 3-0). Insider threats present unique challenges because they are trusted individuals with access to Army capabilities and sensitive operational information. Threats may include individuals or groups, organized, or not organized. 

1-17. Threats are a fundamental part of an overall operational environment. A threat actor can disrupt, destroy, or intercept the flow of information while operating from the other side of the world. Threat actors use an array of capabilities and methods to threaten Army systems and networks. 

#### **THREAT EFFECTS ON SIGNAL SUPPORT** 

1-18. Based on the system and capabilities and the spectrum management practices implemented, commanders project power through delivery of effects. Threat effects created by spectrum-enabled systems focus on operations for the purposes of destruction, degradation, disruption, and deception. Hostile entities target key communications capabilities, nodes, sensors, and networks including on-orbit, link, and terrestrial segments of U.S. positioning, navigation, and timing (PNT) satellites creating potentially significant impacts across all warfighting functions and weapon platforms. 

1-19. Executing successful operations requires U.S. forces to effectively neutralize threat activities in the EMS and distinguish threat and friendly capabilities. To deter and combat threats that directly impact tactical radio communications and user operations, signal personnel coordinate effects and implement risk mitigation activities or techniques. Defensive measures include employing— 

- Radar jamming technology. 

- Self-propelled and manual decoys. 

- Reflective materiel or objects such as metallic strips or aluminum-coated tape used to— 

   - Reflect frequencies. 

   - Cause signal interference. 

   - Clutter the radar with false targets. 

   - Re-radiate radar energy toward the originating source. 

- Other actions include using— 

   - Emission control (EMCON). 

   - Masking and network countermeasures. 

1-20. These measures prevent the threat’s effectiveness or either degrade or neutralize friendly capabilities creating impacting, incapacitating hazards in the form of electromagnetic interference, radiation, system and platform capability issues, and environmental and personnel hazards. 

1-21. Tactical radio networks also encounter exposure to a broad range of peer threats with signals intelligence, electromagnetic warfare, electromagnetic attack, and cyberspace capabilities. For successful electromagnetic and cyberspace warfare operations, signal personnel combine electromagnetic warfare jamming techniques with surveillance and eavesdropping techniques including signals intelligence designed to locate threat transmissions. Working in concert with signals intelligence counterparts and Soldiers in the CEMA section, signal planners and support elements extract and assess signals technical data correlating 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**3** 

**Chapter 1** 

unidentified signals of interest to threat emitters. Their collaborative efforts ensure appropriate defense mechanisms are implemented to maintain continued access to communications networks and capabilities. 

1-22. Preventive measures that align key operations of signals intelligence, electromagnetic attack, and cyberspace operations help to secure the network, build network trust, and guarantee the ability to quickly adjust capabilities based on mission needs and threat actions. Operations and planning efforts are further maximized by implementing electromagnetic warfare concepts and incorporating appropriate tactics, techniques, and procedures at all training levels—individual, collective, institutional, and operational. 

##### **SECTION II – TACTICAL RADIO FUNDAMENTALS** 

1-23. This section covers tactical radio communications including common frequency bands and radio assets available to signal elements for use in Army operations. It also contains a general description of tactical radios and radio capabilities. 

#### **TACTICAL RADIOS IN ARMY OPERATIONS** 

1-24. Tactical radio systems are the primary means for Soldiers to communicate during all phases of Army operations. Squad leaders and higher use tactical radios to communicate and share a common view of the operational environment. Tactical radios provide Army Soldiers the ability to send and receive voice, data, and video communication and may include commercial off-the-shelf radios. The systems use high frequency (HF), ultrahigh frequency (UHF), and very high frequency (VHF) bands. 

1-25. Army forces employ a variety of tactical radios and waveforms. Each system has unique capabilities and characteristics. Signal planners determine how to employ each system depending on the mission. 

#### **COMMON TACTICAL RADIO FREQUENCY BANDS** 

1-26. Radio frequency (RF) waves are a subcomponent of the EMS, a continuum of all electromagnetic waves arranged according to frequency and wavelength. The most common RF ranges for tactical radios are— 

- HF (3–30 MHz). 

- VHF (30–300 MHz). 

- UHF (300–3,000 MHz). 

1-27. The electromagnetic waves within each range have different characteristics that are significant in determining their practical application. A wide variety of tactical radios support communications through several configurations including airborne, maritime, terrestrial fixed station, vehicular, handheld, and manpack. 

#### **TACTICAL RADIO CAPABILITIES** 

1-28. Army forces employ a variety of portable, lightweight tactical radios and waveforms. The type of tactical radio system chosen for employment depends on mission requirements. Tactical radio capabilities include— 

- Automatic link establishment (ALE). 

- Anti-jamming. 

- Beyond line of sight. 

- Data rates. 

- Global Positioning System (GPS). 

- Programmable (software defined). 

- Secure communications. 

##### **SECTION III – NETWORK OVERVIEW** 

1-29. Networks are key enablers of successful operations. They consist of end-user applications, information services and data, and network transport and management devices and systems. Network capabilities enable 

**4** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Overview** 

leaders to receive, process, and disseminate information supporting coordinated planning, preparation, execution, and synchronization with global mission partners. 

1-30. Networks are one of four components of a command and control system established by commanders at every echelon to support decision making, facilitate communication of mission objectives, and enable execution of command and control across all warfighting domains. The command and control system, including people, processes, command posts, and networks, are arranged to provide continuity of command and allow integration and synchronization of combat power during sustained operations. 

1-31. Along with networks, commanders have access to a collection of communications network resources and systems supporting each warfighting function to include tactical radios, data networks, command and control information systems, and on-the-move packages. For a detailed description of networks supporting each warfighting function, refer to FM 6-02. 

#### **JOINT NETWORK** 

1-32. Networks provide the connectivity between Army forces and its mission partners in a joint operations area. Joint forces depend on the Department of Defense information network (DODIN) to connect strategic, operational, and tactical commanders across the globe. 

1-33. The _Department of Defense information network_ is the information capabilities and associated processes owned or leased by the Department of Defense for collecting, processing, storing, disseminating, and managing information, whether interconnected or stand-alone (JP 6-0). The DODIN includes owned and leased communications and computing systems and services, software (including applications), data, security services, other associated services, and national security systems. 

1-34. The DODIN supports military operations by enabling seamless and secure information sharing It encompasses all DOD cyberspace—Army, Navy, Air Force, Marine Corps, and Space Force Service-specific enclaves as well as joint capabilities provided by the Defense Information System Agency. 

#### **ARMY NETWORK** 

1-35. The _Department of Defense information network-Army_ (DODIN-A) is an Army-operated enclave of the Department of Defense information network that encompasses all Army information capabilities that collect, process, store, display, disseminate, and protect information worldwide (ATP 6-02.71). The DODINA and the services it extends enable collaboration and synchronization across all domains and among mission partners. 

1-36. The DODIN-A provides strategic voice and data capabilities and prepositioned connection points to extend communications services to geographically dispersed personnel, both fixed station and deployed expeditionary forces. 

1-37. As part of the command and control system, the network provides the infrastructure for voice, data, and video. The DODIN-A includes tactical radio networks supporting intelligence, sustainment, special operations forces, and medical. These networks enhance situational understanding and enable resource and information sharing and collaboration among units, staff, and support personnel to support the commander’s intent and allow informed decision making. 

##### **TACTICAL RADIOS** 

1-38. The primary function of tactical radio networks is to provide voice communication and situational awareness at all echelons, across all phases of operations. Tactical radio networks enable command and control, communications, information sharing, and coordination between higher and lower echelons as well as adjacent units. Tactical radio networks support command post operations and command and control of mounted and dismounted forces operating while either static or on the move. They enable air-ground communications with Army and joint aviation platforms and other combat enablers such as fires, casualty evacuation, and sustainment. Tactical radio networks assume a secondary support role for data transmission where other data capabilities do not exist. The tactical radio network includes— 

- Combat net radios. 

- Vehicular and dismounted radios. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**5** 

**Chapter 1** 

- Joint Battle Command Platform. 

- Nett Warrior. 

- Blue Force Tracker (I, II, and III). 

- Mobile User Objective System (MUOS). 

- Integrated Tactical Network (ITN). 

###### **Integrated Tactical Network** 

1-39. The ITN provides an independent mobile network solution supporting command and control, situational awareness, and air-to-ground integration. The ITN is not a single system or program of record, but a combination of developing tactical network capabilities augmented with emerging commercial and nondevelopmental items, capabilities, and technologies. ITN capabilities provide echelons brigade and below with smaller, lighter, faster, and more flexible communications systems. With the fielding of the ITN, new commercial components, network transport capabilities, applications, and devices are introduced into the Army’s tactical network environment through network baselines. The network baselines focus on infantry, Stryker, and armored brigade combat teams. ITN capabilities include the ability to enable multipath network diversity using the tactical scalable mobile ad hoc network (TSM) commercial waveforms. This feature enables highly mobile operations and provides tactical agility for units facing disconnected, intermittent, or limited communications. The ITN offers enhanced communications resiliency and security in a contested or congested environment. 

###### **Tactical Radio Networks** 

1-40. Tactical radios enable every mission, from training the force to executing tactical tasks to influence the operational environment in large-scale combat operations. Radios enable secure transmission of real-time tactical information and provide connectivity to designated communications networks or stations. 

1-41. Army forces rely on tactical radio networks to enable communications, situational understanding, and decision making. Tactical radio networks commonly used by maneuver elements include— 

- Command. 

- Sustainment. 

- Operations and intelligence. 

- Medical. 

- Fires. 

- • Surveillance. 

1-42. A communications network is an organization of stations capable of intercommunications, but not necessarily on the same channel. Table 1-1 on pages 7 through 9 shows typical tactical radio networks employed at echelons corps and below and the network stations established within each network. The networks shown merely serve as a guide for establishing radio networks. The actual networks established depend on the existing situation, command guidance, and equipment available.

##### **COMMAND NETWORK** 

1-43. Command networks are protected communications networks supporting commanders’ requirements to send and receive critical information during operations. Command networks link adjacent, higher, and lower echelons of command. The single-channel ground and airborne radio system (SINCGARS) is the 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**9** 

**Chapter 1** 

predominant means of operating command networks, and is a prevalent communications means for tactical operations at echelons division and below. Additional means of communication supporting command networks may include— 

- HF. 

- Narrowband (single-channel) tactical satellite (TACSAT). 

- MUOS. 

- Networking waveforms. 

   - TSM. 

   - Warrior Robust Enhanced Network (WREN). 

1-44. Planners must ensure that radios use an approved cryptological key or encryption device. The primary radio source is determined based on warfighting function considerations and the mission variables of mission, enemy, terrain and weather, troops and support available, time available, civil considerations, and informational considerations—METT-TC (I) criteria as documented in the PACE plan published in Annex H of the operation order. 

1-45. Units establish and rehearse a PACE plan to ensure a viable alternate, contingency, and emergency means of communication is available for communication between team members should the primary means of communication fail. A PACE plan designates the order in which an element will transition to redundant communications means in the event of threat attack or network failure. PACE plan rehearsals should include the commander’s priorities for allocation of bandwidth and order of restoration when communications links are denied or degraded. 

1-46. When necessary, units use retransmission teams to extend the range of the command network and overcome communication obstacles between higher and lower units. Command networks have the highest installation priority. Figure 1-1 on page 11 is an example of the structure of a typical command network. 

**10** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Overview** 



**Figure 1-1. Typical command network structure** 

###### **Sustainment Network** 

1-47. The sustainment network is a dedicated network used by commanders and staff officers during training exercises, sustainment operations, or military engagements. Its purpose is status reporting of sustainment, logistics, and administrative information to enable unit survivability and to ensure U.S. forces possess the supplies, personnel, medical, and force protection needed during tactical engagements. The sustainment network also facilitates horizontal communication with units or subordinates and provides leaders connectivity to their next higher echelon for situational awareness and coordinated operational objectives. An existing sustainment network may provide infiltration routes and information related to sources of supply or transportation. Figure 1-2 on page 12 is an example of a brigade sustainment network. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**11** 

**Chapter 1** 



**Figure 1-2. Example of a brigade sustainment network** 

###### **Operations and Intelligence Network** 

1-48. Operations and intelligence networks enable the transmission of observations, details, and discussions that feed intelligence analysis. The G-2 or S-2 controls operations and intelligence networks. Subordinate elements may monitor the operations and intelligence networks to develop situational awareness. Operations and intelligence networks usually include transmission and reception of surveillance reports related to enemy movement and disposition. A unit information control center may also use this network to coordinate and control surveillance capabilities and sensor teams. Subordinate elements may monitor the operations and intelligence network to develop situational awareness. Each echelon’s intelligence section resides on the operations and intelligence networks of their higher echelon allowing reporting and pertinent information exchange. The information transmitted over operations and intelligence networks may require a secondary network to prevent network overloading. Figure 1-3 on page 13 is an example operations and intelligence network. 

**12** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Overview** 



**Figure 1-3. Example of an operations and intelligence network** 

###### **Medical Network** 

1-49. The medical network provides internal collaboration and support to tactical units to include coordinated Army Health System support in the form of health service support and force health protection. Health service support includes medical evacuations, medical treatments, hospitalization, and medical logistics medical functions. Force health protection encompasses operational public health, veterinary services, combat and operational stress control, dental services, and laboratory services; and may include the collection of medical intelligence. Figure 1-4 on page 14 is an example of a medical network. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**13** 

**Chapter 1** 



**Figure 1-4. Example medical network** 

###### **Fires Network** 

1-50. The fires network is the highest priority network for fires units. The fires network enables the exchange of technical and firing data. The fires network enables leaders to plan and execute the delivery of lethal and nonlethal fires, provides situational awareness, and enhances collaboration and information exchange among fires personnel. Fire support teams use the battalion fires network to send calls for fires based on assigned priorities. Retransmission teams support the fires network as required. 

###### **Surveillance Network** 

1-51. Surveillance networks enable the transmission and reception of reports related to enemy movement and massing. The battalion battlefield information control center establishes this network to coordinate and control the ground surveillance radar and unattended ground sensor teams. The information from this network is vital to commanders and given high priority for activation.

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

##### **Chapter 2** 

### **Tactical Radio Employment by Echelon** 

This chapter addresses the employment of tactical radios at various Army echelons. Tactical radios support all warfighting functions at all echelons across the competition continuum. 

#### **SIGNAL SUPPORT BY ECHELON** 

2-1. A thorough understanding of support requirements allows signal elements to plan, implement, and employ networks to enable information exchange. Signal support teams are resourced with sufficient assets to enable reliable functioning of communications systems and capabilities within command posts at all echelons. Signal soldiers ensure command posts have adequate communications connectivity at each echelon by— 

- Assessing communications requirements for all warfighting functions. 

- Determining capabilities and limitations of available communications systems. 

- Determining potential joint, interorganizational, and multinational communications requirements. 

- Performing a detailed line of sight analysis. 

- Implementing a PACE communication plan. 

- Integrating all available signal assets. 

- Developing a method of deployment so assets are sequenced to coincide with the arrival of forces. 

- Mapping the locations of all communications systems. 

- Implementing retransmission, digital network links, and node placement. 

- Assessing spectrum requirements for emitters, sensors, radars, or any other spectrum-dependent devices. 

- Assessing initial requirements and implementing flexibility for expected changes. 

- Implementing the standard operating procedure (SOP) and communications security (COMSEC) procedures. 

- Conducting communications rehearsals. 

- Assessing the electromagnetic operational environment. 

- Identifying critical vulnerabilities to be exploited by threats. 

- Developing risk assessment for the commander. 

#### **ROLES AND RESPONSIBILITIES** 

2-2. Signal elements and capabilities exist to meet the commander’s information requirements and enable control of forces. Signal teams at each echelon provide communications connectivity within their assigned area to support continuous operations and collaboration between higher, subordinate, and adjacent elements. 

2-3. Tactical radios provide communications and network support at the corps and division levels; echelons brigade, battalions, company, and platoon employ tactical radios to collaborate with adjacent units and higher reporting elements. Maneuver elements have limited organic signal capabilities consisting primarily of single-channel secure radios down to squad level. Their communications systems are mostly general purpose, user-operated equipment. Brigade units rely on tactical radio retransmission networks to extend the effective range of line of sight communication when executing tactical enabling tasks beyond the range of VHF singlechannel radios, or when terrain prevents line of sight communications. 

##### **CORPS AND DIVISION G-6** 

2-4. Signal staffs plan communications and network capabilities to support all anticipated requirements in their assigned area. Signal leaders and staff work closely to align available capabilities with the commander’s priorities. They must clearly articulate the limitations and expected level of degradation so commanders can make appropriate risk decisions. 

2-5. The G-6 coordinates with the G-3 to direct the action and movement of signal elements to support operations through the orders process. Additional G-6 responsibilities relevant to tactical radios include— 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**15** 

**Chapter 2** 

- Developing the scheme of signal support through the military decision-making process, including the PACE communication plan for each phase of the operation. 

- Planning COMSEC. 

- Planning tactical radio networks and retransmission. 

- Conducting spectrum management operations. 

- Coordinate with the space operations officer to prioritize satellite communications (SATCOM) links used for monitoring. 

- Deconflicting spectrum usage with the CEMA section. 

- Overseeing signal maintenance and logistical readiness of tactical radio equipment. 

- Providing tactical radio training management and support to units, as required. 

##### **CORPS AND DIVISION SIGNAL, INTELLIGENCE, AND SUSTAINMENT COMPANY** 

2-6. Operating under the technical control of the G-6, the signal portion of the corps and division signal, intelligence, and sustainment companies provides organic signal capabilities including tactical radio retransmission for communication between higher, adjacent, and subordinate elements. 

###### **Capabilities** 

2-7. Organic signal capabilities are similarly employed by both the corps and the division signal, intelligence, and sustainment company to support operations within their specific echelon. These capabilities support intra-network communication between upper and lower echelons and data fusion into a shareable common operational picture. 

- The corps employs— 

   - HF radios. 

   - VHF radios. 

   - Narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT. 

- The division employs— 

   - HF radios. 

   - SINCGARS. 

   - VHF radios. 

   - Narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT. 

   - MUOS. 

2-8. Tactical radios and signal capabilities enable secure transmission, assured command and control, and multipath diversity. Assured command and control enables commanders to send, receive, and display information in near-real time for rapid decision-making. Multipath diversity supports transport flexibility allowing multiple communications solutions and routing path options to expand the Army’s network capability. 

2-9. Integrating these enabling capabilities is critical to uninterrupted global tactical communications transport and enhances Force employment, lethality, and effectiveness in accomplishing mission objectives. By leveraging multiple communications pathways commanders take advantage of secure information exchange using both commercial and military transport without self-limiting units to encrypted-only networks and have wider network environment options in which data can be categorized and transmitted according to its classification. 

###### **Roles and Responsibilities** 

2-10. Additional responsibilities relevant to tactical radios at echelons corps and division are— 

- Coordinating with the division G-6 spectrum manager for frequency assignments prior to operations. 

- Collaborating with G-6 to ensure the availability of communications resources and capabilities. 

- Establishing narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT communication. 

- Establishing retransmission networks and sites to support beyond line of sight communications. 

**16** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Tactical Radio Employment by Echelon** 

##### **BRIGADE COMBAT TEAM S-6** 

2-11. The S-6 interacts closely with the deputy commander, S-3, and other staff sections to define communications requirements and command post locations through the military decision-making process. The S-6 directs planning and coordination for tactical radio networks, spectrum management operations, retransmission, and COMSEC. To ensure adequate network support, the S-6 collaborates with higher, subordinate, and adjacent units; and recommends network changes, as needed, to support the commander’s intent. The brigade S-6 coordinates with the S-3 to direct the actions and movements of signal company elements to support operations through the orders process. 

###### **Capabilities** 

2-12. The organic signal assets of the brigade combat team support continuous operations to sustain the brigade’s communications networks. Signal capabilities not provided by organic signal elements are requested from division. Brigade combat teams employ— 

- VHF. 

- Narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT and MUOS. 

- HF. 

- SINCGARS. 

- Blue Force Tracker (Joint Battle Command Platform) 

- Tactical networking waveforms. 

   - TSM. 

   - WREN. 

###### **Roles and Responsibilities** 

2-13. The S-6 prepares the PACE plan and exercises technical control over the brigade signal company in executing the plan. Their responsibility includes maintaining the running estimate of communications capabilities and providing signal support plans to build and configure the brigade’s communications capabilities. Additional roles and responsibilities of the S-6 for tactical radio networks include— 

- Developing the scheme of signal support through the military decision-making process, including PACE plans for each phase of the operation. 

- Coordinating spectrum management. 

- Deconflicting spectrum usage with the CEMA section. 

- COMSEC account management. 

- Planning retransmission operations. 

- Overseeing maintenance and logistical readiness of tactical radios. 

##### **BRIGADE SIGNAL COMPANY** 

2-14. The brigade signal company provides voice and data retransmission capabilities. Retransmission teams extend the brigade’s tactical radio network within the brigade’s assigned area. 

###### **Capabilities** 

2-15. Brigade combat teams employ combat net radios to enable communications exchange and situational awareness with the next higher echelon and adjacent units and rely on single-channel communications capabilities to communicate with maneuver elements. Tactical radio systems are employed for mobile and dismounted communications. Tactical radio retransmission extends communications range beyond line of sight. 

###### **Roles and Responsibilities** 

2-16. Operating under the technical control of the brigade S-6, the brigade signal company provides communications and information support at the brigade level. Additional roles and responsibilities of the brigade signal company include— 

- Establishing tactical radio retransmission sites. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**17** 

**Chapter 2** 

- Establishing narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT at the brigade. 

##### **BATTALION S-6** 

2-17. The S-6 interacts closely with the executive officer, S-3, and other staff sections to define communications requirements at command post locations through the military decision-making process. They consult with higher, lower, and adjacent headquarters to ensure effective communications throughout the assigned area. The S-6 should maintain a close working relationship with the commander and battalion S-3 to ensure the communication plan supports the commander’s intent. 

###### **Capabilities** 

2-18. Handheld and manpack radios are the primary communications capabilities employed by the maneuver battalions and companies. These capabilities allow Soldiers to share information across the squad, platoon, and company up to higher headquarters. The battalion’s organic tactical radio capabilities are the main means of communications for maneuver elements away from the command posts. 

###### **Roles and Responsibilities** 

2-19. The primary role of the S-6 is to support the commander’s communication requirements by providing advice to ensure effective communications connectivity within their assigned area. Additional roles and responsibilities of the battalion S-6 include— 

- Managing tactical radio networks. 

- Assisting the S-3 in identifying user requirements. 

- Maintaining the signal running estimate. 

- Creating the PACE plan. 

- Integrating and managing communications resources at battalion and subordinate companies. 

- Coordinating spectrum management for the battalion’s communications systems. 

#### **TACTICAL RADIO RETRANSMISSION AND RANGE EXTENSION** 

2-20. Retransmission allows bridging between separate radio nets and modes of operation (example frequency hopping to single-channel) and may be used to support mission partner networking or as part of a counter electromagnetic warfare plan. Successful retransmission operations requires retransmission teams to anticipate emerging communications requirements in order to maintain a fully operational network to support a commander’s evolving mission. 

2-21. Signal teams ensure adequate coverage of the assigned area by performing a physical reconnaissance of the proposed retransmission site when possible and a full rehearsal to build team cohesion and competence in executing retransmission operations. See chapter 3 for retransmission planning and appendix B for retransmission training and certification. 

##### **RETRANSMISSION TEAMS** 

2-22. Corps, division, and brigade elements use retransmission when executing tactical enabling tasks beyond the range of VHF single-channel radios, or when terrain prevents line of sight communications. Retransmission capabilities used for communications between units and command posts include singlechannel HF radios, narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT radios, two channel manpack cross banding, and tactical messaging. A typical retransmission team has two crew members and the team chief. Two retransmission teams are assigned for echelons corps and brigade. Division elements are authorized up to three retransmission teams. 

##### **RETRANSMISSION EMPLOYMENT** 

2-23. Retransmission sites support maneuver forces by increasing the effective range between radio stations without increasing transmit power. Signal staffs employ retransmission teams to provide complete coverage of the assigned area. Platoon sergeants and platoon leaders conduct pre-combat checks and inspections prior to each mission to ensure tactical responsiveness and support maneuver operations. 

**18** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Tactical Radio Employment by Echelon** 

##### **SITE SELECTION** 

2-24. Proposed site selections are based on G-6 or S-6 signal site analysis. The G-6 or S-6 recommends signal site locations for each proposed course of action during the military decision-making process. Refer to FM 6-02 for detailed information about signal site selection. 

2-25. Selecting the right site location is critical to retransmission team operations and survivability. Poor site placement can result in limited flexibility, limited mobility, threat detection, degraded survivability, and reduced effectiveness. Teams establish and implement contingency plans for alternate site locations considering security and defense requirements. See chapter 3 for additional planning considerations for tactical radio retransmission. 

##### **PRE-COMBAT CHECKS AND PRE-COMBAT INSPECTIONS** 

2-26. The platoon leader or platoon sergeant must evaluate retransmission teams’ preparation for combat. They conduct pre-combat checks to validate the teams’ readiness once the team chiefs report their teams and vehicles are prepared. Because of the difficulty of reaching and resupplying retransmission teams operating from remote sites, it is vital that the platoon leader and platoon sergeant conduct detailed pre-combat inspections of retransmission teams before teams leave the staging area. Refer to ATP 6-02.2 and GTA 1102-001 for more information about retransmission team pre-combat checks and inspection. 

##### **PRE-MISSION BRIEFING** 

2-27. The platoon leader and platoon sergeant present retransmission teams an updated mission briefing immediately before a mission to ensure team chiefs and team members understand the plan. Leaders should have the team chief back brief portions of the plan to verify their understanding of the mission and procedures. The pre-mission briefing includes— 

- Commander’s intent. 

- Convoy plan and procedures. 

- PACE plan. 

- Frequencies. 

- COMSEC. 

- Jamming plan. 

- Supported unit’s scheme of maneuver. 

- Location and role of supporting units. 

- Enemy situation— 

   - Primary enemy location—update daily. 

   - Enemy manned and unmanned aircraft. 

   - Enemy intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance capabilities. 

   - Enemy indirect fire capabilities. 

   - Most likely enemy actions. 

   - Enemy tactics, techniques, and procedures against remote sites like retransmission sites. 

   - Primary and alternate enemy avenues of approach. 

   - If the enemy requires an escort, identify escorting unit. 

   - Timeline of operations. 

   - Weather and terrain. 

   - Location of site. 

- Terrain masking techniques— 

   - Avoiding skylining. 

   - Using terrain to mask transmissions from enemy detection. 

- Primary and alternate retransmission site locations—explain movement triggers. 

- Antenna placement plan— 

   - Knowing where the enemy is in relation to the operation. 

   - Shielding transmissions from the enemy. 

- Site security and defense plan—vital and continuous. 

   - Supporting security elements. 

   - Internal security plan.

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**19** 

**Chapter 2** 

   - Site concealment and camouflage. 

   - Concertina wire. 

   - Range card and fires plan. 

- Compromise plan— 

   - Emergency destruction plan—COMSEC, documents, maps, and equipment. 

   - Escape and evasion plan. 

- Resupply plan. 

- Reporting procedures. 

##### **MOVEMENT** 

2-28. Leaders initiate movements necessary to continue mission preparation or to posture the unit for starting the mission. This step includes execution of priorities of work; movement to an assembly area, battle position, or a new operational environment; or the movement of guides or quartering parties. 

##### **SITE SETUP** 

2-29. Planning the retransmission network is a critical element in site setup. Retransmission teams, who generally work from isolated or remote positions, use network diagrams to give an overview of the proposed location and equipment. Teams conduct risk assessments to determine the likelihood of mission success at the chosen sites. Analysis of terrain explains the terrain’s effect on the mission. Enhancing protection and survivability includes identifying sites that enable reliable signal transmission plus defensible terrain that provides cover concealment and aids the team’s ability to provide site security and defense. To facilitate planning and aid in briefing subordinates, teams identify potential terrain interferences with line of sight and retransmission capabilities which may require SATCOM or HF radios for beyond line of sight communication. See chapter 3 for additional site selection considerations. 

##### **RETRANSMISSION OPERATIONS** 

2-30. Retransmission supports the scheme of maneuver and is dictated by the physical size and terrain of the assigned area. The active engagement of the G-6 or S-6 throughout the military decision-making process helps identify communications requirements early in planning and ensures the retransmission plan supports the commander’s intent and scheme of maneuver. The operation order includes specified tasks for retransmission teams. 

2-31. Retransmission is particularly critical during offensive and defensive operations when maneuver elements cannot access the communications capabilities present in the command posts. Retransmission normally requires— 

- Two tactical radios. 

- One retransmission cable. 

- Two power amplifiers. 

- Two antennas. 

- Appropriate COMSEC material. 

2-32. The tactical situation determines the type of retransmission to use. Planners may configure retransmission networks as either the same network identifier or different network identifier. Using different network identifiers provides a relay to extend the communications range and allow both radios to transmit and receive. Three options exist for retransmission using SINCGARS: 

- Single-channel to single-channel. 

- Frequency hopping to single-channel (mixed mode). 

- Frequency hopping to frequency hopping. 

2-33. Single-channel to single-channel retransmission operation requires at least a 10 MHz frequency separation (phase line separation) to avoid interference. This method requires net control station monitoring for continual signal dispersion. Frequency hopping to single-channel retransmission is not limited by a phase line separation. A **_net control station_ is a communications station designated to control traffic and enforce circuit discipline within a given net.** 

**20** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Tactical Radio Employment by Echelon** 

2-34. There are two modes for frequency hopping retransmission: F1:F1, and F1:F2. With F1:F1, the radio systems operate on the same network identifier for retransmission. Newer radios implement a time delay. This upgrade allows one radio to receive signals and the other to transmit. This time delay allows a sufficient time lapse for two retransmission sites to operate using the same network identifier for distant stations. This eliminates the need for a phase line separation and allows users to pass the geographic separation without adjusting their radios. 

2-35. F1:F2 retransmission operates using two radios with different network identifiers. This method of retransmission considers the fact that legacy SINCGARS radios lack some of the advanced tracking capabilities and functionality. Additional means of retransmission employment include— 

- **Single band retransmission (frequency modulation-frequency modulation).** Single band retransmission is a common employment for retransmission. A frequency modulation signal is received and retransmitted as a second frequency modulation signal. The SINCGARS is the most common platform employed for this retransmission choice, but many of the Army’s radios are capable. The AN/PRC-148 Multiband Inter/Intra Team Radio (MBITR) or AN/PRC-152, for example, can retransmit even in the higher VHF and UHF ranges. 

- **Crossband retransmission.** It is also possible to establish retransmission from one band to another and with completely different means of transmission. This is a powerful option as it gives the commander with only a frequency modulation radio, limited by range and terrain, potentially global reach and the ability to communicate easily in exceptionally challenging terrain. 

- **Narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT.** Satellite communications can be global. With the appropriate cabling, a SINCGARS radio can be connected to a AN/PRC-162 manpack with MUOS for retransmission operations. If available, the AN/PRC-117 or AN/PRC-148 MBITR system can be used with the SINCGARS radio for range extension. A pair of AN/PRC-117s or AN/PRC-148 MBITRs can also perform this function with the proper mounts and cables. 

**_Note._** The Army plans to divest its inventory of the AN/PSC family of radios (replacing them with the AN/PRC-158 or AN/PRC-162) and the AN/PRC-117F/G radios by fiscal year 28 to support cryptographic modernization. 

- **Aerial retransmission.** One form of aerial retransmission is called battlefield airborne communications node. Battlefield airborne communications node mounts a retransmission radio capability on an unmanned aircraft. Manned aerial platforms can also provide retransmission capability. The challenge in using aerial retransmission is the continual movement of an aircraft means that radio coverage also moves and may make knowing where to change from one net to another to maintain communication quite challenging. As a result, aerial retransmission employment is normally used for data networks rather than voice. 

- **Manpack retransmission.** Foregoing the use of a vehicle and a whole team to perform a retransmission mission may be desirable in some cases. If reduced range is not a concern, connecting two dismounted radios with appropriate cabling and an enhanced antenna for maximum range can still achieve the required range extension. 

2-36. Throughout the mission, one team member continuously monitors radio traffic and serves as the shift relief for the retransmission operation and site defense. Depending on the duration of the mission, personnel should consider constructing a remote position where they can observe the vehicle and that offers protection from indirect fire. 

2-37. The retransmission team specified tasks should include criteria for ending or changing the mission in the event of loss of contact with higher authority. Criteria should cover triggers for displacing a retransmission team to an alternate location. 

2-38. Reporting supply issues prevents circumstances that would jeopardize efforts of the retransmission team. Unit SOPs dictate how often to send status reports, what items to track, and the means used to send the reports. Reports on basic supplies, such as food, water, fuel, and ammunition allow close monitoring of the operational environment and increase survivability. Other reports, including a commander’s critical information requirement report, may document enemy size, activity, location, unit, time, equipment, and observed activity. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**21** 

**Chapter 2** 

2-39. Retransmission must be practiced in unit training in preparation for large-scale combat operations. Training events provide opportunities for teams to assess speed and proficiency in executing their missionessential tasks. Unit leaders should factor in time for battle drills, such as gas attack, indirect fire, or emergency destruction drills to develop the team’s ability to operate independently, demonstrate their ability to conduct movement to an alternate site, re-establish tactical radio connectivity, and set up camouflage quickly in preparation for deployment. See appendix B for detailed information on retransmission team training and certification. 

##### **POST MISSION BRIEF—ON SITE** 

2-40. At the conclusion of a mission, team chiefs conduct a post-mission briefing and inspection before leaving the retransmission site. If the platoon leader or platoon sergeant is not available to conduct the onsite briefing, the team chief performs post-mission checks, including— 

- Ammunition. 

- Individual equipment. 

   - Weapons. 

   - Sensitive items. 

   - Eye protection. 

   - Water. 

   - Hand protection. 

   - Identification tags and identification cards. 

   - Body armor. 

- Chock blocks and drip pans. 

- Trailer is connected, brakes are functional, and parking brake is off. 

- Vehicular antennas are tied down. 

- Convoy frequency plan loaded. 

   - Sheriff net (quick-reaction force). 

   - Medical evacuation. 

- Communications checks with supporting units and security elements. 

- Convoy briefing. 

##### **POST MISSION BRIEF—AFTER RETURN** 

2-41. Upon end of mission and return to the staging area or command post, the retransmission team should prepare for immediate redeployment if it becomes necessary. The post-mission briefing and equipment checks after return help the unit collect lessons learned and recover equipment so it is ready for immediate use. The post-mission briefing and after action review include— 

- Weapons, sensitive items, and ammunition checked immediately upon return. 

- After operation preventive maintenance checks and services performed on vehicles and generators. 

   - Petroleum, oils, and lubricants resupplied. 

   - Trucks, generators, and fuel cans refilled. 

   - Trucks and trailers cleaned as needed. 

- Communications equipment. 

   - Zeroized as necessary. 

   - After operation preventive maintenance checks and services performed on all communications equipment. 

   - Inventory-missing or broken equipment identified and replaced. 

- All classes of supply (including food and water) replenished and load plan updated as necessary. 

- Missing personal equipment identified and replaced. 

- Internal after action review conducted. 

   - Input from team. 

   - Consolidation of notes. 

   - After action review comments submitted to higher headquarters for lessons learned and SOP updates. 

- Debriefing to higher headquarters conducted.

##### **Chapter 3** 

### **Planning** 

This chapter describes tactical radio network planning and the roles of signal staff elements in coordinating requirements for effective signal support. It also provides planning considerations and essential retransmission planning requirements so planners can effectively assess resource capabilities and limitations. 

##### **SECTION I – Tactical Radio Network Planning** 

3-1. Information from the higher headquarters operation order establishes the basis for tactical radio network planning. Communications planners must understand the commander’s intent and concept of operations defined in the base plan or order. The type, size, and complexity of the radio system establishes ideal scenarios of employment and the method of control. 

#### **PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS** 

3-2. Signal planners are active in the operations process. They define support requirements based on the mission variables of METT-TC (I). Planners must understand their organization’s authorized and available communications capabilities and limitations, including personnel and logistical requirements needed to employ and sustain the capabilities. Commanders and leaders develop this understanding during unit training by employing organic equipment, correcting deficiencies, and implementing maintenance and supply programs. 

##### **RUNNING ESTIMATE** 

3-3. Comprehensive running estimates provide valuable information on communications capabilities and are essential to formulating the scheme of signal support. A running estimate continuously assesses operations and is used to determine if they are proceeding according to the commander’s intent and are supportable and feasible. Commanders use recommendations from running estimates to enhance further decision making. Signal staffs use running estimates to advise the commander and make recommendations. Planners consider several additional factors when planning tactical radio networks including— 

- Communications requirements for each warfighting function. 

- Capabilities and limitations of available communications systems. 

- Requirements for mission partner interoperability. 

- Available frequencies. 

- Waveform applications required. 

- Detailed line of sight analysis. 

- HF analysis. 

- Redundancy in means to communicate. 

- Integration of all available signal assets. 

- Method of deployment—sequence assets to coincide with the arrival of forces. 

- Command post locations. 

- The use of retransmission, digital network links, and node placement. 

- Priorities of support to manage available communications bandwidth. 

- Spectrum requirements for tactical radios. 

- COMSEC distribution plans. 

- Signal operating instructions. 

- Conduct of communications rehearsals. 

##### **SITE CONSIDERATIONS** 

3-4. The reliability of radio communications depends largely on the selection of a good radio site. A unit must select an acceptable location that enables them to accomplish their assigned mission. Since it is difficult 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**23** 

**Chapter 3** 

to select a radio site that satisfies all technical, tactical, and security requirements, signal planners should select the most suitable site available. Site selection considers defensibility and logistical supportability as well as communications suitability. 

3-5. Perfect cover and concealment may impair communications. The location selected should provide the best cover and concealment possible, consistent with good transmission and reception. The permissible amount of impairment depends upon the range required, the power of the transmitter, the sensitivity of the receiver, the efficiency of the antenna system, and the nature of the terrain. 

3-6. Leaders and planners also consider that communications systems with specific operating characteristics, capabilities, and limitations create unique circumstances that directly impact site selection. To obtain efficiency of transmission and reception, the following factors should also be considered: 

- For operation at frequencies above 30 MHz, planners should select a location that allows line of sight communications. 

- Dry ground has high resistance and limits radio range. If possible, planners should locate the station near moist ground, which has much less resistance. Water, especially fresh water, greatly increases the distance covered. 

- Operators should install antennas away from foliage and dense brush. 

- When located near man-made obstructions— 

   - Site selection should avoid tunnels, underpasses, and steel bridges due to the high absorption of RF energy. 

   - Radio sites should avoid buildings between radio stations, particularly steel and reinforced concrete structures since they interfere with transmission and reception. 

   - Radio sites should avoid suspended telephone, telegraph, and high-tension power lines. Wire lines absorb RF energy from radiating antennas in their vicinity. They also introduce electrical interference to receiving antennas. 

   - Radio sites should avoid heavily traveled roads and highways. In addition to the noise and confusion caused by tanks and trucks, vehicle ignition systems may cause electrical interference. 

   - Operators should locate battery charging units and generators away from radio sets. 

   - Operators should not locate radio stations close to one other. 

   - Planners should select radio sites in relatively quiet areas to avoid distraction by outside noises. The copying of low-level received messages requires great concentration by the radio operator. 

**_Note._** See appendix H for information related to antenna selection and techniques and appendix I for detailed information about specific tactical radio systems and platforms. 

##### **STAFF COORDINATION** 

3-7. Effective command and control requires continuous close coordination, synchronization, and information sharing across staff sections. As a coordinating staff officer, the G-6 or S-6 provides signal subject matter expertise for the commander and other staff elements through the planning process. G-6 or S-6 planners should work closely with— 

- The G-3 or S-3 to understand the commander’s intent and concept of operations and ensure signal plans support the proposed course of action. 

- The G-2 or S-2 to understand the threat and appropriate countermeasures. 

- The cyberspace electromagnetic warfare officer (CEWO) to minimize the electromagnetic signatures of signal sites. 

- The G-4 or S-4 to support sustainment, maintenance, and repair of signal systems. 

#### **RADIO NETWORK PLANNING TOOLS** 

3-8. Tactical network planning tools enable Soldiers to operate without overburdening the network planners and managers with added complexities. These tools help planners develop, manage, and monitor tactical radio networks; share network plans; view network performance metrics; and provide situational awareness data across the assigned area. Planning tools used for tactical radio systems include— 

- Joint Automated Communications-Electronics Operating Instructions System.
