**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Planning** 

- Ruggedized Applications Platform-Tactical Radios. 

- Automated Communications Engineering Software. 

- Electromagnetic Warfare Planning and Management Tool. 

- Systems Planning, Engineering, and Evaluation Device. 

- Voice of America Coverage Analysis Program. 

- Naval Research Laboratory Builder. 

##### **JOINT AUTOMATED COMMUNICATIONS-ELECTRONICS OPERATING INSTRUCTION SYSTEM** 

3-9. The G-6 or S-6 staff’s radio network planning ensures interoperability of RF networks, cryptographic key tag generation, transmission security key generation, signal operating instructions production, and radio network planning. Joint Automated Communications-Electronics Operating Instruction System and Automated Communications Engineering Software allow the G-6 or S-6 staff to perform pre-deployment and post-mission planning radio network planning. 

##### **RUGGEDIZED APPLICATIONS PLATFORM-TACTICAL RADIOS** 

3-10. The Ruggedized Applications Platform-Tactical Radio is a host platform that combines multiple software applications into a single tactical network management tool that plans configures, loads, and monitors legacy waveforms and advanced networking waveforms. It supports secure but unclassified and classified radio networks. Atom, the primary Ruggedized Applications Platform-Tactical Radio software, is a radio planning and loading tool that provides simultaneous support for tactical radios and a collection of legacy and advanced waveforms. 

##### **AUTOMATED COMMUNICATIONS ENGINEERING SOFTWARE** 

3-11. Automated Communications Engineering Software is a network planning software program that plans, creates, distributes, manages, and verifies cryptonets and key related information. Automated Communications Engineering Software allows users to perform fully automated cryptographic network planning; signal operating instructions; and electromagnetic protection planning, management, validation, generation, and distribution at the time and location needed. 

3-12. The Automated Communications Engineering Software provides the capability to support black key packaging and distribution and provides fills for the electronic fill device, AN/PYQ-10, with secure network information through a direct cable connection or over-the-air rekey. Pre- and post-mission planning also consist of electromagnetic protection data and radio network engineering for secure communication. 

3-13. The network planning functionality of Automated Communications Engineering Software incorporates cryptonet planning, key management, and key tag generation. The data for a given plan includes individual networks, which are assigned individual network members. Designated network members define the platforms, specific equipment fill locations and associated key tags, and keys with the equipment locations. 

3-14. Network members download the equipment records, which include platform data, network data, and key tags associating the data with the required key. Similarly, the electromagnetic protection data and signal operating instructions generated by the Automated Communications Engineering Software workstation operator enable the data download to the electronic fill device. Mission requirements dictate whether the COMSEC account is resourced with an Automated Communications Engineering Software workstation. 

##### **ELECTROMAGNETIC WARFARE PLANNING AND MANAGEMENT TOOL** 

3-15. The Electromagnetic Warfare Planning and Management Tool is a software application that uses computerized modeling and planning capabilities to assess and manage electromagnetic warfare asset in realtime. The Electromagnetic Warfare Planning and Management Tool supports spectrum planning by allowing Soldiers to sense emitter activity and sensor data within their electromagnetic operating environment. This tool provides commanders essential information and intelligence for analysis to develop a common operational picture while operating remotely. The Electromagnetic Warfare Planning and Management Tool integrates electromagnetic order of battle data into current and future simulations and command and control systems. This allows commanders to identify and visualize both threat and friendly force digital footprints and execute synchronized electromagnetic warfare, spectrum management, intelligence, and signal operations for successful offensive and defensive operations and planning. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**25** 

**Chapter 3** 

##### **SYSTEMS PLANNING, ENGINEERING, AND EVALUATION DEVICE** 

3-16. The Systems Planning, Engineering, and Evaluation Device is a complete stand-alone, self-installing software package that provides the tools necessary to plan and analyze communications equipment. The software contains HF analysis, radio coverage analysis, point-to-point, and satellite planning tools, which allows planning in response to rapidly changing communications architectures. 

3-17. The Systems Planning, Engineering, and Evaluation Device communications planner has threedimensional mapping and mission planning capabilities, including frequency-dependent rejection interference analysis, enhanced jammer modeling, and effectiveness prediction. The software contains HF analysis, radio coverage analysis, point-to-point, and satellite planning tools, which allows planning in response to rapidly changing communications architectures. Communications planners load topographical information before each operation to generate reports, maps, and matrixes. 

##### **VOICE OF AMERICA COVERAGE ANALYSIS PROGRAM** 

3-18. Voice of America Coverage Analysis Program software released to the public is downloadable from the Department of Commerce National Telecommunications Information Administration, Institute for Telecommunications Sciences to use as a HF prediction and analysis tool. Planners must input several parameters before the Voice of America Coverage Analysis Program can provide a propagation prediction determined by the method and the antennas used. Voice of America Coverage Analysis Program offers— 

- Graphical user interface. 

- Detailed point-to-point graphs. 

- Signal-to-noise radio. 

- Required power gain. 

- Signal power. 

- Maximum usable frequency. 

- Take-off and arrival angles. 

- Point-to-point performance versus distance for any given parameters at one or all user assigned frequencies. 

- Methods for antenna patterns. 

##### **INTERACTIVE SCENARIO BUILDER (BUILDER)** 

3-19. The Interactive Scenario Builder (Builder) is a mission planning tool that provides a three-dimensional interactive representation, including RF propagation and geospatial and temporal situational awareness. The Interactive Scenario Builder (Builder) incorporates antenna and radar cross-section pattern data and meteorological, terrain, and countermeasure effects to model communications systems. This modeling helps calculate RF propagation and enhances the geospatial situational awareness in the commander’s assigned area by visualizing National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency map products. The National GeospatialIntelligence Agency map products include visual imagery and digital terrain elevation data. The National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency Builder can be used for pre-mission planning, near real-time situational awareness, and after-action reviews. The Interactive Scenario Builder’s (Builder) interactive software is authorized to operate on Nonclassified Internet Protocol Router Network and SECRET Internet Protocol Router Network. 

#### **COMMUNICATIONS SECURITY PLANNING** 

3-20. _Communications security_ is protective measures to deny unauthorized access to information of value derived from use of communications systems (JP 6-0). Cryptographic systems and devices protect networks and the information being transmitted and give the command confidence in the security of transmissions. Leaders, network planners, and end users must guard against threat attempts to intercept and exploit U.S. operation plans through its networks and information services. 

3-21. Automated Communications Engineering Software operators develop the COMSEC network overlay based on mission requirements or PACE plan. The network planner tailors Automated Communications Engineering Software and workstations as needed to correspond to planning requirements using the generalpurpose planning module software application. Once tailored and complete, COMSEC account managers that maintain centralized accountability of cryptographic material and equipment, process the cryptonet plan 

**26** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Planning** 

for delivery to the network planner and Automated Communications Engineering Software operators and subsequent distribution to the end user. 

3-22. The Automated Communications Engineering Software includes a planning module for each network category. The categories of supported network plans include— 

- Combat net radios. 

- General-purpose networks. 

- Adaptive networking wideband waveform. 

3-23. The network planner finishes processing the cryptonet and provides the cryptonet plan, signal operating instructions, and loadset data to local end users. The COMSEC account manager generates or provisions and distributes the key to end users by loading keys into the users’ fill devices. Users load the end cryptographic units using a fill device. Refer to ATP 6-02.75 for more information about COMSEC planning. 

##### **KEYING MATERIAL** 

3-24. The COMSEC account manager is responsible for the receipt, custody, security, accountability, safeguarding, inventory, transfer, and destruction of COMSEC material. For a cryptonet to communicate securely, all members of the net must use compatible equipment and keying material. COMSEC account managers distribute and control keying material and manage routine updates so all authorized users—and only authorized users—have the correct key to communicate. 

3-25. Commanders carry the responsibility of safeguarding and controlling COMSEC material in their units to ensure its continuous integrity and prevent access by unauthorized persons. The unit SOP includes risk assessments to establish and document security measures for COMSEC material and controlled cryptographic items. The commander appoints an account manager for the COMSEC account. 

3-26. Safeguarding and controlling COMSEC material is also the responsibility of individuals in possession of the material. While COMSEC accounting is centralized, individual users hold keying material and devices at the lowest echelon that can maintain adequate physical security. End users protect and account for COMSEC material in their possession and report anything that could jeopardize its security or integrity. The unit SOP provides local procedures for safeguarding, controlling, and emergency destruction of COMSEC material. Refer to ATP 6-02.75 for additional information on techniques for COMSEC. 

##### **KEY DISTRIBUTION** 

3-27. The Army uses electronic key generation and distribution as its primary means of transferring keying material from the COMSEC account in electronic form using a fill device. Encrypted key distribution allows secure delivery of a key to an end cryptographic unit and reduces the risk of compromise during transmission. Encrypted key distribution starts with the COMSEC account manager using the planning workstation to generate an encrypted key. COMSEC distribution takes place through— 

- **Transfer.** The distribution of COMSEC material from one account to another. Transfers move accountability and responsibility for the transferred COMSEC material from the sending account to the receiving account. 

- **Issue.** The distribution of one or more accountable COMSEC items from a COMSEC account to a local element. The issuing element retains accountability for the material. Issuing and filling keys are the two functional areas of key distribution between a COMSEC operating account and a local element. 

###### **Initial Key Load** 

3-28. The  stores, sends and receives mission data (hopsets, loadsets and signal operating instructions information). COMSEC account managers ensure loading and key distribution for various systems by first inputting the key into the electronic fill device. For further information on loading instructions for specific end cryptographic units, refer to the equipment technical manual and the radio and system operators handbook. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**27** 

**Chapter 3** 

###### **Over-the-Air Rekey** 

3-29. The net control station may initiate over-the-air key distribution any time during the effective cryptographic period, or immediately before the end of the cryptographic period. Before transmitting a key, the net control station notifies all recipients over a secure communications link and outlines the details of key transmission. 

##### **EMERGENCY DESTRUCTION** 

3-30. Allowing COMSEC materials to fall into enemy hands compromises secure communications. COMSEC compromise disrupts radio and data networks until operators and net control stations can conduct emergency cryptographic key supersession. If a keyed radio or a loaded electronic fill device falls into an enemy’s hands, the unit SOP should assume the enemy has English-speaking Soldiers who can operate the radio and key loading device. SOPs should also assume the enemy is able to listen to U.S. secure frequency hopping network communications and can transmit on that same U.S. network if desired. 

3-31. Unit SOPs must include procedures to evacuate COMSEC material and encryption devices or destroy them in in place. By including emergency destruction battle drills in mission rehearsals, the risk of system compromise is mitigated, and forces are prepared to respond and defend during real-world operations. Refer to ATP 6-02.75 for more information about COMSEC operations. 

#### **EMISSION CONTROL PLANNING** 

3-32. Commanders consult with the G-2 or S-2 and electromagnetic warfare personnel to identify threat, electromagnetic warfare, and signals intelligence capabilities. Additionally, electromagnetic warfare professionals and spectrum managers must evaluate the effectiveness of friendly force EMCON measures and recommend modifications or improvements. Units should implement and exercise EMCON plans before execution and conduct vulnerability analysis and assessments of friendly communications assets to understand the electromagnetic environment. 

3-33. G-6 or S-6 planners collaborate with the CEWO during signal site planning and consider using electromagnetic masking and electromagnetic decoys to protect the locations of critical nodes. EMCON helps prevent threat electromagnetic warfare assets from locating and attacking friendly forces. Everyone in the organization must understand the importance of EMCON for it to be effective. See appendix A for additional techniques for EMCON. 

3-34. Radio discipline is a basic and effective EMCON technique to protect against threat detection. Radio discipline can take the form of limiting transmissions, antenna masking, and the use of minimum required power. Additional techniques for EMCON include— 

- Preplanning messages before transmitting. This minimizes the number of pauses in the transmission and decreases transmission time. It also ensures the conciseness of the message. 

- Transmitting quickly and precisely. This reduces the need to repeat a radio transmission. Unnecessary repetition increases transmission time and the threat’s opportunity to intercept U.S. transmissions and gain valuable information. 

- Using equipment capable of data burst transmission. This is a significant advantage of TACSAT systems. 

- Using an alternate means of communication. Soldiers use alternate means of communications, such as cable, wire, or messages to convey necessary directives and information. 

- Using brevity codes and procedure words. This provides no security; however, its purpose is to shorten messages rather than conceal their content. 

#### **SPECTRUM PLANNING** 

3-35. Spectrum planning coordinates frequency use before activation of emitters to mitigate or eliminate electromagnetic interference or other adverse effects by implementing friendly force recognition, antifratricide, and electromagnetic protection measures. Deconfliction ensures spectrum-dependent devices do not create unintended electromagnetic interference (frequency fratricide) with friendly communications, unmanned aircraft systems, weapon systems, or space-based GPS-enabled equipment providing precise PNT. 

3-36. Spectrum planning considerations include— 

**28** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Planning** 

- Transmitter and receiver locations. 

- Antenna technical parameters and characteristics. 

- Number of frequencies desired and frequency separation requirements. 

- Nature of the operation (fixed, mobile land, mobile airborne, or maritime). 

- Physical effects of the operational environment (ground, soil, humidity, and topology). 

- Start and end dates for use. 

- Joint restricted frequency list. 

3-37. Spectrum managers use various tools to manage and plan the unit’s spectrum needs and objectives. Examples include— 

- Automated Communications Engineering Software. 

- Electromagnetic Warfare Planning and Management Tool. 

- Host-Nation Spectrum Worldwide Database Online. 

- Joint Automated Communications-Electronics Operating Instructions System. 

- Spectrum Situational Awareness System. 

- Systems Planning, Engineering, and Evaluation Device. 

- Global Electromagnetic Spectrum Information System. 

- Spectrum XXI. 

#### **PRIMARY, ALTERNATE, CONTINGENCY, AND EMERGENCY COMMUNICATION PLANS** 

3-38. Commanders depend on communications systems to exercise command and control of subordinate formations and coordinate mission plans with higher and adjacent units as well as supporting or supported joint forces, host-nations, and multinational forces. The PACE communication plan provides redundant communications if the primary means of communication fails and designates the order in which an element will move through redundant means of communication until contact is established with the desired distance elements. 

3-39. The PACE communication plan applies to a specific mission or task, not a unit. It enables operational continuity during all operational phases ensuring maneuver elements can continue to communicate to perform mission objectives. Higher headquarters evaluates communication requirements with subordinate echelons and works with the G-6 or S-6 to develop an effective plan to mitigate the impact of degraded networks. By implementing a PACE communication plan, signal planners reduce communications disruption in congested or contested environments. 

3-40. Planners identify appropriate PACE means of communication for each phase of the operation and publish them in Annex H (Signal) of the operation order. The PACE plan is implemented in accordance with command post battle drills. 

3-41. If the commander determines that the primary means of communication is impacted, they take immediate action to mitigate the effects or initiate the PACE plan. Quickly changing to an alternate or contingency means of communications reduces disruption of command and control. 

3-42. The unit must be capable of operating each means of communication in the PACE plan. If the designated equipment is non-mission capable, that capability should not be included in the PACE plan. Planners should ensure unit personnel are adequately trained on systems designated as alternate, contingency, or emergency means of communication. Planners should also consider the totality of information on the primary form of communication and assess whether that level of bandwidth can fulfill alternate, contingency, or emergency elements of the PACE and plan appropriately. When developing PACE plans, planners should ensure all affected elements have the equipment to execute the plan. Upon receipt of an order from a higher headquarters, the receiving unit evaluates the PACE plan for two essential elements: 

- Availability of assets to execute the plan to higher. 

- Ability to nest with the higher command’s PACE plan. 

3-43. If a unit cannot execute the full PACE plan to its higher command, it must inform the issuing headquarters with an assessment of shortfalls, gaps, and possible mitigation measures as part of mission analysis during the military decision-making process. Subordinate elements must be capable of providing 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**29** 

**Chapter 3** 

higher echelons with redundant means of communications support. Identified PACE capabilities must be diverse to ensure continued communication should any portion of the communication path or capability become non-mission capable or compromised. This allows forces to carry out mission objectives without impacting the other PACE capabilities. 

3-44. Units should rehearse and validate the PACE plan during mission and communications rehearsals to ensure the alternate, contingency, and emergency means of communication are viable and that all personnel understand the triggers and can execute the plan as necessary. Table 3-1 shows an example PACE plan aligned with warfighting functions.

#### **LINK GEOMETRY** 

3-45. G-6 or S-6 planners conduct terrain modeling and analysis for airborne, ground-based, and surface and subsurface spectrum-dependent devices to support tactical mission and operational planning. To increase team member survivability, spectrum managers ensure equipment is properly emplaced during planning. This maximizes linkage between communications and decrease susceptibility of detection from threat directional finding. Proper link geometry masks signals from threat direction-finding and jamming capabilities. 

#### **SITE SIGNATURE CONTROL** 

3-46. Tactical radios and retransmission sites can be easily detected by threats from the air or through the EMS. To prevent this, soldiers use protective measures to conceal their activities and minimize the risk of being detected. This includes using camouflage and concealment, as well as adhering to noise and light discipline. These measures help to keep personnel safe and maintain their ability to act freely on the battlefield. Leaders combine multiple measures to mask signatures. These measures include— 

- Camouflage and concealment. 

- Noise and light discipline. 

- Electromagnetic signature reduction. 

##### **CONCEALMENT AND CAMOUFLAGE** 

3-47. Concealment offers protection from observation or surveillance and helps prevent threat detection. SOPs should include provisions to conceal personnel and equipment. Installing antennas as low as possible 

**30** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Planning** 

on the backside of terrain features, and behind man-made obstacles helps conceal communications equipment while still permitting communication. 

3-48. It is difficult to conceal large communication systems, but camouflage can make these systems harder to identify. Camouflage offers concealment and helps minimize the visual signature. Radar reflective camouflage netting partially mitigates the command post’s electromagnetic signature. Using antenna multiplexers further reduces electromagnetic footprint by reducing the number of antennas needed. 

##### **NOISE AND LIGHT DISCIPLINE** 

3-49. Generators, vehicles, and communications assemblages can produce significant noise revealing the location of command post and unit positions thereby increasing vulnerability. Operators reduce detection by minimizing and masking the noise from signal systems and disabling or turning equipment off when not in use. Units can also use available natural cover or place equipment against a wall or physical structure to minimize noise and avoid detection. Additional practices include limiting radio use and restricting unnecessary vehicle and foot movement and securing loose metal parts to prevent them from making noise in windy weather conditions. Operators can turn the speaker volume to the lowest setting to avoid location detection. 

3-50. Excessive light makes tactical sites easy to locate at night. Units practice light discipline to minimize the probability of detection. Minimal use of flashlights or other electrical lights helps prevent visual detection. During site setup, Soldiers should cover or blacken reflective surfaces, such as bare metal surfaces or vehicle windshields when not in use. When using infrared light, leaders must consider the threat’s night vision and infrared capabilities. Leaders should restrict unnecessary vehicle and foot movement once the signal site is set up. 

##### **ELECTROMAGNETIC SIGNATURE REDUCTION** 

3-51. Soldiers use various spectrum-dependent devices that a peer threat can detect and target. These threats have advanced capabilities to locate the electromagnetic signatures of communications systems using ground-based or unmanned aerial electromagnetic warfare systems. To safeguard against these risks, leaders must minimize the unit’s electromagnetic signature. This includes assessing and employing digital rather than analog command and control, implementing electromagnetic protection techniques, and establishing EMCON procedures. 

#### **SIGNAL OPERATING INSTRUCTIONS** 

3-52. Signal operating instructions contain relevant instructions for radio communications. The signal operating instruction is a governing document for routine signal operations of a unit. It includes information on communications system priorities and general guidance for communications protocol. The signal operating instructions contain details on the unit’s radio nets; assigning net control stations; and primary and alternate operating frequencies for each net. The signal operating instructions also contain guidance on authentication procedures and COMSEC. 

#### **HIGH FREQUENCY RADIO PLANNING** 

3-53. HF radios provide beyond line of sight secure voice and data communications on two modes of HF propagation—ground wave and skywave. Planners consider that system adjustments are required to compensate for ionosphere and an ever-changing terrestrial environment, electromagnetic interference from other stations, atmospheric interference, and man-made noise. Determining the most favorable conditions is crucial to HF radio planning. Planners determine the optimal frequency, time of day, power, and antenna configuration to ensure the criteria align with the capabilities and assets of the unit. Listed at paragraph 3- 16–3-18 are detailed descriptions of HF planning tools— 

- Systems Planning, Engineering, and Evaluation Device. 

- Voice of America Coverage Analysis Program. 

3-54. HF radio data capabilities are limited by bandwidth, spectrum physics, and space and terrestrial weather. Planner awareness of the capabilities and limitations of HF radios will maximize availability. HF radio assets are most useful for long-range communications. Planners must understand the concepts of propagation, path loss, and digital signal processing. Antenna selection is also critical in HF radio planning. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31** 

**Chapter 3** 

To select the best antenna for HF radios, planners need to understand wavelength, frequency, resonance, and polarization. See appendix E for information on HF operations and appendix H for antenna selection techniques. 

#### **NARROWBAND (SINGLE-CHANNEL) TACTICAL SATELLITE PLANNING** 

3-55. Narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT supports tactical communications worldwide by providing interoperability between legacy TACSAT radios, software-defined radios, and hardware-defined radio waveforms. The interoperability enables voice and limited data exchange for beyond line of sight tactical radio users. Planners use the Joint Integrated Satellite Communications Tool on SECRET Internet Protocol Router Network to prepare and submit satellite access requests for narrowband satellite communications service. 

3-56. Planning considerations for narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT include establishing redundant and auxiliary communications and mitigation techniques to minimize interference and threat attacks on critical satellite resources. Additionally, planners should— 

- Formulate communications plans that minimize RF footprint. 

- Use modulation techniques that provide a low probability of signal detection or reception. 

- Identify bandwidth requirements (lower data rates require less RF power). 

- Practice EMCON (limit radio transmissions). 

- Establish PACE plans to mitigate effects of a congested or contested environment. 

- Locate antennas away from command posts to mitigate collateral damage if communications systems are destroyed. 

3-57. Units establish and document valid service requirements in the Satellite Communications Database Management Tool prior to requesting satellite access in the Joint Integrated Satellite Communications Tool. Users submit connectivity requirements using the Joint Satellite Communications Database Management Tool. The combatant command forwards satellite requirements to the joint satellite communications panel administrator using the Joint Satellite Communications Database Management Tool. Following receipt and assessment by the Space Force, the request findings are returned to the joint satellite communications panel administrator. The communications planner at the division G-6 or other operations cell submits satellite access requests through corps headquarters to the theater army for verification once a unit receives a validated requirement for satellite access. The approved satellite access authorization permits operational access. 

3-58. Planners should submit satellite access requests with sufficient time before the mission date to ensure the request can be validated and submitted to the supporting regional satellite communications support center 30 days before the requested access date. This timeline may be shortened if the validation authority identifies a satellite access request as an exception to policy, crisis, or contingency satellite access request. Planners use the Joint Integrated Satellite Communications Tool application on SECRET Internet Protocol Router Network to prepare and submit narrowband satellite access requests. When submitting a satellite access request, the requesting unit should ensure— 

- Signal planners prepare a satellite access request. 

- Unit forwards the satellite access request to the theater army satellite communications manager for validation; units in the continental United States submit requests to FORSCOM. 

- Theater army or FORSCOM submits request to the combatant command for mission approval. 

- The combatant command forwards validated satellite access requests to the supporting regional satellite communications support center for resourcing. 

##### **DEMAND-ASSIGNED MULTIPLE ACCESS** 

3-59. Demand-assigned multiple access (DAMA) is a technique that matches user demands to available satellite bandwidth. Satellite channels are grouped as a bulk asset. DAMA assigns users variable time slots that match the radio operator’s requirements. Since most users do not require constant bandwidth, this concept allows bandwidth sharing and results in greater flexibility in managing available satellite resources. The increase in networks or radio users supported using DAMA depends on the type of users. Regardless of format, DAMA generally increases satellite capability by a factor of 5–14 times over dedicated channel operation. 

**32** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Planning** 

3-60. Extensive planning and coordination are required for establishing SATCOM. Planners should become familiar with— 

- The SATCOM database. 

- Requirements submission. 

- Operating in degraded and denied environments. 

- Satellite access requests. 

- Access priorities. 

- Apportionment. 

- Access planning. 

- Redundant communications procedures. 

- Allocation process. 

- After action reporting. 

- Planning checklist. 

##### **INTEGRATED WAVEFORM** 

3-61. Integrated waveform is an enhanced method of multiplexing radio networks on the same channel. This technique uses carrier phase modulation to allow more access using the same channel. Carrier phase modulation provides higher data throughput on UHF dedicated satellite channels in line of sight mode. The integrated wave form offers advantages over DAMA. The integrated waveform replaces DAMA to provide multiple users access to the SATCOM resources. Planning considerations for integrated waveform include— 

- Communication classification level. 

- Waveform flexibility. 

- Mission partner collaboration. 

- Common operational picture accounting. 

- Range extension and scalability. 

- Configuration interoperability. 

##### **MOBILE USER OBJECTIVE SYSTEM** 

3-62. MUOS terminals are a combination radio and modem configuration. Using appropriate provisioning applications, unit planners ensure the radio and modem configurations are provisioned, programmed, and loaded to support operations upon deployment. Provisioning is the process whereby a terminal is registered with the MUOS network management system and given access to operate on the MUOS network in an approved configuration. Planners initiate important steps to register and obtain approval to operate on the MUOS network in an approved configuration. To provide on-the-move communications, planners initiate the pre-operations process which includes— 

- Terminal capabilities planning. 

   - Coordinate terminal requirements. 

   - Coordinate network requirements. 

- Access authorization process. 

   - Coordinate terminal profiles with operational provisioning authority using the Joint Satellite Communications Management and Planning System. 

   - Coordinate group service authorizations with the regional satellite communications support center using the Joint Integrated Satellite Communications Tool. 

- COMSEC ordering process. 

   - Order keying material. 

   - Coordinate group service keying material. 

   - MUOS keying material. 

- COMSEC receipt process. 

   - Coordinate terminal registration. 

   - Coordinate keying material transfers. 

   - Ensure the terminal’s radio configuration files are built within the provisioning application and complete for transfer. 

- Local provisioning process. 

   - Ensure terminal accesses system using over-the-air file transfer. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**33** 

**Chapter 3** 

 Ensure communications and network parameters are met and capabilities are fully functional. 

3-63. MUOS group network planning requires the planner to coordinate terminal requirements with the communications element or spectrum manager and the narrowband communications and Internet Protocol structure with the unit’s DODIN operations personnel. MUOS services are managed and processed by the regional satellite communications support centers and require completion of a satellite access request. Requests must be submitted according to access authorization policies and applicable enterprise tools and applications. Terminal provisioning requests and satellite access requests should be submitted no later than 90 days prior to mission start date. Unit SOPs should provide detailed descriptions for terminal provisioning request policies, access authorization, COMSEC keying material ordering policies, and satellite access request policies. Any compromise or loss of a terminal profile must be reported to the Global Narrowband Watch Office or a regional satellite communications support center. 

3-64. TACSAT communications is a key component to many ground and aviation forces’ communications plans. Maintaining capabilities that allow units to communicate with allied and joint forces is essential during transitioning from legacy TACSAT-based networks to MUOS in order to address capability gaps caused by new equipment fielding during network modernization. A method of bridging legacy satellite network users and MUOS users is done over a MUOS legacy gateway component which requires significant coordination with MUOS network control centers and end users. It is important for planners to ascertain communications requirements during transitional periods so forces operating at a distance can both maintain communication with lines of support and provide vital situational awareness information to commanders. 

#### **DATA NETWORK PLANNING** 

3-65. Deployed units must be able access the needed data at the right time and at the right place. Datacentricity and interoperability, key aspects to network viability, create resiliency, eliminate silos, and enable greater interoperability with mission partners. Sharing common applications, services infrastructure, and transport layers enables data synchronization between echelons and across formations. 

3-66. To plan effective data networks, network planners must understand the current threat situation and the electromagnetic order of battle. An _electromagnetic order of battle_ is a subset of the overall order of battle that consists of the identification, strength, command structure, disposition, and operating parameters of the electromagnetic spectrum-dependent systems (JP 3-85). 

3-67. Signal support plans must also account for threat electromagnetic warfare capabilities that potentially impact operations, disrupt communication, and degrade command and control. Collaboration between G-6 or S-6 planners, the G-2 or S-2 section, space operations officer, and the CEWO is essential to ensure adequate consideration is given to the effects of friendly communications when developing an electromagnetic protection plan. Network planners use automated planning and management tools to facilitate network planning. Additional considerations of network planning include— 

- System and network availability. 

- Frequency allocation. 

- Internet Protocol planning. 

- Mission partner interoperability. 

- Frequency planning. 

##### **INTEGRATED TACTICAL NETWORK PLANNING** 

3-68. Operational flexibility, interoperability, and security are essential components to ITN planning. Through common application standards, services infrastructure, and network transport, the ITN provides a broader range of capabilities to extend the data and voice communication when required to Army, allied forces, and mission partners. Commercial capabilities and cross-domain solutions integrated into the ITN create greater flexibility. The same versatility enables continued communications support and secure interaction between lower and higher echelons including interoperability with mission partners using a shared common operational environment. 

3-69. ITN systems must provide maximum effectiveness while operating in highly dynamic environments, under challenging environmental conditions where peer threats exist. For further information on safeguarding ITN systems and subsystems, refer to MIL-STD-461G. 

**34** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Planning** 

3-70. Planning is essential to maintain signal and data confidentiality, integrity, and availability and deny threat efforts to exploit network vulnerabilities. Key requirements for network modernization and ITN planning include— 

- Equipment capable of supporting multiple mission modes including user interface. 

- Visual tools that are customizable and enhance decision making and action. 

- Equipment readiness and real-time monitoring of system health to enable predictive maintenance and reduce major component failures. 

- Multipath transport systems for voice and data communications. 

- Multi-level security classification across multiple security domains. 

- Information systems that mediate and translate data to enhance situational and understanding. 

- Relevant, multi-source data accessibility. 

- Systems that allow flexibility using a system of systems architecture and standards or interoperability and rapid upgrades. 

- Network transport compatibility and bandwidth aggregation that enable resiliency by leveraging available commercial, military terrestrial, aerial, and celestial capabilities. 

- Remote and on-the-move voice and data communication in mobile, handheld, and mounted configurations. 

- Spectrum supportability risk assessment conducted in accordance with DODI 4650.01 and AR 5-12 and submitted to the Army Spectrum management Office. 

- Automated PACE prioritization and failover path selection to minimize disruption to tactical operations. 

- Tactical radio network extension to augment or replace retransmission team operations. 

3-71. To ensure ITN capabilities and network systems maintain compatibility, planners ensure spectrum supportability risk assessments are performed and appropriately submitted to the Army Spectrum Management Office to receive a spectrum supportability decision. Refer to ATP 6-02.70 for frequency request procedures. 

##### **JOINT AND MULTINATIONAL INTEROPERABILITY NETWORK PLANNING** 

3-72. Building joint communications to support the joint force communications requires knowledge of the joint force organization, the commander’s concept of operations, available communications resources and capabilities, and how they are employed (JP 6-0). Agreed-upon standards for information and data exchange ensure there is cohesive integration of the network architecture whether systems and capabilities are commercial-off-the-shelf or are transport network capabilities or systems belonging to mission partners. 

3-73. The joint and coalition network section of the theater army G-6 plans and coordinates the management of joint functional computer systems—both hardware and software. The section integrates joint information systems with command post and sustainment information systems. The section develops the information management plan and maintains Global Command and Control System (Army and joint) servers to provide the common operational picture for the theater. The section represents the theater army on Army, joint, and multinational information management boards. The section also develops and maintains the concept of operations for multinational network information exchange, develops network architecture and support requirements for joint and multinational exercises, and plans implementation for new technologies. 

##### **SECTION II – RETRANSMISSION PLANNING** 

3-74. Employing a retransmission site requires deliberate mission planning before deploying the team. Retransmission teams ensure required supplies, equipment, and site support are coordinated and available before leaving the staging area, command post, or forward operating base. Planners must coordinate for security and sustainment when deploying signal sites remotely from the supported unit. Team members assist in coordinated defense of retransmission sites but may require augmentation to adequately defend remote sites. Military police can provide additional security or serve as a response force to communication centers or nodes in the support area and along ground lines of communication, to include retransmission sites, when needed. Refer to ATP 3-39.30 for more information on security and mobility support. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**35** 

**Chapter 3** 

#### **OVERVIEW** 

3-75. Essential to retransmission planning and survivability are support tasks performed by non-signal personnel such as quick reaction force, physical security, site defense, and casualty evacuation. Additional factors for retransmission planning include— 

- Units supported. 

- Number of nets or nodes required. 

- Running estimates. 

- Number of signal systems on hand and mission capable. 

   - Capacity to support retransmission across the assigned area. 

   - Radio coverage and capabilities of the radio systems being used. 

- Compromise procedures. 

- Contingency planning. 

   - Planned retransmission locations throughout the mission, including alternate sites, with line of sight analysis. 

   - Triggers for displacement to alternate locations. 

   - Quick reaction force and relocation evacuation criteria. 

   - Primary and alternate frequencies. 

- Frequencies for emergency contact. 

   - Medical evacuation. 

   - Command. 

   - Nearest units. 

- Time required to be on station and retransmitting for supported units. 

- Route and site security requirements. 

- Ingress and egress routes. 

- Whether unmanned aircraft systems or other aerial retransmission capabilities can meet the requirement. 

- Plan to communicate with the parent unit (for example, contact the G-6 or S-6 every two hours with status reports). 

- Resupply plan. 

- Concealment of the site. 

- Plans to mask or reduce the electromagnetic signature of the site. 

- Reseed plan (vehicles, radios, and personnel). 

#### **RETRANSMISSION PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS** 

3-76. Team chiefs, platoon sergeants, and platoon leaders should thoroughly inspect all retransmission systems and personnel before employing a remote retransmission site. Upon return, teams must recover their equipment and prepare for immediate follow-on missions. 

3-77. Employing a retransmission team remotely from established command posts or other units requires additional planning and resources for site defense, logistics, and emergency evacuation. Planning considerations for retransmission include— 

- Site analysis. 

- Site selection. 

- Site reconnaissance. 

- Site security and defense. 

- Sustainment and resupply. 

3-78. Because retransmission teams often operate remotely from other elements. It is important for teams to track maneuver operations and anticipate emerging communications requirements. This awareness helps teams maintain fully operational networks supporting the commander’s mission requirements. 

##### **SITE ANALYSIS** 

3-79. The G-6 or S-6 should conduct a signal site analysis of the assigned area. Signal site analysis and selection is a collaborative effort between the G-6 or S-6 and the G-3 or S-3. A thorough site analysis enables 

**36** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Planning** 

a meaningful course of action comparison during planning and provides flexibility when executing operations. The G-6 or S-6 analysis focuses on the ability of communications systems to provide coverage from the proposed site. The G-3 or S-3 analysis focuses on mobility, survivability, and sustainability. 

3-80. If the mission changes and operations move to another area, planners should already know which areas they can support with effective communications. Signal planners consider the operating characteristics, limitations, and effective planning distance for each available communications asset. As planners define communications requirements, the number of retransmission sites needed to cover the assigned area may help determine supportability of proposed courses of action during the military decision-making process. 

3-81. The G-6 or S-6 coordinates with the G-2 or S-2 for terrain analysis and the current threat estimate. Signal planners evaluate the terrain (including man-made features) at proposed retransmission sites to visualize how they can support the mission. Terrain and structures may make a proposed site unsuitable for retransmission operations. Planners consider the current threat estimate to prevent placing communications sites near known or anticipated enemy positions. 

3-82. The G-6 or S-6 considers these facts and assumptions: 

- Maintenance status of communications equipment and systems. 

- Technical limitations of equipment— 

   - Range. 

   - Weather. 

   - Bandwidth. 

   - Frequencies. 

- Areas of limited communications coverage. 

- Higher headquarters support. 

- Services. 

- Personnel status. 

- Effects of the operational environment on communications. 

- Threat intelligence collection and electromagnetic warfare capabilities. 

- COMSEC changes. 

- Security and defense of remote retransmission sites. 

##### **SITE SELECTION** 

3-83. Selecting a retransmission site location is best accomplished by conducting a thorough analysis of the potential site. If the situation does not allow for a site reconnaissance, leaders can use available planning resources such as line of sight analysis tools and satellite imagery. Leaders consider how well the site is concealed from major roads or other vantage points and how the site’s natural terrain can support the mission. Planners should consider the enemy’s most likely and most dangerous courses of action when evaluating proposed retransmission sites. 

3-84. Based on the signal site analysis, the G-6 or S-6 recommends retransmission sites to support all proposed courses of action during the military decision-making process. The final site selected must be— 

- Able to provide range extension. 

- Accessible. 

- Defensible— 

   - Defensive plan. 

   - Escape routes. 

   - Cover. 

   - Concealment. 

- Logistically supportable. 

###### **Range Extension** 

3-85. Extending the network to tactical units provides commanders increased situational awareness and enables communication with dispersed or deployed units. Signal teams employ tactical radio retransmission to increase the geographic area a commander can effectively control. Ground and aerial retransmission links 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**37** 

**Chapter 3** 

can extend the effective range of tactical radio networks to beyond line of sight distances in environments where satellite access is denied or degraded. 

###### **Accessibility** 

3-86. The proposed site should be accessible with terrain flat enough to accommodate antenna emplacement. Favorable locations include ridgelines and hilltops away from roads, trails, and natural lines of drift that offer multiple avenues of escape. 

###### **Defensibility** 

3-87. Because of their small size and limited defensive capabilities, retransmission teams require assistance from the supported unit to defend against enemy attack. Leaders must ensure the retransmission site is defensible and offers adequate escape routes to prevent overrun by an enemy force. 

3-88. Retransmission sites supporting mobile defense should co-locate with other Army elements whenever possible. If the retransmission site operates remotely, the site requires security augmentation to survive a ground attack or level II and III threats. They must be prepared to survive enemy air; artillery; and chemical, biological, radiological, or nuclear hazards with limited external assistance. Refer to ATP 3-39.30 for more information on threat categories levels II and III. 

3-89. Planners must coordinate for security and sustainment when employing signal sites remotely from the supported unit. Planners coordinate with the G-2 or S-2 for the current threat estimate and the G-3 or S-3 for security force arrangements when planning site defense. Members of the retransmission team assist in the coordinated defense of retransmission sites but require augmentation from the parent unit to adequately defend remote sites. 

###### **Logistical Supportability** 

3-90. Mission-critical materials must be planned, and throughput must be coordinated to transport items to the site. Stockpiling of unit sustainment supplies (rations, water, and fuel) may also be required. When there are no pre-positioned caches, it is possible to deliver resupply packages to isolated personnel by aircraft. The need for this type of activity should be weighed against the risk to the isolated person and the forces involved, and the capability to mitigate that risk (JP 3-50). 

3-91. Supply is essential for enhancing Soldiers’ quality of life and provides the materiel required to accomplish the mission. Platoon leaders develop the sustainment plan by determining what is on hand so they can accurately project requirements. Company leaders must know what designated critical supplies the platoon has on hand. The platoon leader establishes priorities for delivery; however, combat demands that class I, III, and IX supplies and equipment take priority for signal platoons because they are critical to successful operations. Resupply operations are either planned or emergency. Refer to ATP 6-02.2 for more information about sustainment of retransmission sites. 

##### **CONTINGENCY PLANNING** 

3-92. Leaders should ensure personnel understand the steps and can execute the contingency plan as necessary. Rehearsals should include crew drills such as requests for indirect fire support, contingency actions, actions on contact, and emergency destruction. Contingency plans include vehicle breakdowns, lost vehicles, and accidents. 

##### **ALTERNATE LOCATIONS** 

3-93. Signal leaders must ensure planned retransmission sites will continue to support the unit in case of displacement to alternate and supplementary fighting positions (ATP 6-02.2). Signal plans should include movement triggers for dislocation to alternate site locations. Triggers for evacuation or displacement to alternate locations may include— 

- Approaching enemy forces. 

- Receiving indirect fires. 

- Impending inclement weather. 

- Imminent wildfires.

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Planning** 

##### **COMPROMISE PROCEDURES** 

3-94. A COMSEC compromise occurs when COMSEC material is irretrievable or lost, information has been disclosed to unauthorized individuals, or a violation of the security policy for a system in which unauthorized intentional or unintentional disclosure, modification, or destruction may have occurred. Users experiencing compromised COMSEC should immediately stop transmission on the compromised system and notify the G-6 or S-6. At a minimum, operators should provide the following information to the G-6 or S-6: 

- Unit experiencing compromise. 

- Equipment type and nomenclature. 

- Crypto segment or crypto period. 

- Time and location of compromise. 

3-95. All radio operators must be able to recognize an incident that may develop into a COMSEC compromise and report it to the appropriate security authorities. Undetected or unreported incidents or compromises are serious security vulnerabilities. Reportable COMSEC incidents include— 

- Loss of accountability. 

- Improper destruction. 

- Possible tampering. 

- Found on installation. 

- Unsecured or unauthorized access. 

- Improper storage of COMSEC material or equipment. 

- Unauthorized use of keying material. 

- Loss of COMSEC material. 

- Loss of two-person integrity for top secret key material.

##### **Appendix A** 

### **Recognizing and Responding to Electromagnetic Attack** 

This appendix addresses tactics, techniques, and procedures to recognize and respond to threat effects and electromagnetic attack. Included are measures used to recognize and prevent threat activities and effects. 

#### **THREAT TACTICS, TECHNIQUES, AND PROCEDURES** 

A-1. Information and its management, dissemination, and control have always been critical to the successful conduct of tactical missions. Given today’s advancements in information and information systems technology, this importance is growing in scope, impact, and sophistication. The opposing force recognizes the unique opportunities that information warfare gives tactical commanders, and it continuously strives to incorporate information warfare activities in all tactical missions and battles (TC 7-100.2). 

A-2. A peer threat’s information warfare activities may include synchronized attack methods to create convergence of capabilities and their effects across multiple disciplines and domains to increase the probability of their success. Attacking across multiple domains and echelons, a peer threat will integrate electromagnetic attack, military deception, lethal fires, perception management, information attack, and computer warfare to deny U.S. and allied forces access to the EMS in a contested environment. 

A-3. Understanding threat capabilities in the EMS is key to sound signal support plans. Attacks on friendly command and control nodes may combine electromagnetic attacks, other information warfare effects, and lethal fires to deny friendly forces the use of spectrum-dependent systems. To accomplish this goal, threat forces gather technical and combat information about friendly forces. As threat forces locate and identify friendly units, threat information warfare elements establish priorities to— 

- Jam communications assets. 

- Deceptively enter radio networks. 

- Interfere with the normal flow of U.S. and allies’ communications. 

A-4. Commanders, their staffs, and equipment operators must train to recognize and react to peer threat information warfare tactics, techniques, and procedures if they are to continue communicating in a contested environment. 

#### **MEASURES TO PREVENT THREAT EFFECTS** 

A-5. There are operational tactics, techniques, and procedures signal planners and operators can take to mitigate peer threat capabilities in the EMS. If a threat cannot detect friendly signals, they cannot geolocate or jam those signals. 

###### **Limiting Electromagnetic Signature** 

<mark>A-6. To protect against a peer threat’s ability to locate and target radio signals, the G-6 or S-6 must plan signal support in a way that limits the electromagnetic signature of the signal site. Measures to reduce the electromagnetic signature of command posts and communications sites include—</mark> 

- <mark>Careful site selection for communications equipment.</mark> 

- <mark>Employment of directional antennas.</mark> 

- <mark>Operations using the lowest power required.</mark> 

- <mark>Limiting radio transmissions.</mark> 

- <mark>Using burst transmission to minimize transmission time.</mark> 

- <mark>Using a random battle rhythm schedule.</mark> 

<mark>A-7. Electromagnetic protection techniques can also help mask the electromagnetic signatures of command posts and communications sites. The CEWO assists the G-6 or S-6 in planning electromagnetic protection measures to reduce the command post’s electromagnetic signature.</mark> 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**41** 

**Appendix A** 

###### **_Terrain Masking_** 

A-8. Terrain masking can effectively block radio signals from reaching threat direction-finding capabilities. Positioning communications systems with large terrain features or man-made structures between the communications system and the forward line of own troops effectively blocks a threat from detecting the signal. 

###### **_Camouflage Net Masking_** 

A-9. Radar reflective camouflage netting is an effective means of blocking unintended electromagnetic radiation from the rear and sides of directional antennas. Camouflage netting to the sides and back of a line of sight or SATCOM antenna ensures only the main beam of the antenna radiates. This main beam is highly directional; it is much harder to detect since the enemy would need to be directly in the transmission path. 

###### **_Line of Sight_** 

A-10. High-throughput line of sight radios can carry high bandwidth data over distances up to 25 miles, but the links need to be engineered to minimize the chance of detection, targeting, and jamming. If the line of sight path is parallel to the forward line of troops, a threat is less likely to detect the signal, and threat jammers will be unable to reach the antenna with a signal strong enough to jam the radio. 

###### **_Remote Antennas_** 

A-11. Large command posts and high-throughput communications systems emit a significant amount of electromagnetic energy. While planners and operators can mask some of this energy with careful siting, terrain masking, and directional antennas, some electromagnetic energy remains. Because peer threats target friendly command and control capabilities, anything near the communications system is at risk of destruction from lethal fires. 

A-12. Commanders and signal planners should consider locating major communications assemblages as far from the supported command post as practical. Placing terrain features, man-made structures, or distance between communications systems and command posts provides the command post protection from lethal fires. Commanders and planners must consider the additional physical security and site defense requirements for a remote site during planning. 

###### **_Frequent Command Post Dislocation_** 

A-13. Despite all efforts to reduce and mask the electromagnetic signature of a command post, a peer threat is likely to locate it eventually. Moving a command post frequently reduces the chances of destruction. Frequent moves are especially important when operating within the range of enemy artillery. 

A-14. Maintaining continuity during displacement of a command post or catastrophic loss requires designating alternate command posts and passing control between command posts. During training exercises, units must practice frequent command post dislocation and handoff between the main and tactical command posts. 

##### **INTEGRATION WITH OTHER STAFF ELEMENTS** 

A-15. The G-6 or S-6 cannot plan signal support alone. Other staff sections’ functions and capabilities help inform sound, survivable signal support plans. Staff sections’ activities during the military decision-making process reinforce each other’s planning efforts. 

A-16. The signal planner, spectrum manager, CEWO, space operations officer, and G-2 or S-2 personnel assess threats to friendly communications during the military decision-making process. Integrated planning counters threat attempts to exploit vulnerabilities of friendly communications systems. When creating an electromagnetic protection plan, the CEWO and signal planners consider deployment, employment, replacement, and concealment of communications systems.

**Recognizing and Responding to Electromagnetic Attack** 

###### **Cyberspace Electromagnetic Activities Section** 

A-17. The CEMA section from brigade to corps coordinates and synchronizes cyberspace and electromagnetic warfare operations into the scheme of maneuver. This section includes the CEWO, the spectrum manager, the electromagnetic warfare technician, and electromagnetic warfare noncommissioned officers. The CEMA section is key to the collaboration of cyberspace and electromagnetic warfare operations. The CEWO is the cyberspace planner and understands the operations and missions of the unit and the commander’s intent. 

A-18. The CEMA section participates in the planning and targeting process and leads the CEMA working group to support the military decision-making process. The cyberspace planner submits requests for effects provided by non-organic resources. Other key staff members who assist in CEMA include the G-2 or S-2, G-3 or S-3, and G-6 or S-6. The CEMA section— 

- Plans, requests, and synchronizes effects in cyberspace and the EMS to support freedom of maneuver. 

- Coordinates with higher headquarters staff to integrate and synchronize information collection efforts to support cyberspace and electromagnetic warfare operations. 

- Synchronizes cyberspace and electromagnetic warfare effects requests with organic targeting capabilities. 

- Prepares and submits effect requests using the cyber effects request format or electromagnetic attack request format. 

- Develops, maintains, and disseminates cyberspace and EMS input for the common operational picture. 

- Deconflicts spectrum use for electromagnetic warfare capabilities with the G-6 or S-6 spectrum manager. 

- Provide recommendations on commander’s critical information requirements. 

###### **G-6 or S-6** 

A-19. The G-6 or S-6 section prepares annex H (Signal) to the operation plan or order, with appropriate cross-references to the electromagnetic warfare portions of annex C (Operations), operations security portion of annex E (Protection), the military deception plan, and the signal operating instructions. The G-6 or S-6 integrates and coordinates DODIN operations and spectrum management operations through close collaboration with the CEMA section during planning and operations. The G-6 or S-6 also— 

- Plans redundant means of communications for those systems most vulnerable to jamming. 

- Provides COMSEC to protect systems most vulnerable to threat information collection. 

- Plans measures to protect critical friendly frequencies from intentional and unintentional electromagnetic interference. 

- Enforces proper use of radio, electromagnetic protection, and transmission security procedures. 

- Performs spectrum management, including designating alternate frequencies for radio networks and modeling the electromagnetic environment. 

- Publishes and updates signal operating instructions. 

- Disseminates the joint restricted frequency list—taboo, protected, and guarded frequencies. 

- Implements cybersecurity on the network and automated information systems. 

###### **G-3 or S-3** 

A-20. The G-3 or S-3 is the staff lead in planning and the conduct of Army operations. During the planning and execution of electromagnetic warfare and signal support, the G-3 or S-3— 

- Exercises staff responsibility for electromagnetic protection. 

- Includes electromagnetic support and electromagnetic attack scenarios in all command post and field training exercises. 

- Evaluates electromagnetic protection techniques employed. 

- Includes electromagnetic protection in unit training.

**Appendix A** 

###### **Cyber Electromagnetic Warfare Officer** 

A-21. The CEWO plans, coordinates, and directs the execution of electromagnetic warfare activities. The CEWO is also the lead for the CEMA section. The CEWO— 

- Serves as subject matter expert on threat electromagnetic warfare capabilities and electromagnetic warfare rules of engagement. 

- Leads the CEMA working group. 

- Submits information requirements to the G-2 or S-2 to support electromagnetic warfare planning and assessment. 

- Supports the G-2 or S-2 during intelligence preparation of the operational environment. 

- Coordinates with the G-6 or S-6 to plan, implement, and assess friendly electromagnetic protection measures. 

- Coordinates with the G-6 or S-6 spectrum manager to ensure planned electromagnetic warfare activities do not interfere with friendly communications. 

- Deconflicts electromagnetic warfare operations with the CEMA spectrum manager. 

- Collaborates with the G-2 or S-2 to synchronize and deconflict electromagnetic warfare operations with intelligence activities. 

###### **Space Operations Officer** 

A-22. The primary task of the space operations officer to advise the supported commander of current space assessment from planning through mission execution. Their responsibility includes providing commanders insight on effective space-related planning, operational support, and space capabilities. These tasks require an expert knowledge of space-based systems and situational awareness of the space domain and its operations to include an understanding of the process for acquiring space support. 

A-23. Space operations officers integrate space-based capabilities and effects in conjunction with the support staff and designated senior officer responsible for coordinating space requirements. They assist in identifying requirements based on available capabilities and needed materials for a unit to fulfill their mission. A space operations officer’s role includes providing space-related support and expertise on space capabilities. Additional responsibilities include— 

- Providing information on space-based capabilities to enhance the commander’s situational awareness and common operational picture. 

   - Recommending mitigation actions to the commander and staff elements. 

   - Analyzing space and terrestrial weather impacts to mission. 

   - Coordinating planning activities with internal and external organizations. 

- Assisting the G-6 in maximizing SATCOM support to operations. 

   - Understanding SATCOM architecture. 

   - Assessing impact of loss of SATCOM operations and recommend issue solutions. 

   - Integrating space capabilities and synchronize effect. 

- Assessing GPS satellite technical capabilities. 

   - Assessing requirements and gaps consistent with space technology resolutions. 

   - Analyzing the impacts of navigation warfare and ensure PNT considerations are integrated into plans and operations. 

- Recommending ways to deny threat use of all satellite-based navigation systems, in coordination with G-2. 

   - Assessing timing and impacts of multiplexing of communications in coordinate with G-6. 

- Coordinating with the CEMA section, G-2 or S-2, and G-6 or S-6 for space-based environmental monitoring and terrestrial weather impacts to operations. 

   - Assessing space-based and terrestrial weather and their effects on forecasting accuracy. 

   - Analyzing weather impacts to assets including sensors, electro-optical, multispectral imagery, and infrared detection and data collection capabilities. 

###### **G-2 or S-2** 

A-24. The G-2 or S-2 provides current intelligence estimates during the military decision-making process, and updated estimates during operations. The intelligence estimate includes—
