**Recognizing and Responding to Electromagnetic Attack** 

- Current enemy tactics, techniques, and procedures. 

- Enemy locations in relation to friendly forces. 

- The enemy’s electromagnetic order of battle—enemy capabilities that could be used to deny friendly use of the EMS. 

- Known cyberspace threats to inform cybersecurity and defensive cyberspace operations efforts. 

#### **RECOGNIZING AND RESPONDING TO THREAT EFFECTS AND ELECTROMAGNETIC INTERFERENCE** 

A-25. Because peer threats consider friendly command and control capabilities to be high priority targets, they have developed capabilities to deny friendly use of the EMS for communications. Soldiers and leaders must learn to identify and respond to threat effects in the EMS. 

###### **Electromagnetic Jamming** 

A-26. _Electromagnetic jamming_ is the deliberate radiation, reradiation, or reflection of electromagnetic energy for the purpose of preventing or reducing an enemy’s effective use of the electromagnetic spectrum, with the intent of degrading or neutralizing the enemy’s combat capability (JP 3-85). 

A-27. Jammers operate against receivers, not transmitters. Two modes of jamming are spot and barrage jamming. Spot jamming concentrates power on one channel or frequency. Barrage jamming is power spread over several frequencies or channels at the same time. It is important to recognize jamming, but it can be difficult to detect. 

###### **Communications Jamming** 

A-28. Radio operators must learn to recognize and react to electromagnetic jamming. This is not always an easy task since interference can be either internal or external. Other sources having nothing to do with jamming may cause electromagnetic interference. Unintentional electromagnetic interference may be caused by— 

- Other radios (friendly and enemy). 

- Other electronic, electrical, or electromechanical equipment. 

- Atmospheric conditions. 

- Equipment malfunction. 

A-29. Radio operators must train to differentiate internal and external interference quickly. Electromagnetic jamming most commonly affects single-channel radio systems. These radios include HF, VHF, and UHF radios. Jamming effects may be obvious or subtle. Obvious jamming is normally simple to detect. When experiencing jamming, it is more important to recognize and overcome the incident than to identify it formally. 

A-30. Subtle jamming is less obvious because subtle jamming signals produce no sound from the receivers. Although everything may appear normal to the radio operator, the receiver cannot receive an incoming friendly signal. Often, users assume their radios are malfunctioning instead of recognizing subtle jamming. Table A-1 on page 46 lists common jamming signals. Refer to GTA 13-01-001 for detailed information on electromagnetic attack tactics, techniques, and procedures. Additional information is listed at the Cyber Lessons and Best Practices website. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**45** 

**Appendix A** 

**Table A-1. Common jamming signals** 

|**_Signal_**|**_Description_**|
|---|---|
|Random Noise|Synthetic radio noise. Random noise is indiscriminate<br>in amplitude and frequency. It is similar to normal<br>background noise and can degrade all types of signals.<br>Operators often mistake random noise jamming for<br>receiver or atmospheric noise and fail to take<br>appropriate electromagneticprotection actions.|
|Stepped<br>Tones|Tones transmitted in increasing and decreasing pitch.<br>They resemble the sound of bagpipes. Stepped tones<br>are effective against single-channel amplitude<br>modulation or frequencymodulation voice circuits.|
|Spark|Bursts of short duration and high intensity; they are<br>repeated at a rapid rate. This signal is effective in<br>disrupting all types of radio communications. Spark<br>jamming is easy to produce and an effective jamming<br>signal.|
|Gulls|Quickly rising and slowly falling variable radio<br>frequency. The effect produced is similar to the cry of a<br>seagull. Gulls produce a nuisance effect and are very<br>effective against voice radio communications.|
|Random<br>Pulse|Pulses of varying amplitude, duration, and rate. Pulses<br>disrupt radar and various data transmission systems.|
|Recorded<br>Sounds|Any audible sound, especially of a variable nature.<br>Recorded sounds can distract radio operators and<br>disrupt communications. Music, screams, applause,<br>whistles,machinerynoise,and laughter are examples.|
|Wobbler|A single frequency, modulated by a low and slowly<br>varying tone. The result is a howling sound that causes<br>a nuisance effect on voice radio communications.|
|Preamble Jamming|A tone resembling the synchronization preamble of the<br>speech security equipment broadcast over the<br>operating frequency of secure radio sets. Results in all<br>radios being locked in the receive mode. Preamble<br>jamming is especially effective when employed against<br>radio networks that use speech securitydevices.|



###### **_Preventive Measures_** 

A-31. Measures operators and planners can use to reduce susceptibility to jamming include— 

- Minimize radio transmissions (optimal radio transmissions are six seconds or less). 

- Use electromagnetic counter-countermeasures, such as frequency hopping. 

- Train using radio silence. 

- Use low power setting on radios for normal operations to reduce the probability of detection. 

- Use terrain masking to reduce the probability of detection and block potential sources of jamming. 

###### **_Indicators_** 

A-32. The threat strives to perfect and use new and more confusing forms of jamming, which requires radio operators to be increasingly alert to the possibility of jamming. Training and experience allow operators to determine whether a particular signal is a jamming signal. During operations, radio operators should remain alert to possible jamming indicators. Observable indications of jamming include— 

- Apparently random noise or static over voice channels. 

- Recorded sounds—messages or music—over voice channels. 

- No answer to a radio transmission. 

**46** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Recognizing and Responding to Electromagnetic Attack** 

###### **_Reaction_** 

A-33. Communications jamming requires prompt corrective action to restore critical communications capabilities. Possible reactions to jamming include— 

- **Continuing to operate.** Enemy jamming usually involves a period of jamming followed by a brief listening period. Operator activity during this short period indicates to threats whether their jamming efforts were successful. Continuing to operate normally gives the enemy no indication of success or failure. If the enemy hears discussion of the problem on the air, or radio the operation terminates, the enemy may assume their jamming is effective. Operators should never terminate operation of a radio network unless ordered to do so. Operators should be careful not to disclose adverse effects of enemy jamming. This means normal operations should continue even when degraded by jamming. 

- **Increasing transmitter power output.** Using low power for normal operations minimizes the chance of detection. Once the enemy begins jamming the radios, the risk of detection becomes secondary to the radio delivering required communications. Higher radio power may overcome the enemy’s jamming signal but increases the risk of detection by enemy direction finding capabilities. 

- **Improving the signal-to-jamming ratio.** The signal-to-jamming ratio is the relative strength of the desired signal to the jamming signal at the receiver. If the desired signal is much stronger than the jamming signal, the jamming does not significantly degrade communications. To improve the signal-to-jamming ratio operators and signal leaders can consider the following— 

   - **Adjusting or changing the antenna.** When jamming occurs, the radio operator should adjust the antenna to receive the maximum incoming signal strength. Depending on the antenna, some methods include reorienting the antenna, changing antenna polarization at all stations, or installing an antenna with a greater range. 

   - **Establishing a retransmission site.** A retransmission site can increase the effective range and power of a signal between radio stations without increasing transmit power. 

   - **Relocating the antenna.** Operators may use terrain masking to block the incoming jamming signal. This may require relocating the antenna and associated radio setup to several hundred meters from its previous location. 

- **Changing frequencies.** If a communications network cannot overcome enemy jamming, the commander may direct forces using an alternate or spare frequency. Preplanned and wellcoordinated actions are required for practical dummy stations to continue to operate on the jammed frequency to mask the change to an alternate frequency. During a jamming incident, it may be difficult to coordinate a frequency change. All radio operators require knowledge of when, and under what circumstances, they should switch to a backup frequency. If the frequency change is not smooth, the enemy may discover what is happening and try to degrade communications on the new frequency. 

- **Executing the PACE plan.** Quickly changing to the alternate or contingency means of communications reduces disruption of the command and control system. 

- **Using signals intelligence or electromagnetic warfare capabilities to locate the jamming signal.** Leveraging signals intelligence or electromagnetic warfare capabilities requires coordination and collaboration with the G-2 or S-2 and the CEWO. 

A-34. If any of the corrective actions taken mitigate the enemy jamming, operators should continue operation of the network and submit a joint spectrum interference resolution (JSIR) report to higher headquarters. JSIR reports document a history of problems and help identify possible causes for subsequent interference. Maintaining a historical record of interference helps develop countermeasures to future jamming incidents. Refer to ATP 6-02.70 for more information about JSIR reporting. 

###### **Positioning, Navigation, and Timing Jamming** 

A-35. A typical brigade combat team has over 2,500 PNT-enabled devices and over 250 SATCOM-enabled devices that are used to conduct precision fires, movement, maneuver, communications, protection, command and control, and other mission requirements. Peer threats have capabilities to contest the ground, space, and link segments of satellite-based PNT. These attacks may have significant impacts across all warfighting functions and many weapon platforms. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**47** 

**Appendix A** 

- A-36. Electromagnetic jamming of PNT satellite capabilities affects not only communications, but also many other capabilities in tactical formations. Systems affected include— 

   - Communications systems. 

   - Friendly force tracking. 

   - Navigation. 

   - Reconnaissance. 

   - Radar systems. 

   - Precision guided munitions. 

###### **_Preventive Measures_** 

- A-37. Measures to reduce susceptibility to, and mitigate the effects of, PNT jamming include— 

   - Using only encrypted PNT systems. 

   - Employing antenna masking. 

   - Using terrain masking. 

   - Training and maintaining the ability to navigate using a map and compass. 

###### **_Indicators_** 

- A-38. User indications that an enemy may be jamming PNT signals include— 

   - Loss of satellite signal. 

   - Red GPS icon on the network management system. 

   - Loss of timing or incorrect time displayed on equipment. 

   - Inaccurate map display or imprecise position or geolocation indicator data. 

   - Jamming environment warning message. 

###### **_Reaction_** 

- A-39. Because of the diverse and widespread effects of enemy GPS jamming, a prompt, coordinated response is necessary. Operators of all affected systems should— 

   - **Navigate using map and compass.** While this does not restore system timing and situational awareness displays, navigation using a map and compass cannot be jammed. 

   - **Increase distance between affected systems and jammer.** If the jammer operates from a known location, increased distance or terrain masking may mitigate interference. 

   - **Use signals intelligence or electromagnetic warfare capabilities to locate the jamming signal.** Leveraging signals intelligence or electromagnetic warfare capabilities requires coordination and collaboration with the G-2 or S-2 and the CEWO. 

   - **Report jamming to higher headquarters.** Submitting a JSIR report to higher headquarters documents a history of problems and helps identify possible causes for subsequent interference. 

###### **Satellite Communications Jamming** 

A-40. Expeditionary forces rely heavily on SATCOM capabilities for beyond line of sight network transport. Systems and capabilities affected by SATCOM jamming include— 

- Friendly force tracking. 

- Wideband SATCOM. 

- TACSAT radios. 

- Intelligence reporting systems. 

###### **_Preventive Measures_** 

- A-41. Operational and employment measures to prevent SATCOM jamming include— 

   - Minimizing transmissions on narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT radios (radio transmissions six seconds or less is optimal). 

   - Using terrain masking. 

   - Using camouflage net masking. 

**48** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Recognizing and Responding to Electromagnetic Attack** 

###### **_Indicators_** 

A-42. Possible operator indications of satellite jamming include— 

- Seemingly random noise or static on narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT radios. 

- Recorded sounds, such as messages or music, over narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT radios. 

- No answer to transmission. 

- Red satellite icon on network management system display. 

- Loss of data from the satellite. 

- Low signal-to-noise indicated on wideband satellite terminal. 

###### **_Reaction_** 

A-43. The reactive measures listed here apply mostly to narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT systems. When a narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT radio operator recognizes a jamming attempt, they may— 

- **Increase radio transmit power.** Only increase power on wideband SATCOM terminals if directed by the satellite controller. 

- **Change to a preapproved alternate frequency.** Minimizes electromagnetic footprint of communications systems operating within the EMS environment. 

- **Execute the PACE plan.** Quickly changing to the alternate or contingency means of communications reduces disruption of the command and control system. 

- **Use signals intelligence or electromagnetic warfare capabilities to locate the jamming signal.** Leveraging signals intelligence or electromagnetic warfare capabilities requires coordination and collaboration with the G-2 or S-2 or the CEWO. 

- **Report jamming to higher headquarters.** The higher headquarters’ frequency manager and CEWO can correlate reports from units across the assigned area to isolate enemy jammers and plan countermeasures, including nominating targets for lethal fires. 

- **Use line of sight systems for network transport.** Units cannot communicate beyond line of sight, or through significant physical obstacles. 

#### **ELECTROMAGNETIC INTERFERENCE** 

A-44. With the increased use of the EMS and complex, sophisticated radio systems and electronic devices, there is increased vulnerability to electromagnetic interference. _Electromagnetic interference_ is any electromagnetic disturbance, induced intentionally or unintentionally, that interrupts, obstructs, or otherwise degrades or limits the effective performance of electromagnetic spectrum-dependent systems and electrical equipment (JP 3-85). 

#### **TYPES OF ELECTROMAGNETIC INTERFERENCE** 

A-45. Electromagnetic interference can be caused by threat, neutral, friendly, or natural sources. Electromagnetic interference from natural sources, or natural electromagnetic interference, includes geographic electromagnetic phenomena, atmospheric noise (thunderstorms), and galactic noise (stars). Electromagnetic interference from man-made sources includes power lines, power generators, alarm systems, electrified railroads, and fluorescent lights. 

A-46. Since electromagnetic interference prevents successful radio transmission, units must recognize potential internal and external sources of electromagnetic interference, both intentional and unintentional. They must then take appropriate action to minimize its effects on friendly communications. 

###### **Environmental** 

A-47. Weather data and other naturally occurring phenomena are part of the information required by commanders and staffs when planning, preparing, executing, and assessing combat operations. Naturally occurring subsurface and surface phenomena create hazards that may endanger personnel or cause electromagnetic interference. Advanced knowledge of electromagnetic interference resulting from weather or environmental factors allows ground forces to avoid adverse conditions while taking advantage of other 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**49** 

**Appendix A** 

conditions that enhance operations. Natural weather or space environmental factors that can affect signal transmission include— 

- Solar radio bursts. 

- Noise storms. 

- Ionospheric scintillation. 

- Aurora activity. 

- Solar flares. 

A-48. The Air Force Space Weather Operations Center issues real-time and forecast space weather data addressing natural electromagnetic interference from solar radio bursts or noise storms, ionospheric scintillation, aurora activity, or solar flares. To establish the presence of solar electromagnetic interference, planners determine whether the link line of sight is aligned with the sun and whether the side lobes of the antenna intersect the sun’s radio frequency output. For ionospheric scintillation, planners determine whether communication links intersect a region of the ionospheric scintillation. 

A-49. Planners analyze weather data, identify potential weather effects, and assess the impact of weather on systems, and provide vital information to optimally employ communications assets. Planners and signal personnel should consider local weather conditions when assessing electromagnetic interference causes and effects as well as Space Force-provided data, alerts, and warnings on meteorological, oceanographic, and space environment factors that could affect military operations. 

###### **Cosite** 

A-50. Cosite interference is the effect of unwanted energy, due to emissions, radiation, or induction, on reception in a radio communications system. This could cause system performance degradation, misinterpretation, or loss of information. Several options are available to mitigate cosite interference, but there are no comprehensive solutions. The user must decide if an option is applicable to the tactical situation and take the appropriate action to resolve cosite interference. 

A-51. Some equipment systems are not as critical as others. The G-6 or S-6 recommends a system priority list that ensures the transmission of mission-critical information. During interference events, the G-6 or S-6 should be prepared to shut down less critical systems to preserve the functionality of mission-critical systems. To reduce cosite interference— 

- **Transmit at the lowest power level when possible.** This allows co-located SINCGARS and other transmission systems to operate with minimal interference at <mark>the receivers.</mark> This option may be unacceptable due to the significant transmission range reduction of the SINCGARS. 

- **Locate antennas remotely and transmit from the command post at low power to a full power retransmission system.** Certain critical command post networks would then be able to maintain their high-power advantage. 

- **Separate antennas as much as possible when in the same or nearby frequency ranges.** The greater the separation between the transmitting and receiving antennas, the less interference encountered. Tilting the tops of the transmitting and receiving antennas away from each other can enhance vertically polarized ground wave communications. Tilt angles from 15–30 degrees provide the best results. 

#### **JOINT SPECTRUM INTERFERENCE RESOLUTION** 

A-52. The JSIR report addresses electromagnetic interference incidents, electromagnetic attack, and directed energy affecting the DOD. The objective is to report and help resolve electromagnetic attack and persistent, recurring electromagnetic interference. Units must report all electromagnetic interference disturbances regardless of the severity, intensity, or duration. Users encountering electromagnetic interference submit reports of suspected hostile electromagnetic interference using the secure JSIR-Online Portal. Users should not delay submitting reports if information is not readily available. They should use follow-up reports to provide additional information as it becomes available. 

A-53. Table A-2 on page 51 shows the minimum information requirements for JSIR reporting. The message subject line should indicate whether the report is initial.

A-54. It is critical for users to attempt to resolve electromagnetic interference problems at the lowest possible level before submitting JSIR reports to higher headquarters. The unit experiencing the electromagnetic interference incident forwards the initial JSIR report through the chain of command to the unit operations center. 

A-55. Electronic report submissions are recorded using the joint spectrum management system or Spectrum XXI programs. The sender classifies the report by evaluating the security sensitivity of the electromagnetic interference on the affected system, and by considering the classification of the text comments. 

A-56. Spectrum managers assign precedence to the JSIR report consistent with the urgency of the reported situation. Routine or priority precedence is used for JSIR reports, unless the organization originating the report believes the incident is hazardous to military operations. Table A-3 on page 52 is an example security classification guide used in JSIR reporting. 

A-57. Each Army unit submits reports through its chain of command, up to the theater army, the combatant command, and to the United States Army Communications-Electronics Services Office. Users submit

information copies of incident reports to the Joint Spectrum Center for inclusion in the JSIR database. Refer to CJCSM 3320.02E for additional information on JSIR reporting procedures. 

**Table A-3. Joint spectrum interference resolution security classification guide** 

|**_Information Revealing_**|**_Security Classification_**|
|---|---|
|The specific identification of an unfriendly platform or location, by<br>country or coordinates, as the source of interference or<br>electromagnetic attack.|Secret.|
|Specific susceptibility or vulnerability of U.S. electromagnetic<br>equipment and systems.|Secret.|
|Parametric data of classified U.S. electromagnetic equipment.|In accordance with the<br>classification guide of the affected<br>equipment.|
|Suspected interference from unidentified sources while operating in<br>or near hostile countries.|Secret.|
|Interference to U.S. electromagnetic equipment and systems<br>caused byelectromagnetic attack exercises in foreign nations.|Confidential.|
|Suspected interference from friendlysources.|Unclassified or secret (if the report<br>reveals a specific equipment<br>vulnerability).|
|Information referring to joint spectrum interference resolution<br>reporting stating that joint spectrum interference reporting analyses<br>are a function of the Joint Spectrum Center.|Unclassified.|



#### **INTERFERENCE RESPONSE** 

A-58. To avoid future electromagnetic interference, units must respond in a consistent, methodical manner. One way to reduce the operational impact of electromagnetic interference is to incorporate preventive measures that identify and mitigate electromagnetic interference effects. By instituting best practices and lessons learned, units can also reduce future interference that disrupts communication and minimizes the negative impact of electromagnetic interference on operations. The electromagnetic interference battle drill example at table A-4 provides appropriate responses to electromagnetic interference. Refer to CJCSM 3320.02E for information about electromagnetic interference resolution. 

**Table A-4. Electromagnetic interference battle drill** 

|**_Step_**|**_Description_**|
|---|---|
|1|Follow equipment troubleshooting steps (verify frequency, cable, antenna<br>connections, and communications security). If interference continues, follow steps<br>2 through 6.|
|2|Determine start and stoptimes or duration of interference.|
|3|Identifyinterference effect(interferingvoice,noise,or static).|
|4|Identifyother emitters in the assigned area.|
|5|Check adjacent and nearbyunits for similarproblems.|
|6|Prepare and submit a joint spectrum interference resolution report through the G-6<br>or S-6 spectrum manager.|
|**Legend:**<br>G-6<br>S-6|assistant chief of staff, signal<br>battalion or brigade signal staff officer|

#### **EMISSION CONTROL** 

A-60. _Emission control_ is the selective and controlled use of electromagnetic, acoustic, or other emitters to optimize command and control capabilities while minimizing, for operations security: a. detection by enemy sensors, b. mutual interference among friendly systems, and/or c. enemy interference with the ability to execute a military deception plan (JP 3-85). 

##### **EMISSION CONTROL CONDITIONS** 

A-61. EMCON prevents the threat from discovering and attacking the locations of friendly forces with electromagnetic warfare or lethal fires. It is important to understand the general categories and status criteria for EMCON conditions. Based on the tactical situation or operational environment, the commander can dictate the appropriate EMCON condition. During operations, commanders should consider EMCON condition 3 (amber) as the baseline condition. Units should routinely execute transitions to EMCON 4 (red) and EMCON 5 (black) during conflict. 

A-62. Table A-5 on page 55 captures the five EMCON conditions and the general descriptive criteria associated with each condition. The EMCON emitter matrix establishes the operational criteria for emitters at each EMCON condition and works in conjunction with the PACE plan. Every emitter within the unit should be included and the corresponding criteria described. The CEWO assists the G-3 or S-3 and the G-6 or S-6 in determining operating criteria for each type of emitter for each EMCON condition. Refer to FM 3- 12 for staff responsibilities including information on planning, coordination, and execution of electromagnetic protection activities. 

**54** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Recognizing and Responding to Electromagnetic Attack** 

**Table A-5. Emission control conditions** 

|**EMCON Condition**|**Description**|
|---|---|
|**EMCON 1**<br>**Green**|Describes a situation where there isno apparent hostile activityagainst<br>friendly emitter operations. Operational performance of all spectrum-<br>dependent systems is monitored; password and encryption systems are<br>used as a layer ofprotection.|
|**EMCON 2**<br>**Yellow**|Describes anincreased risk of attackafter detection. Increased monitoring<br>of all EMS activities is mandated, and all Department of Defense end users<br>must make sure their systems are secure, encrypted, power levels<br>monitored, and transmissions limited. EMS usage may be restricted to<br>certain emitters. Rehearsal for elevated EMCON conditions is ideal.|
|**EMCON 3**<br>**Amber**|Describes when arisk has been identified. Electromagnetic counter-<br>countermeasures (encryption/frequency hop/directional antennas) on<br>important systems are a priority, and the CEWO’s alertness is increased.<br>All unencrypted systems are disconnected.|
|**EMCON 4**<br>**Red**|Describes when anattack has taken place,but the EMCON system is not<br>at its highest alertness. Non-essential emitters may be taken offline,<br>alternate or contingency means of communication may be implemented,<br>and modifications are made to standard lower EMCON configurations<br>(power levels and antenna types).|
|**EMCON 5**<br>**Black**|Describes whenattacks are taking placewithin the EMS. The most<br>restrictive electromagnetic protection methods are enforced. Any<br>compromised systems are isolated from the rest of the network.|
|**LEGEND**<br>CEWO<br>EMCON<br>EMS|cyber electromagnetic warfare officer<br>emission control<br>electromagnetic spectrum|

##### **EMISSION CONTROL TECHNIQUES** 

A-64. Users of RF-enabled devices adhere to EMCON techniques to prevent unintentional transmission of signals. These signals, if intercepted and analyzed, could compromise the mission. Appropriate countermeasures should be applied along with applicable operations security guidelines for that device or radio to prevent exploitation of compromising emanations.

##### **Appendix B** 

### **Retransmission Team Training and Certification** 

Retransmission team certification consists of training and testing team members to perform critical individual and collective tasks and drills to an established standard. Section I provides detailed information to implement signal assessment tables I–IV, section II covers team training, and section III provides battle drills in which the team must be proficient. 

##### **SECTION I – Signal Assessment Tables** 

B-1. Signal assessment tables provide a tiered approach to collective signal training. The tables provide a crawl, walk, run, training methodology from individual through team, section, platoon, and higher collective tasks. Tables I–IV provide a training framework for individual and team tasks. The individual and collective training tasks within this section comply with training developed and approved by the Army Training and Development Capability office. Refer to FM 7-0 or the Army Training Network Website, for more information on building effective unit training plans. 

#### **TABLE I – INDIVIDUAL TRAINING** 

B-2. During table I training, team chiefs train Soldiers to conduct the individual tasks that support the team’s collective tasks. Leaders train Soldiers on equipment maintenance during table I training. Team chiefs train their teams using table I and determine when individuals are prepared for table II certification. Table I training includes personnel and equipment training. 

##### **PERSONNEL TRAINING** 

B-3. Military training improves the capabilities of Soldiers in their respective roles. Team chiefs ensure Soldiers complete training in the following— 

- Role-based cyberspace workforce training. 

- Individual task training. 

- Preventive maintenance. 

- AR 350-1 training requirements. 

###### **Role-Based Cyberspace Workforce Training** 

B-4. Cyberspace workforce training unifies the overall cyberspace workforce and establishes specific workforce elements for information technology, cybersecurity, cyberspace effects, intelligence, and enablers to align and manage the cyberspace workforce. During table I training, Soldiers complete cyberspace workforce training based on their respective roles. The minimum cyberspace workforce training for general purpose users is the DOD cyber awareness challenge. 

###### **Individual Task Training** 

B-5. Team chiefs document individual task training in the Digital Training Management System and the Soldier’s individual training record. Leaders monitor Soldier training and individual task completion to certify proficiency in technical, tactical, and leadership skills. Individual task training includes common task training and military occupational specialty task training. 

###### **_Common Tasks_** 

B-6. Team chiefs enforce common tasks taught to Soldiers during initial training. These tasks are considered critical for the Soldiers’ survival. Retransmission teams must be proficient in all common warrior tasks and battle drills. Warrior tasks and battle drills enhance a Soldier’s readiness. Warrior tasks are a collection of individual Soldier skills known to be critical to a Soldier’s survival. Refer to STP 21-1-SMCT for specific tasks. Examples of common tasks include— 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**57** 

**Appendix B** 

- Weapons training. 

- Tactical communications. 

- Urban operations. 

- Tactical combat casualty care— 

   - First aid. 

   - Self-aid buddy care. 

###### **_Military Occupational Specialty Tasks_** 

B-7. Team chiefs ensure Soldiers receive training specific to their military role, including the use of any specialty equipment. Soldiers perform and reinforce military occupational specialty skills during unit training events. Individual tasks for retransmission teams include— 

- Task 113-25U-1003, Operate Radio Retransmission Station. 

- Task 113-611-1000, Select a Radio Retransmission Site. 

- Task 113-25U-3004, Direct Retransmission Operations. 

- Task 113-596-1006, Install Antenna Group OE-254. 

- Task 113-100-0002, Install COM 201 Antenna. 

- Task 113-000-1001, Operate the Radio Retransmission Station using SINCGARS Family of Radios. 

- Task 113-2kw-0001, Operate Generator Set 2 kilowatts. 

- Task 113-2kw-0000, Perform Field-Level Maintenance on Generator Set 2 kilowatts. 

###### **Preventive Maintenance** 

B-8. Preventive maintenance checks and services identify and correct faults and ensure continued operation of the equipment. Soldiers train to conduct preventive maintenance specified in the equipment technical manual. 

###### **AR 350-1 Training Requirements** 

B-9. AR 350-1 training requirements cover unit and individual training. Team leaders monitor training throughout the year and ensure Soldiers complete Army training. Examples of AR 350-1 training includes— 

- Anti-Terrorism Level 1. 

- Combating trafficking in person. 

- Controlled unclassified information. 

##### **EQUIPMENT TRAINING** 

B-10. Units conduct individual equipment training and qualification to certify Soldiers on their assigned equipment. Equipment training includes— 

- Supply discipline. 

- Operator maintenance. 

- Driver training. 

- Weapons training. 

- COMSEC. 

###### **Supply Discipline** 

B-11. Team chiefs enforce supply discipline by maintaining equipment accountability of all classes of supplies. Team chiefs must enforce programs to ensure resources are used without fraud, waste, and abuse. 

###### **Operator Maintenance** 

B-12. Operators perform preventive maintenance checks and services on their assigned equipment. Based on the mission, operators diagnose faults and replace parts and subcomponents from available spares. Team chiefs ensure Soldiers conduct operator level preventive maintenance according to the technical manual maintenance schedule. 

**58** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Retransmission Team Training and Certification** 

###### **Driver Training** 

B-13. Driver training qualifies Soldiers to operate the team’s assigned vehicle. Retransmission teams are expected to conduct operations at night, including tactical movement to and displacement from retransmission sites. Soldiers must be able to operate vehicles at night during blackout conditions and adverse weather conditions. Soldiers must be proficient in the use of night observation devices. Driver training includes— 

- Driver responsibilities. 

- Laws and regulations. 

- Basic operating procedures. 

- Emergency response procedures. 

- Accident causes and reporting. 

- Hazards and safety measures. 

- Operation of vehicles with trailers attached. 

- Operation of vehicles on steep slopes. 

- Operation of government vehicles off post. 

- Transportation of personnel. 

- Maintenance responsibilities and forms. 

- Convoy operations. 

- Driving using night observation devices. 

- Hands-on training. 

- • Performance road test. 

###### **Weapons Training** 

B-14. Units conduct individual weapons training and qualification to certify Soldiers on their assigned weapons. Soldiers who fail to qualify will participate in retraining and qualification. 

###### **Communications Security** 

B-15. Soldiers train to use and protect COMSEC devices and keying material. For more information about COMSEC, refer to ATP 6-02.75. COMSEC training includes— 

- Key loading. 

- Emergency destruction. 

- Over-the-air rekeying. 

- Incident reporting. 

- Material accountability. 

#### **TABLE II – INDIVIDUAL SKILLS TEST** 

B-16. During table II skill testing, a staff sergeant or above certifies a Soldier’s proficiency in the individual skills trained during table I training. Soldiers certify on personnel and equipment tasks. 

B-17. In table II, Soldiers will take an individual skills test that certifies their competence on the supporting tasks necessary to install, operate, and maintain their assigned system. Soldiers must complete a physical fitness test, qualify on assigned weapon, complete role-based cyberspace workforce training, and recommended by their team chief as proficient in their individual supporting tasks. 

##### **PERSONNEL CERTIFICATION** 

B-18. Soldiers will be trained to conduct all supporting individual tasks in the system they are assigned. They will be trained and resourced for other individual certifications required to perform their tasks to include— 

- Role-based cyberspace workforce certification. 

- Individual task proficiency. 

- Physical fitness test qualification. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**59** 

**Appendix B** 

###### **Role-Based Cyberspace Workforce Certification** 

B-19. The certifying leader verifies cyberspace workforce certification through the Army Training and Certification Tracking System Website. The certifying leader documents completion according to the unit SOP. 

###### **Proficiency in Individual Tasks** 

B-20. The certifying leader evaluates Soldiers’ proficiency in individual common and military occupational specialty tasks. Proficiency is achieved when Soldiers perform a training task to standard. 

###### **_Common Tasks_** 

B-21. The Soldier’s manual of common tasks provides information to plan, prepare, train, and evaluate individual training of warrior tasks. Refer to STP 21-1-SMCT for training and evaluation outlines for common tasks. 

###### **_Military Occupational Specialty Tasks_** 

B-22. Certifying leaders verify Soldiers’ proficiency in the individual tasks that support retransmission operations. The evaluation checklists are available on the Army Training Network Website. 

###### **Physical Fitness Test** 

B-23. Certifying leaders verify the Soldier has passed a physical fitness test through the unit training noncommissioned officer. Soldiers who fail their physical fitness test are subject to administrative action and retest. 

##### **EQUIPMENT CERTIFICATION** 

B-24. Equipment certification provides a guide to the unit ensuring Soldiers meet proficiency standards. Records of certification are retained for training management purposes. Certifying leaders verify the following tasks— 

- Weapons qualification. 

- Vehicle licensing. 

- Equipment accountability. 

###### **Weapons Qualification** 

B-25. Soldiers qualify on weapons assigned to their team. Certifying leaders verify weapons qualification through the company training records. 

###### **Vehicle Licensing** 

B-26. Retransmission teams require at least two licensed drivers for the team vehicle. Certifying leaders verify valid licenses through inspection. 

###### **Equipment Accountability** 

B-27. Table II certification includes verifying accountability of assigned equipment. Certifying leaders conduct hand receipt inspections and equipment layouts to verify equipment accountability. 

#### **TABLE III – TEAM TRAINING** 

B-28. After individual Soldiers complete table II certification, the team can begin training collective tasks and drills. Teams train and rehearse collective tasks and battle drills based on their assigned equipment and mission. 

B-29. Table III is the crawl phase of the overall team certification. During table III training, teams train according to the unit SOP and the training and evaluation outlines for the team collective tasks and battle 

**60** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Retransmission Team Training and Certification** 

drills. Teams progress from daytime iterations under static conditions to nighttime iterations under dynamic conditions. As the team masters the collective tasks, they conduct some training events in mission oriented protective posture 4. Table III consists of personnel and equipment training. The section sergeant conducts table III training and recommends when teams are ready to progress to table IV certification. 

##### **PERSONNEL TRAINING** 

B-30. Team training improves the capabilities of Soldiers in their respective team roles. Section sergeants ensure Soldiers complete training in the following. 

- Table II certification. 

- SOP. 

- Drills. 

- Training reinforcement. 

###### **Table II Certification** 

B-31. Table II individual certification is a prerequisite for table III team training. Leaders verify all team members have completed table II certification before beginning table III training. 

###### **Standard Operating Procedures** 

B-32. SOPs use past lessons and best practices to make the unit more effective. Team members review the SOP to ensure they are familiar with unit-specific requirements. 

###### **Drills** 

B-33. Teams build proficiency by conducting drills that focus on collective skills. Drills for retransmission teams consist of— 

- Warrior battle drills. 

- Retransmission team drills. 

- Command post battle drills. 

- Battle drills. 

###### **_Warrior Battle Drills_** 

B-34. Retransmission teams must be proficient in all common warrior battle drills. Battle drills are group skills designed to teach a unit to react and survive in common combat situations. Refer to STP 21-1-SMCT for a complete list of warrior battle drills with their supporting individual warrior tasks. Team chiefs must also be proficient in skill level 2 drills in STP 21-24-SMCT. Examples of warrior battle drills important to a retransmission team include— 

- React to contact. 

- Establish security at the halt. 

- Perform tactical combat casualty care. 

- React to ambush— 

   - Near. 

   - Far. 

###### **_Retransmission Team Drills_** 

B-35. Team drills are an effective mechanism to validate and sustain team training. Teams should train and conduct team drills according to the unit’s combined arms training strategy or SOP. The unit should implement team certification standards to support the unit’s mission and incorporate these standards into their operational planning. 

###### **_Command Post Battle Drills_** 

B-36. Command post battle drills ensure all team members understand a technique or a specific set of procedures. Examples of command post battle drills for a retransmission team include— 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**61** 

**Appendix B** 

- Site defense. 

- Emergency destruction. 

- React to jamming. 

###### **_Battle Drills_** 

B-37. Battle drills are collective actions performed without the application of a deliberate decision-making process. A battle drill is initiated on a cue, such as an enemy action or a leader’s command. A battle drill provides a reflexive response to a given stimulus. Battle drills require minimal leader orders to accomplish. Synchronization and reaction speed are enhanced when battle drills are identified, and the required reactions are defined in the unit SOP and rehearsed during training. 

###### **Training Reinforcement** 

B-38. Training on the collective tasks reinforces individual task training. Reinforcement training of collective tasks should be performed to standard through continual training. 

##### **EQUIPMENT TRAINING** 

B-39. Section sergeants conduct equipment training on the team’s assigned equipment. Equipment training includes— 

- Team collective tasks. 

- Supply discipline. 

- Operator maintenance. 

- Load plan complete. 

##### **TEAM COLLECTIVE TASKS** 

B-40. A collective task requires participation from each team member acting in concert to accomplish the team’s assigned mission. Retransmission teams train to achieve proficiency in collective task 11-CW-7017. A complete training and evaluation outline is available on the Army Training Network Website. 

###### **Supply Discipline** 

B-41. Team leaders must adhere to supply discipline procedures and conduct training to Soldiers. Supply discipline training includes— 

- Proper use of equipment. 

- Safeguarding government property. 

- Inventory techniques. 

- Hand receipts procedures. 

###### **Operator Maintenance** 

B-42. Signal teams and operators should perform preventive maintenance checks and services on their assigned equipment. Based on the mission, teams and operators diagnose faults and replace parts and subcomponents from available spares. Leaders should ensure operator level preventive maintenance is conducted according to technical manual maintenance schedules. 

###### **Load Plan Complete** 

B-43. Vehicle load plans should be based on equipment that is needed first and readily accessible. Section sergeants inspect load plans to ensure teams carry what is necessary for the mission. The standardization of load plans allows the platoon leader and platoon sergeant to quickly check accountability of equipment and standards.

#### **TABLE IV – TEAM CERTIFICATION** 

B-44. Soldiers must complete table III training before conducting table IV team certification. During table IV team certification, a sergeant first class or above certifies team proficiency in personnel and equipment tasks. 

B-45. During table IV certification, teams are evaluated on the execution of collective tasks and drills. The certification begins with site occupation and consists of day and night iterations. During certification, the team must complete deployment readiness exercise level I, preventive maintenance checks and services, and submit DA Form 5988-E ( _Equipment Maintenance and Inspection Worksheet [EGA]_ ) on the assigned system. 

##### **PERSONNEL TASKS** 

B-46. Team certification improves the capabilities of Soldiers in their respective team roles. Section sergeants document Soldiers’ proficiency in— 

- Unit-specific requirements. 

- Day and night performance of tasks and drills. 

- Minimal distractions.

###### **Unit-Specific Requirements** 

B-47. Sections complete certification using training and evaluation outlines or unit SOP. A sergeant first class or above certifies teams. Examples of unit-specific training requirements include— 

- Unit-specified time standards. 

- Location. 

- Mission oriented protective posture conditions. 

- Operational environment. 

- Reporting requirements. 

###### **Day and Night Iterations** 

B-48. Teams perform tasks in day and night iterations according to unit-specific requirements for certification listed in table IV. Sergeants first class or above certify teams performing day and night iterations. 

###### **Minimal Distractions** 

B-49. Teams train and certify on tasks with minimal distractions. Leaders safeguard their organization’s approved training from external influences that may disrupt training. 

###### **Deployment Readiness Exercise** 

B-50. Unit commanders conduct deployment readiness exercises. Leaders ensure movement requests and equipment documentation is complete. Deployment readiness exercise level I evaluates a unit’s ability to— 

- Alert and assemble the unit. 

- Conduct Soldier readiness tasks. 

- Verify appropriate deployment certification. 

- Verify appointment orders. 

**_Note._** Refer to AR 525-93 and AR 600-8-101 for details on conducting deployment readiness exercises. 

##### **EQUIPMENT TASKS** 

B-51. Certifying leaders verify the team’s proficiency in equipment-related tasks. Equipment certification includes— 

- Team collective tasks.

- Maintenance and service verification. 

- Hand receipt verification.

###### **Team Collective Tasks** 

B-52. Certifying leaders verify retransmission teams are proficient in their team collective tasks and drills. Each task is evaluated based on the training and evaluation outline. Collective task proficiency is rated as— 

- Trained.

- Untrained. 

###### **Maintenance and Service Verification** 

B-53. Unit maintenance personnel verify all annotated faults on the DA-5988-E and order any parts required to resolve faults. Leaders monitor status of ordered parts by verifying entry into the Standard Army Maintenance System-Enhanced. 

###### **Hand Receipt Verification** 

B-54. During table IV certification, hand receipt holders verify accountability for assigned equipment. Certifying leaders annotate discrepancies and notify unit supply personnel of shortages. Unit supply personnel review all sub-hand receipts for accuracy before signing.

##### **SECTION III – BATTLE DRILLS** 

B-56. A battle drill ensures all participants understand a technique or a specific set of procedures. Throughout unit training and preparation for missions, retransmission teams rehearse battle drills. These rehearsals do not require a complete operation order to execute. Leaders place priority on drills for actions they anticipate occurring during the operation. Refer to FM 6-0 for more information about battle drills. See section II for abbreviated drill outlines for retransmission team battle drills. 

- B-57. To ensure survivability, retransmission teams should be proficient in the following squad battle drills: 

   - Battle Drill 1: React to Direct Fire Contact while Dismounted—Squad (07-SQD-D9501). 

   - Battle Drill 2: Break Contact—Squad (07-SQD-D9505). 

   - Battle Drill 3: React to Ambush (Near)—Squad (07-SQD-D9502). 

   - Battle Drill 4: React to Indirect Fire while Mounted. 

**_Note._** The following are abbreviated drill outlines. The full battle drills with task measures and evaluation standards are available on the Army Training Network Website. 

#### **SAFETY** 

B-58. In a training environment, leaders must perform a risk assessment before each training event in accordance with ATP 5-19. Leaders complete the current DD Form 2977 ( _Deliberate Risk Assessment Worksheet_ ) during the planning and completion of each task and sub-task by assessing the risk factors and the mission variables of METT-TC (I). 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**65** 

**Appendix B** 

**_Note._** Leaders must monitor personnel for potential heat injury during simulated chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear conditions while wearing individual mission-oriented protective posture equipment. Local policies and procedures must be followed if there is an increased heat category to avoid heat-related injury. Leaders consider the mission-oriented protective posture work-rest cycles and water replacement guidelines in accordance with current chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear doctrine. Refer to ATP 3-11.32 for more information on chemical, biological, radiological, or nuclear protection. 

##### **BATTLE DRILL 1: REACT TO DIRECT FIRE CONTACT WHILE DISMOUNTED—SQUAD (07SQD-D9501)** 

###### **Conditions** 

The squad is conducting operations in a live training environment independently or as part of a platoon or larger force. The squad is dismounted. While stationary or moving, the enemy engages the squad with direct fire. Some iterations of this task should be performed in mission oriented protective posture 4. 

###### **Cue** 

The drill begins when the enemy initiates direct fire contact. 

###### **Standards** 

The squad reacts to direct fire contact while dismounted according to ATP 3-21.8. The team in contact returns fire immediately and seeks cover. The team in contact locates the enemy and places well-aimed fire on known enemy positions. Leaders point out enemy positions and identify the types of weapons, such as small arms and light machine guns. The team not in contact assumes the nearest covered and concealed position. The squad leader reports the contact to the platoon leader. 

###### **Task Steps and Performance Measures (Asterisks Indicate Leader Performance Steps)** 

1. The team in contact immediately returns well-aimed fire on known enemy positions. 

2. The team not in contact assumes the nearest covered and concealed position (see figure B-2 on page 67). 

**66** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Retransmission Team Training and Certification** 



**Figure B-2. Covered and concealed position** 

- 3. Team leaders engage known enemy positions with well-aimed fire and report information to the squad leader. 

- 4. Team leaders control the fire of their teams by using standard fire commands (initial and supplemental) 

- containing the following information: 

   - a. Alert. 

b. Weapon or ammunition (optional). 

- c. Target description. 

- d. Direction. 

- e. Range. 

- f. Method. 

g. Control (optional). 

h. Execution. 

- i. Termination. 

5. Soldiers maintain visual or vocal contact with their team leader and the other Soldiers on their left or right (if applicable). 

6. Soldiers maintain contact with the team leader and indicate the location of the enemy positions. 

- *7. Leaders visually or vocally check the status of their personnel. 

- *8. Team leaders maintain visual contact with the squad leader. 

9. The squad leader moves up to the team in contact and links up with its leader (see figure B-3 on page 68). 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**67** 

**Appendix B** 



**Figure B-3. Squad leader links up with the team leader** 

* 10. The squad leader determines whether or not the squad can gain and maintain suppressive fires with the team already in contact (based on the volume and accuracy of enemy fires against the team in contact). 

* 11. The squad leader confirms the commander’s criteria to disengage and determines whether or not the squad must move out of the engagement area. 

- 12. The squad leader assesses the situation and identifies— 

a. The location of the enemy position and obstacles. 

b. The size of the enemy force engaging the team in contact. (The number of enemy automatic weapons, the presence of any vehicles, and the employment of indirect fires are indicators of enemy strength.) 

- c. Vulnerable flanks. 

d. Covered and concealed flanking routes to the enemy positions. 

* 13. The squad leader decides whether to conduct an assault, bypass (if authorized by the platoon leader), or break contact. 

- 14. The squad leader reports the situation to the platoon leader and begins to maneuver the squad. 

##### **Battle Drill 2** : **BREAK CONTACT—SQUAD (07-SQD-D9505) )** 

###### **Conditions** 

The squad is conducting operations in a live training environment independently or as part of a platoon or larger force. The squad is moving as part of a larger force, conducting a movement to contact or an attack. Following direct fire contact with the enemy, the squad leader decides to break contact. Some iterations of this task should be performed in mission oriented protective posture 4. 

###### **Cue** 

The drill begins when the squad receives direct fire contact from the enemy. 

**68** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Retransmission Team Training and Certification** 

###### **Standards** 

The squad breaks contact according to ATP 3-21.8. Using fire and movement, the squad continues to move until the enemy cannot observe or place fire on them. The squad leader reports the contact to the platoon leader. 

###### **Task Steps and Performance Measures (Asterisks Indicate Leader Performance Steps)** 

- 1. While receiving direct fire from the enemy or on orders, the squad leader directs the squad to break contact. 

* 2. The squad leader directs one team to suppress by fire to support the disengagement of the remainder of the squad. 

* 3. The squad leader orders a distance and direction, terrain feature, or last rally point of the movement of the team in contact. 

- 4. The squad leader employs direct fires to suppress enemy positions (see figure B-4). 



**Figure B-4. Employing direct and indirect fires to suppress enemy** 

5. The moving team moves to occupy the overwatch position, employing smoke M203 or M320, smoke grenades, indirect fires, and other options to screen movement. If necessary, employ fragmentation and concussion grenades to facilitate breaking contact. 

6. The base-of-fire team continues to suppress the enemy. 

7. The moving team occupies their overwatch position and engages enemy positions (see figure B-5 on page 70). 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**69** 

**Appendix B** 



**Figure B-5. Moving element occupies overwatch and engages enemy** 

8. The squad leader directs the base-of-fire team to move to its next covered and concealed position. Based on the terrain and the volume and accuracy of the enemy’s fire, the moving team may need to use fire and movement techniques (see figure B-6). 



**Figure B-6. Fire and movement technique** 

9. The squad continues to move away from the enemy until— 

**70** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Retransmission Team Training and Certification** 

a. It breaks contact (the squad must continue to suppress the enemy as it breaks contact). 

b. It passes through a higher-level support-by-fire position. 

c. Its teams are in the assigned positions to conduct the next mission. 

- 10. The squad leader moves the squad onto an azimuth or alternate route away from enemy forces. 

**_Note._** The squad leader should change the unit’s direction of movement once contact is broken. This reduces the ability of the enemy to place effective indirect fire on the squad. 

11. Teams and Soldiers that become divided stay together and move to the last designated rally point. 

* 12. Leaders account for Soldiers, report the situation to the platoon leader, reorganize as necessary, and continue the mission. 

##### **BATTLE DRILL 3: REACT TO AMBUSH (NEAR)—SQUAD (07-SQD-D9502) )** 

###### **Conditions** 

The squad is conducting operations in a live training environment independently or as part of a platoon or larger force. The squad is moving tactically either dismounted or mounted. The enemy initiates contact with direct fire within hand grenade range from covered and concealed positions. All or part of the squad is receiving accurate enemy direct fire. Indirect fire is available. Some iterations of this task should be performed in mission oriented protective posture 4. 

###### **Cue** 

This drill begins when the enemy initiates ambush within hand grenade range. 

###### **Standards** 

The squad reacts to an ambush according to ATP 3-21.8. 

###### **Dismounted** 

Soldiers in the kill zone immediately return fire on known or suspected enemy positions and assault through the kill zone. Soldiers not in the kill zone locate and place well-aimed suppressive fire on the enemy. The squad assaults through the kill zone and destroys the enemy. 

###### **Mounted** 

Vehicle gunners immediately return fire on known or suspected enemy positions as the squad continues to move out of the kill zone. Soldiers on disabled vehicles in the kill zone dismount, occupy covered positions, and engage the enemy with accurate fire. Vehicle gunners and Soldiers outside the kill zone suppress the enemy. The squad assaults through the kill zone and destroys the enemy. The squad leader reports the contact to the platoon leader. 

###### **Task Steps and Performance Measures (Asterisks Indicate Leader Performance Steps)** 

1. The squad is moving dismounted (see figure B-7 on page 72), receives direct fire from the enemy, and takes the following actions. 

a. Soldiers in the kill zone execute one of the following two actions: 

(1) Return fire immediately. If cover is not available, immediately assault through the kill zone without order or signal. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**71** 

**Appendix B** 



**Figure B-7. Squad receives enemy fire** 

(2) Return fire immediately. If cover is available, without order or signal, occupy the nearest covered position and throw obscurant smoke grenades (see figure B-8). 



**Figure B-8. Squad immediately returns fire** 

a. The Soldiers in the kill zone assault through the ambush using fire and movement. 

b. The Soldiers not in the kill zone identify the enemy location, place well-aimed suppressive fire on the enemy’s position, and shift fire as Soldiers assault the objective. 

c. The Soldiers assault through and destroy the enemy position (see figure B-9 on page 73). 

**72** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Retransmission Team Training and Certification** 



**Figure B-9. Squad assaults through enemy position** 

d. The squad leader reports the contact to the platoon leader. 

2. The squad is moving mounted, receives direct fire from the enemy, and takes the following actions: 

a. The vehicle gunners in the kill zone immediately return fire and deploy vehicle obscurant smoke, while moving out of the kill zone. 

b. Soldiers in disabled vehicles in the kill zone immediately obscure themselves from the enemy with smoke, dismount if possible, seek covered positions, and return fire. 

c. The vehicle gunners and Soldiers outside of the kill zone identify the enemy positions, place well-aimed suppressive fire on the enemy, and shift fire as Soldiers assault the objective. 

* d. The squad leader calls for and adjusts indirect fire and requests close air support according to the mission variables of METT-TC (I). 

e. The Soldiers in the kill zone assault through the ambush and destroy the enemy. 

- f. The squad leader reports the contact to the platoon leader. 

##### **BATTLE DRILL 4: REACT TO INDIRECT FIRE WHILE DISMOUNTED—SQUAD (07-03-D9504)** 

###### **Conditions** 

The squad is conducting operations in a live training environment independently or as part of a platoon or larger force. The squad is dismounted. While stationary or moving, a member of the squad alerts INCOMING, or a round impacts nearby. Some iterations of this task should be performed in mission oriented protective posture 4. 

###### **Cue** 

The drill begins when any squad member alerts INCOMING, or a round impacts nearby. 

###### **Standards** 

The squad reacts to indirect fire while dismounted according to ATP 3-21.8. Soldiers immediately seek the best available cover. The squad moves out of the area to the designated rally point after the impacts. The squad leader reports the contact to the platoon leader.

**Figure B-10. Soldiers reacting to indirect fire** 

- *3. The squad leader orders the squad to move to a rally point by giving a direction and distance. 

4. Soldiers move rapidly in the given direction and distance to the designated rally point and reestablish security after the impacts.

##### **Appendix C** 

### **Combatant Commander Communications Team** 

This appendix discusses combatant commander communications team personnel supporting the combatant commander and theater army commander. It describes the team’s role and responsibilities to provide secure, reliable communications support. 

#### **MISSION** 

C-1. The combatant commander communications team provides communications support using secure frequency modulation radios and narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT to combatant commanders and theater army commanders. 

#### **ROLES AND RESPONSIBILITIES** 

C-2. The combatant commander communications team is comprised of— 

- Network management technician. 

- Information systems chief. 

- Senior combatant commander information technology noncommissioned officer. 

- Combatant commander information technology noncommissioned officer. 

- Senior combatant commander communications noncommissioned officer. 

- Combatant commander communications noncommissioned officer. 

- Transmission systems operations noncommissioned officer. 

##### **NETWORK MANAGEMENT TECHNICIAN** 

- C-3. The network management technician’s responsibilities are— 

   - Supervises and manages the tactical networking environment and administers the local area network and radio system for the combatant commander or theater army commander’s location. 

   - Plans, administers, manages, maintains, operates, integrates, secures, and troubleshoots commercial off-the-shelf communications and automated information systems and radio systems. 

   - Leads the team and manages the training of personnel on the installation, administration, management, maintenance, operation, integration, securing, and troubleshooting of commercial offthe-shelf, intranets, radio systems, and video teleconferencing systems. 

   - Performs system integration and administration. 

   - Implements the cybersecurity program to protect and defend information, computers, and networks from disruption, denial of service, degradation, or destruction. 

   - Develops policy recommendations and advises commanders and staffs on planning, installing, administering, managing, maintaining, operating, integrating, and securing commercial off-theshelf communications, radio systems, and video teleconferencing systems on Army, joint, combined, and multinational networks. 

##### **INFORMATION SYSTEMS CHIEF** 

- C-4. The information systems chief’s responsibilities are— 

   - Assumes the duties of the network management technician, when required. 

   - Serves as the principal information systems noncommissioned officer for the combatant commander communications team. 

   - Supervises, plans, coordinates, and directs the employment, operation, management, and unit-level maintenance of multi-functional and multi-user information processing systems in mobile and fixed facilities. 

   - Provides technical and tactical advice to the command and staff concerning all aspects of information processing system operations, maintenance, and logistical support.
