##### **Appendix D** 

### **Waveforms and Waveform Applications** 

This appendix discusses the waveforms and waveform applications used in tactical radio networks. Tactical radios provide the means to pass voice and data across the assigned area. 

#### **WAVEFORM OVERVIEW** 

D-1. A waveform is the representation of a signal that consists of the frequency, modulation type, message format, and transmission system. The term waveform refers to a known set of characteristics, for example, frequency bands, modulation techniques, message standards, and transmission systems. In tactical radio system usage, the term waveform describes the entire set of radio functions that occur from the user input to the RF output. 

D-2. Waveforms of the tactical networking environment process voice and essential data elements. Data elements within the tactical networking environment are centered on friendly forces, situational awareness, and transport capabilities to enable communications in disconnected, intermittent, and limited bandwidth environments. The spectrum and bandwidth availability are limited, and only critical functions operate within this portion of the tactical networking environment. 

###### **Wave Modulation** 

D-3. Frequency modulation and amplitude modulation (AM) transmitters produce RF carriers. The carrier is a wave of constant amplitude, frequency, and phase that modulates by changing its amplitude, frequency, or phase. Modulation is superimposing information, voice, or coded signals on a carrier. Figure D-1 shows different wave shapes. 



**Figure D-1. Wave shapes** 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**79** 

**Appendix D** 

###### **Amplitude Modulation** 

D-4. AM is the variation of the RF power output of a transmitter at an audio rate. The RF energy increases and decreases in power, according to the audio frequencies superimposed on the carrier signal. 

D-5. When the RF carrier modulates by complex tones, such as speech, each separate frequency component of the modulating signal produces its own upper and lower sideband frequencies. The upper sideband contains the sum of the RF and audio frequency signals, and the lower sideband includes the difference between the RF and frequency signals. Figure D-2 shows an AM carrier. 



**Figure D-2. Amplitude modulation carrier** 

D-6. The space occupied by a carrier and its associated sidebands in the RF spectrum is a channel. In AM, the width of the channel (bandwidth) is equal to twice the highest modulating frequency. For example, if a band of frequencies modulates a 5,000 kHz (5 MHz) carrier ranging from 0.2–5 kHz (200–5,000 cycles), the upper sideband extends from 5000.2–5005 kHz. The lower sideband extends from 4,999.8–4,995 kHz. The bandwidth is the difference between 5,005 Hz–4,995 kHz, a total of 10 kHz. 

D-7. Radio transmitters operating in the medium and HF bands generally use AM. The information of an AM signal exists solely in the sidebands. 

###### **Single-Sideband** 

D-8. Each sideband contains all the information needed for communication. Both sidebands occur within the modulation circuitry of the single-sideband radio set; the transmitter suppresses the carrier on one sideband before any signal transmits. Figure D-3 on page 81 shows a single-sideband carrier. 

**80** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Waveforms and Waveform Applications** 



**Figure D-3. Single-sideband carrier** 

D-9. The upper sideband is higher in frequency than the carrier, and the lower sideband is lower in frequency. Planners use either an upper or a lower sideband for communication and make appropriate adjustment to the transmitter and the receiver to the same sideband. 

D-10. Most Army single-sideband equipment operates in the upper sideband mode. The transmission of only one sideband leaves open that portion of the RF spectrum normally occupied by the other sideband of an AM signal. This allows more RF carriers within a given frequency range. Single-sideband transmission is used in applications to— 

- Obtain greater reliability. 

- Limit size and weight of equipment. 

- Increase effective output without increasing antenna voltage. 

- Operate a large number of radio sets without heterodyne interference (whistles and squeals) from RF carriers. 

- Operate over longer ranges without signal degradation due to selective fading. 

#### **WAVEFORM APPLICATIONS** 

D-11. The Network Operations Management System provides a mechanism to plan, configure, and load waveform applications onto the radio platforms. The waveform application is composed of all current and future software-defined waveform applications that provide voice and data across the transport layer of the network. Waveform applications are peer-to-peer programs that facilitate the exchange of application data across the spectrum of radio networks. Knowledge of waveform applications and characteristics is essential for network planners as they develop their network architecture to meet their commander’s command and control requirements. 

###### **Mobile User Objective System Waveform** 

D-12. The MUOS is a UHF SATCOM waveform that enables satellite network connectivity and communications services for mounted and dismounted units to support beyond line of sight communications capability at brigade and below. 

D-13. MUOS is primarily intended for mobile user’s aerial, maritime, and ground vehicles, and dismounted soldiers. MUOS extends voice and data communications beyond line of sight. The MUOS waveform operates 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**81** 

**Appendix D** 

as a global cellular service provider to support Soldiers with modern cell phone-like capabilities. It adopts a commercial third generation wideband code division multiple access mobile phone architecture for use in the military UHF SATCOM frequency spectrum using four geosynchronous satellites in place of cell towers covering the globe. By operating in the UHF band, 300–320 MHz, to transmit user to base and 360–380 MHz to receive base to user, a lower frequency band is required than that which is used by conventional terrestrial cellular networks. MUOS provides Soldiers with the tactical ability to communicate in disadvantaged environments, such as heavily forested regions where the forest canopy would unacceptably attenuate higher frequency signals. The radio uses the MUOS waveform when operating on one of four 5 MHz channels on one of sixteen beams on one of four geosynchronous satellites. 

D-14. MUOS waveform provides military point-to-point and communication users with precedence-based and pre-emptive access to voice and data up to 64 kilobits per second that span the globe. Each MUOS terminal has a 10-digit Defense Switched Network assigned phone number when provisioned. Users in the field establish connections on demand within seconds, freeing resources for other users. The multichannel, multifunction tactical radios that support the MUOS waveform technology are the AN/PRC-158 and AN/PRC-162. 

###### **Integrated Waveform** 

D-15. SATCOM integrated waveform is an enhanced method of multiplexing radio networks on the same channel. An integrated waveform uses carrier phase modulation to allow more access using the same channel. Carrier phase modulation implemented in radios provides higher data throughput on the UHF dedicated satellite channels in line of sight mode. An integrated waveform is an augmentation to time division multiple access services; its flexible waveform structure allows communication access tailoring based upon operational requirements. Narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT-capable radios that support integrated waveform technology are the AN/PSC-5C/D, AN/PRC-117F/G, AN/PRC-148, AN/PRC-158, AN/PRC-162, and AN/PRC-163. Integrated waveforms— 

- Support data rates up to 19.2 kilobits per second. 

- Provide up to 14 networks operating at 2400 bits per second each. 

- Support narrowband voice operations with mixed excitation linear prediction. 

**_Note._** The Army plans to divest its inventory of the AN/PSC family of radios(replacing them with the AN/PRC-158 or AN/PRC-162) and the AN/PRC-117F/G radios by fiscal year 28 to support cryptographic modernization. 

###### **SATURN** 

D-16. The Second Generation Anti-jam Tactical Ultrahigh Frequency Radio for North Atlantic Treaty Organization (SATURN) is an Air Force sponsored waveform with fast frequency hopping capabilities that replaces the legacy Havequick waveforms. SATURN is the current solution to counter threats from precision signal intercepting capabilities and jamming efforts. SATURN is designed for military airborne operations as defined by STANAG 4372. Although there are similarities between the legacy Havequick waveform, significant differences exists between the performance of the digital waveform and the Havequick analog waveform. SATURN’s frequency hopping capabilities outperform Havequick waveforms allowing users to define up to 40 frequencies as opposed to 16 frequencies allowed by Havequick. Additional capabilities are— 

- Supports improved data rates, synchronous and asynchronous. 

- Allows automatic transmission time updates and synchronization. 

- Provides cease reception and cease transmission capabilities (receiver and transmitter break-in). 

- Supports contested environments including air-to-ground, air-to-air, command and control voice line-of-sight communications. 

**82** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

##### **Appendix E** 

### **High Frequency Radio Networks** 

This appendix describes HF radio networks and capabilities. It also discusses establishing HF networks at echelons corps through brigade. This appendix concludes with factors to consider for successful HF network operations. 

#### **OVERVIEW** 

E-1. Units use HF networks when unit dispersal exceeds the planning range for VHF radios. Commanders routinely establish a HF command network as a secondary means of controlling operations. The primary advantage of HF radio is its capability to provide long-range, over-the-horizon communication. Additionally, HF radio networks— 

- Provide enhanced voice and data connectivity using ALE. 

- Extend beyond line of sight and point-to-point communications. 

- Provide lower probability of transmission interception. 

- Provide secure and reliable data and voice encryption. 

- Allow synchronous collaboration using tactical chat applications. 

- Maximize secure voice availability with mixed excitation linear prediction. 

- Supplement VHF systems. 

E-2. HF radios’ longer range overcomes terrain limitations and is useful where line of sight cannot be achieved. HF is the only tactical communications asset that may achieve long-range communications independent of terrestrial or satellite relays. HF communications may be either voice or secure data. 

#### **ESTABLISHING HIGH FREQUENCY RADIO NETWORK COMMUNICATION** 

E-3. HF radio networks provide commanders reliable, secure, fast, and flexible communications exchange with higher and subordinate echelons. Units from battalion to theater establish HF radios and radio networks to enable communication during offensive or defensive operations. 

E-4. All echelons employ HF networks to support the command network. Each echelon typically establishes sustainment networks and operations and intelligence command networks to support fires and reconnaissance operations. Radio stations are divided into nets according to the function that each station serves within the command. The station in charge of maintaining the net control station is usually a station in a superior unit communicating with stations in one or more subordinate units. 

##### **HIGH FREQUENCY RADIO NETWORK** 

E-5. HF communications networks are easily established, reconfigured, and maintained for Soldiers requiring long-distance, wide-area, fixed or on-the-move, ground, and ground-to-air communications. HF communications networks are established at large command post that lack the mobility required for tactical enabling tasks. The corps, division, and brigade employ HF radios when there are terrain and distance challenges that disrupt or degrade single-channel VHF communications. HF radio, terrestrial beyond line of sight systems require an understanding of antenna design and awareness of the capabilities needed to support requirements. When determining site locations, planners work with G-3 or S-3 to minimize adverse effects that terrain and structures have on a unit’s communications capabilities. 

###### **Corps** 

E-6. The G-6 coordinates with higher, adjacent, and subordinate echelons to ensure adequate network support. The corps G-6 and the corps signal, intelligence, and sustainment company provide HF radio support for the corps headquarters and the various companies in the corps headquarters. These systems are mostly 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**83** 

**Appendix E** 

user-owned and operated systems with the corps signal, intelligence, and sustainment company responsible for network control. 

###### **Division** 

E-7. The division G-6 and the division signal, intelligence, and sustainment company provide HF radio support for the division command posts. The division G-6 and the division signal, intelligence, and sustainment company provide HF radio support for the division headquarters and the various companies in the division headquarters. 

###### **Brigade Combat Team** 

E-8. The brigade signal company provides HF radio support to the brigade command posts. The brigade’s organic signal assets support continuous operations to sustain the brigade communications network. The AN/PRC-150 and AN/PRC-160 are both manpack radios that can be integrated into a mount for operation within a tactical operations center, or vehicle mounted. 

###### **_Brigade Combat Team S-6_** 

E-9. The brigade employs traditional HF networks to support command networks. Brigade combat teams typically establish command, administrative and logistics, and operations and intelligence networks to support fires and reconnaissance operations. A typical brigade has enough HF radios to establish command networks down to the company and lower levels as needed. The brigade S-6 collaborates with the higher headquarters J-6 or G-6, assigned or attached battalion S-6 staffs, and adjacent units to ensure effective communications throughout the brigade assigned area. 

###### **_Brigade Signal Company_** 

E-10. HF radios enable brigade elements to coordinate operations with higher, lower, and adjacent headquarters throughout their assigned area. The brigade signal company extends voice and data retransmission capabilities for its tactical radio network. 

E-11. The brigade signal company uses HF radios as a redundant means of communications when terrain or distance challenge line of sight radio capabilities. The brigade signal company supports both the brigade and subordinate battalion command posts. 

###### **_Maneuver Battalion S-6_** 

E-12. The maneuver battalion S-6 employs traditional HF networks to support command networks. The maneuver battalion S-6 typically establishes command, administrative and logistics, and operations and intelligence networks to support fires and reconnaissance operations. A typical maneuver battalion has enough HF radios to establish command networks down to the company as needed. The maneuver battalion collaborates with the higher headquarters and adjacent units to ensure effective communications throughout the assigned area. 

##### **DATA NETWORK** 

E-13. Combat aviation brigades and air cavalry units use HF data networks to provide long-range, beyond line of sight communications. Cavalry squadrons and troops use the low power HF data network for their command networks when the distance is not an issue. The same is true for divisional and regimental cavalry. Figure E-1 on page 85 shows a typical cavalry squadron HF data network. 

**84** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**High Frequency Radio Networks** 



**Figure E-1. Example of a cavalry squadron high frequency data network** 

##### **HIGH FREQUENCY RADIO CONSIDERATIONS** 

E-14. HF radio equipment is capable of both long- and short-range secure voice and data communications. Data communications capability is typically limited to rates of 2.4 kilobits per second. Data transmission requires modems specifically designed for operation in the HF range. 

E-15. Reliable HF communication requires a detailed analysis of the terrain and availability of appropriate frequencies. HF performance factors include— 

- The sun’s position in relation to transmitter and receiver stations. 

   - Lower frequencies are optimal during times of darkness. 

   - Higher frequencies are optimal during times of sunlight. 

- Weather seasons. 

   - Lower frequencies are optimal during winter weather conditions. 

   - Higher frequencies are optimal during summer weather conditions. 

- Solar activity. 

   - HF band propagation is optimal during the solar maximum (maximum sunspot activity period). 

   - HF band propagation is extremely limited during the solar minimum (minimum sunspot activity period). 

- Local electromagnetic interference from man-made equipment, which can greatly reduce the available frequencies in the HF band. 

E-16. Successful data communications over the HF range depends on several factors including equipment position, proper equipment grounding, and types of antennas used. Other considerations are the tactical employment of the radio equipment, path assessment and analysis, and frequency planning and assignment. When commercial data terminal equipment is used, signal teams and operators employing HF radios need to be aware of radio interference and potential shock hazards that can easily affect unprotected data terminal equipment. Whenever possible, HF radios should be remoted from data terminal equipment. 

E-17. The primary limiting factors when using HF radios are frequency allocation and management and bandwidth availability. Frequency allocation and management is concerned with frequency, time of day, time of year, and location. The ability to reflect HF radio waves off the ionosphere to a distant location is in a constant state of flux because of ionospheric activity. Therefore, planners consider environmental limitations associated with HF radios including characteristics of ground wave and skywave propagation. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**85** 

**Appendix E** 

E-18. The maximum ground wave range for most tactical HF communications is about 20–30 kilometers (12–22 miles) for manpack equipment and 80–100 kilometers (50–62 miles) for high-power vehicular and van equipment. The range may be decreased by heavy vegetation, mountainous terrain, or dry desert soil. Beyond this range, it is necessary to communicate by skywave. 

E-19. Skywave frequencies are a very important consideration, as those above a certain value will not bend back to earth but will transmit through the ionosphere into outer space. On the other hand, lower frequencies are noisier and become absorbed by the ionosphere. The reflective nature of the ionosphere will change when sunlight hits it each day. As a result, at least two frequencies are usually required during a 24-hour period: a lower nighttime frequency and a higher daytime frequency. 

E-20. Another consideration in using HF communications is how to compensate for skip zones, a phenomenon where no signal is received from a particular transmitter for a particular frequency. Skip zones are formed when the nearest point at which a skywave is received is beyond the furthest point at which a ground wave is received. Multipath interference occurs when both the skywave and the ground wave signals from the transmitter arrive at different times at the receiver. By using an antenna with a high radiation takeoff angle, HF radio waves can be bounced off the ionosphere and come back to earth closer than they can with more commonly used antennas. This can cause the skip zone to disappear if the waves do not penetrate the ionosphere. 

E-21. The use of high radiation take-off angle or near-vertical incident skywave communications using a horizontally polarized antenna is useful for HF radio transmission since signals can be transmitted from moving vehicles. Near-vertical incident skywave communications require a horizontally polarized antenna and are done over frequencies from 2–12 MHz. The limit of the effective range of near-vertical incident skywave communications is usually about 300 miles. With proper application, near-vertical incident skywave communication provides reliable, continuous communications beyond the range of HF ground wave and VHF and UHF line of sight. 

E-22. The site selection for the antenna is another important consideration. Usually, the tactical situation determines the position of the antenna. The ideal setting is a clear, flat area with no trees, fences, power lines, or mountains. Situations often require constructing an antenna on an irregular or less than optimal site. This does not mean the antenna will not work. It means that the site affects the antenna’s pattern and function. 

###### **Antenna Types** 

E-23. HF antennas vary in size, shape, and capability. HF antennas are generally longer and require more clearance space to assemble and establish radio communications. Before selecting an antenna, operators should examine the available construction materials. For instance, horizontal dipoles require at least two supports to erect. A third (center) support is necessary for frequencies 5 MHz or lower. Operators should not construct a dipole antenna without the necessary support structure. If the support structures are unavailable, the operator should select another antenna type. Planners and operators examine the proposed antenna site to determine whether the antenna can fulfill the mission requirements. 

E-24. Radio waves in the 3–30 MHz frequency range can reflect and return to Earth by the ionosphere with predictable regularity. To optimize the probability of a successful skywave communications link, operators select the frequency, and take-off angle most appropriate for the time of day transmission is to take place. Various large conducting objects, and the Earth’s surface, modify an antenna’s radiation pattern. Sometimes nearby scattering objects may modify the antenna’s pattern favorable by concentrating more power toward the receiving antenna. 

E-25. Selecting the right antenna for a HF radio circuit is very important. When selecting a HF antenna, planners consider the type of propagation desired. Ground wave propagation requires low take-off angle and vertically polarized antennas. The whip antenna included with most radio sets provides good omnidirectional ground wave radiation. Selecting an antenna for skywave propagation is very complex. Listed below are considerations for selecting antennas for skywave propagation— 

- **Determining the circuit range distance required for the take-off angle.** A circuit distance of 966 kilometers (600 miles) requires a take-off angle of approximately 25 degrees during the day and 40 degrees at night. 

**86**

- **Selecting a high gain antenna.** The optimal range for propagation predictions is 25–40 degrees. This prediction indicates the take-off angles required. 

- **Determining the required coverage.** A point-to-point circuit uses either a bidirectional or directional antenna. The receiving station location dictates the antenna choice. See table E-1 for the take-off angles versus distance. 

**_Note._** A radio circuit with mobile vehicle stations (or several stations located away from the transmitter) requires an omnidirectional antenna.

E-26. The antenna’s take-off angle is the angle above the horizon that an antenna radiates the largest amount of energy. The take-off angle of an HF communications antenna can determine whether a circuit is thriving or not. HF skywave antennas are designed to support exact take-off angles, depending on the circuit distance. Operators use high take-off angles for short-range communications and use low take-off angles for longrange communications. Figure E-2 on page 88 depicts an example of an antenna take-off angle.

E-27. Figure E-3 shows the vertical antenna pattern for the 32-foot vertical whip antenna. The numbers along the outer ring 90, 80, and 70 degrees represent the angle above the Earth—90 degrees would be straight up, and 0 degrees would be parallel to the ground. Along the bottom of the pattern are numbers from -10 (at the center) to + 15 at the edges. These numbers represent decibels over a theoretical isotropic radiator. 



**Figure E-3. 32-foot vertical whip antenna pattern** 

E-28. Using the example at figure E-3, signal leaders can determine the antenna gain at a particular frequency and take-off angle and locate the desired take-off angle on the plot. The plotted center line represents the pattern of the desired frequency. The gain appears at the bottom scale. To find the gain of a 32-foot vertical 

**88** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025**

whip at 9 MHz with a 20-degree take-off angle, locate 20 degrees along the outer scale and follow this line to the 9 MHz pattern line. The intersecting line represents the 9 MHz pattern line. The resulting antenna gain in the example appears where the intersecting vertical and horizontal lines meet at the bottom scale or slightly less than 2.5 decibels isotropic. The gain of the 32-foot vertical whip at 9 MHz and 20 degrees is 2 decibels above effective isotropic power.

E-30. The near-vertical incidence skywave antenna, (AS-2259/GR) is a lightweight sloping dipole omnidirectional antenna. The AS-2259/GR antenna provides high-angle radiation, near-vertical incidence to permit short-range skywave propagation to support HF communications up to 800 kilometers (500 miles). The frequency range of the antenna is 2.0–12.0 MHz. The maximum RF power capacity is 1,000 watts. Figure E-4 on page 90 shows a near-vertical incidence skywave antenna, AS-2259/GR.

###### **_Harris RF-1944, Inverted Vee High Frequency Antenna_** 

E-31. The Harris RF-1944 Inverted Vee HF antenna is a lightweight, broadband dipole commercial off-theshelf antenna that provides radiation patterns to support HF skywave communications. The antenna’s primary use is for its ALE and frequency hopping capabilities. Other capabilities of the RF-1944 antenna are— 

- Horizontal polarization. 

- 0–804.7 kilometer (0–500 miles) radiation pattern. This range is ideal for HF skywave communications. 

- 1.6–30 MHz frequency range. 

- Up to 20 watts power and 50 ohm input impedance. 

- Gain— 

   - 16 decibels at 2 MHz. 

   - 2 decibels at 30 MHz. 

- Weight— 

   - 9 pounds. 

E-32. The RF-1944 antenna does not include a mast. The primary components are a balanced to unbalanced transformer, two radiation elements with integral terminating loads, two ground stakes, a coaxial cable, a weighted throwing line, and a carrying bag. The RF-1944 antenna’s light-weight design makes it easily transportable in mobile, tactical environments. 

**_Note._** A balanced-to-unbalanced transformer is a device used to couple a balanced device or line to an unbalanced device or line. 

###### **_V Antenna_** 

E-33. The V antenna (see figure E-5 on page 91) is a medium- to long-range, broadband skywave antenna. The V antenna provides point-to-point communications to ranges exceeding 4,000 kilometers (2,500 miles). The V antenna consists of two wires arranged to form a V, with its ends at the apex (where the legs come 

**90** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**High Frequency Radio Networks** 

together) attached to a transmission line. Radiation lobes off each wire combine to increase gain in the direction of an imaginary line bisecting the apex angle; the pattern is bidirectional. Adding 300 ohm terminating resistors to the far end of each leg makes the pattern unidirectional (in the direction away from the apex angle). 



**Figure E-5. V antenna** 

E-34. The angle between the legs varies with the length of the legs to achieve maximum performance. Table E-3 shows the angle and the length of the legs. When using the V antenna with more than one frequency or wavelength, operators should use an apex angle that is midway between the extreme angles as determined in table E-3.

|2|70|

###### **_Vertical Half-Rhombic Antenna and Long-Wire Antennas_** 

E-35. The vertical half-rhombic antenna and the long-wire antenna are two field expedient directional antennas. The long wire antenna directive pattern radiates in the horizontal and vertical planes. The vertical half-rhombic antenna radiates to the front and back of the sloping wires if resistors are not used. These antennas consist of a single wire, preferably two or more wavelengths long, supported on poles at the height of 3–7 meters (10–20 feet) above the ground. The antennas operate satisfactorily as low as 1 meter (3.2 feet) above the ground. Resistors on the far end of the wire connect to ground through a non-inductive resistor of 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**91** 

**Appendix E** 

500–600 ohms. To avoid resistor burnout from the output power of the transmitter, leaders ensure personnel use a resistor rated at least one-half the wattage output of the transmitter, good ground with multiple ground rods, or a counterpoise at both ends of the antenna. Figure E-6 is an example of a vertical half-rhombic antenna. Figure E-7 is an example of the long wire antenna. 



**Figure E-6. Vertical half-rhombic antenna** 



**Figure E-7. Long-wire antenna** 

###### **_Sloping V Antenna_** 

E-36. The sloping V antenna is a field expedient directional antenna with legs sloping downward from its apex. To ensure that the antenna radiates in only one direction, add non-inductive terminating resistors from the end of each leg, not at the apex to ground. The resistors should be approximately 500 ohms and have a power rating at least one-half that of the output power of the transmitter used. Without the resistors, the antenna radiates bidirectionally, to the front and back. A balanced transmission line feeds the antenna. Figure E-8 on page 93 shows an example of a sloping V antenna.

###### **_Inverted L Antenna_** 

E-37. The inverted L is a combination antenna made up of vertical and horizontal wire sections. It provides radiation when using no resistors from the vertical element for ground wave propagation, and high-angle radiation from the horizontal element for short-range skywave propagation, 0–400 kilometers (0–250 miles). The classic inverted L has a quarter-wave vertical section and a half-wave horizontal section. 

E-38. Table E-4 shows the frequency and length of the horizontal element. Reasonable performance for short-range skywave circuits is obtained with a vertical height of 11–12 meters (35–40 feet). Figure E-9 on page 94 shows an example inverted L antenna.

###### **_Near Vertical Incidence Skywave Propagation_** 

E-39. HF radio and the near-vertical incidence skywave mode play an essential role in establishing reliable communications. HF communications typically deploy first and are frequently the only means of communicating with fast-moving or widely separated units. Communications planners, commanders, and operators must be familiar with near-vertical incidence skywave techniques and their applications and shortcomings. 

E-40. Near-vertical incidence skywave propagation uses antennas with high-angle radiation and low operating frequencies. Just as the proper selection of antenna can increase the reliability of a long-range circuit, the same holds true for short-range communications. 

E-41. Near-vertical incidence skywave propagation uses a high take-off angle of 60–90 degrees to radiate the signal upward. The signal reflects from the ionosphere and returns to Earth in a circular pattern around the transmitter. In near-vertical incidence skywave propagation, there is an area between the maximum ground wave distance and the shortest skywave distances where no communication is possible. Communications are continuous out to several hundred kilometers from the transmitter. 

E-42. The nearly vertical angle of radiation also requires lower frequencies. Generally, near-vertical incidence skywave propagation uses frequencies up to 8 MHz. The steep up-and-down propagation of the signal gives the radio operator the ability to communicate over nearby ridgelines, mountains, and dense vegetation. A valley location may give the radio operator terrain shielding from hostile intercept or protect the circuit from ground wave and long wave electromagnetic interference. Antennas used for near-vertical incidence skywave propagation require high take-off angle radiation with very little ground wave radiation. Figure E-10 on page 95 is an example of near-vertical incidence skywave propagation. 

**94** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**High Frequency Radio Networks** 



**Figure E-10. Near-vertical incidence skywave propagation** 

E-43. Using the HF antenna selection matrix in table E-2 on page 89, the AS-2259/GR and the half-wave dipole are the only antennas listed that meet the requirements of near-vertical incidence skywave propagation. While the inverted V and inverted L have high angle radiation, they can also have strong ground wave radiation that could interfere with the close-in, near-vertical incidence skywave communications. 

- E-44. Disadvantages of near-vertical incidence skywave propagation include— 

   - Increased potential for electromagnetic interference—where near-vertical incidence skywave and ground wave signals are present, the ground wave can cause destructive electromagnetic interference. 

   - Ground wave radiation suppression—proper antenna selection suppresses ground wave radiation, minimizes electromagnetic interference effects, and maximizes the amount of energy output while in the near-vertical incidence skywave mode. 

- E-45. Advantages of using near-vertical incidence skywave propagation in a tactical environment include— 

   - Skip zone-free omnidirectional communication. 

   - Terrain does not affect the loss of signal. This gives a more consistent received signal level over the operational range instead of one that varies widely with distance. 

   - Operators can operate from protected, dug-in positions. Commanders do not have to control the high ground for HF communication. 

   - Orientation, such as doublets and inverted antennas are not as critical. 

- E-46. Advantages of using near-vertical incidence skywave when operating against an electromagnetic warfare-capable threat include— 

   - **Lower probability of geolocation.** Near-vertical incidence skywave energy from above at very steep angles reduces direction finding from nearby but makes beyond ground wave range more difficult. Successful near vertical incidence skywave is achieved with very low power HF sets. 

   - **Communications are more laborious to jam.** Ground wave jammers are subject to path loss. Terrain features attenuate a ground wave jammer without degrading the desired communication path. This forces the jammer to move very close to the target or transmit power. Either tactic makes jamming more difficult. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**95** 

**Appendix E** 

###### **_Field Expedient Antennas_** 

E-47. Field expedient antennas are generally used for HF communication. Construction material is what differentiates field expedient antennas used for HF and VHF or UHF field applications. Users may have to construct an emergency antenna from common items. Plastic and glass, including plastic spoons, buttons, bottlenecks, and plastic bags, can be effective insulators. Wood and rope also act as insulators, although they are less effective than plastic and glass. 

###### **_High Frequency Antennas Usable at Very High Frequency and Ultrahigh Frequency_** 

E-48. Simple vertical half-wave dipole (doublet) and quarter wave monopole antennas are effective for omnidirectional transmission and reception over short-range distances. Longer distances typically use rhombic antennas made of wire similar in design to HF versions to provide an advantage at frequencies as high as 1 GHz. 

E-49. The dipole (doublet) antenna, also considered a center fed antenna, is a half-wave antenna consisting of two-quarter wavelength sections on each side of the center. Figure E-11 is an example of the improvised dipole (doublet) antenna used with VHF and UHF radios. 



**Figure E-11. Half-wave dipole (doublet) antenna** 

E-50. A transmission line conducts electrical energy from one point to another and transfers the output of a transmitter to an antenna. It is possible to connect an antenna directly to a transmitter. In a vehicular installation, for example, the antenna is mounted outside, and the transmitter is inside the vehicle. 

E-51. Operators may use pieces of wood to support center-fed half-wave frequency modulation antennas. These antennas rotate to any position to obtain the best performance. If the antenna is vertical, the transmission line should be positioned horizontally from the antenna or a distance equal to at least one-half of the antenna’s length, before dropping down to the radio set. Figure E-12 on page 97 shows examples of horizontal (A) and vertical (B) center-fed half-wave antennas. 

**96** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**High Frequency Radio Networks** 



**Figure E-12. Center-fed half-wave antenna** 

E-52. Figure E-13 on page 98 is an example of an improvised vertical half-wave antenna. VHF and UHF radios primarily use this technique. An improvised vertical half-wave antenna is effective in heavily wooded areas to increase the range of portable radios. The top guy wire can connect to a limb or pass over the limb and connect to the tree trunk or a stake.

##### **HIGH FREQUENCY ANTENNA PLACEMENT** 

E-53. During operations, units are not always able to locate their fixed and mobile radio assets in ideal positions. Planners should comply with as many of the following criteria as possible to gain the best technical advantages for the tactical situation— 

- Use of ground radials and ground stakes under a vertical antenna to improve antenna efficiency and lower take-off angles for better ground wave communications. 

- Placement of vertical antennas on higher ground, if possible, to enhance ground wave communications. 

- Avoiding placement of vertical antennas behind metal fencing that shield ground wave signals. 

- Avoiding placement of a vertical antenna close to vertical conducting structures such as masts, light poles, trees, or metal buildings. Antennas need to be at least one wavelength from conducting structures to eliminate major pattern distortions and antenna impedance changes by induced current and reflections. 

- Location of antennas as far from the radio as practical to reduce electromagnetic interference effects between the radio the radio and antenna. 

E-54. Units that operate in less than ideal positions may need to communicate using simplex operation. Simplex operation, or one-way-reversible, consists of sending and receiving radio signals on one antenna. HF radios normally use simplex operation. Units use two antennas during duplex operation: one for transmitting and one for receiving. The transmitter generates a radio signal; a transmission line delivers the signal from the transmitter to the antenna. 

**98** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**High Frequency Radio Networks** 

E-55. The transmitting antenna sends the radio signal into the atmosphere toward the receiving antenna, which intercepts the signal and sends it through a transmission line to the receiver. The receiver processes the radio signal and is supported by an AN/UXC-10 facsimile. Figure E-14 is an example of a typical transmitter and receiver connection. 



**Figure E-14. Transmitter and receiver connection example** 

E-56. The function of all antennas depends on whether it is transmitting or receiving. A transmitting antenna transforms the output RF signal in the form of an alternating electrical current produced by a radio transmitter and RF output power. The signal is then transmitted into an electromagnetic field. The receiving antenna reverses this process; it transforms the electromagnetic field into electrical energy delivered to a radio receiver. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53**

##### **Appendix F** 

### **Julian Day, Synchronization Time, and Time Conversion** 

Accurate time is essential for SINCGARS radios to operate in the frequency-hopping mode. This appendix addresses the Julian day, synchronization time, and ZULU time. A time variance greater than plus or minus four seconds can disrupt the SINCGAR’s frequency-hopping capability. 

#### **JULIAN DAY** 

F-1. The SINCGARS uses a special two-digit form of the Julian day as part of the synchronization time. The two-digit Julian day begins with 01 on 1 January and continues through 00, repeating as necessary to cover the entire year. 

F-2. Table F-1 on page 102 shows the two-digit Julian day calendar for a regular year. Table F-2 on page 103 shows the two-digit Julian day calendar for a leap year. The regular year, two-digit Julian calendar terminates on 65 (or 66 for the leap year). Net control station personnel and operators change the Julian day to 01 on 1 January each year. Resetting the Julian day can be accomplished by— 

- Sending an electronic remote refill. 

- Reloading the time from a Defense Advanced Global Positioning System Receiver. 

- Changing the date in the radio using the radio transceiver (RT) keypad.

#### **SYNCHRONIZATION TIME** 

F-3. The SINCGARS radio uses seven internal clocks to maintain proper synchronization time: There is a base clock and a clock for each of the six frequency hopping channels. Manual and cue settings display the base clock time. 

F-4. The Defense Advanced Global Positioning System Receiver provides units a ready source of precision timing. Opening networks on GPS time and updating net control station RT synchronization time to GPS time daily keeps all networks within the plus or minus four-second window required for frequency hopping communications.

#### **ZULU TIME** 

F-5. ZULU time remains in synchronization with the United States Naval Observatory master atomic clock. An alternative is to use the time from a Defense Advanced Global Positioning System Receiver for tracking at least one satellite. The net control station should update and verify net time daily or according to the unit SOP.
