##### **Appendix G** 

### **Communications in Adverse Conditions** 

The appendix addresses radio operations in cold weather, jungle, urban, desert, mountain areas, and nuclear areas. Communications in austere, unusual environments require special consideration. 

#### **OPERATIONS IN COLD WEATHER** 

G-1. Single-channel radio equipment has certain capabilities and limitations that require careful consideration when operating in extremely cold weather. Despite significant limitations, the radio is still the normal means of communication in such environments. 

G-2. An important attribute of a radio in cold weather operations is versatility. Vehicle-mounted radios can move to almost any location where there is a command headquarters. Smaller, manpack radios move to any area accessible by aircraft or on foot. 

G-3. Electromagnetic interference by ionospheric disturbances limits radio communications in extremely cold areas. These disturbances, known as ionospheric storms, have a degrading effect on skywave propagation. Ionospheric storms and aurora activity can cause complete failure of radio communications. Static may block out some frequencies completely for extended periods during storm activity. Fading caused by changes in the density and height of the ionosphere can also occur and may last from several minutes to several weeks. These occurrences are difficult to predict; but when they do occur, the spectrum manager needs to assign alternate frequencies. 

##### **TECHNIQUES FOR BETTER COMMUNICATIONS IN COLD WEATHER** 

G-4. Radio operators implement preventive measures to reduce the adverse effects of extremely cold weather conditions when possible. To avoid cold weather-related malfunctions, communications equipment should be stored inside vehicles. Storing equipment inside vehicles provides added protection from severe weather conditions and potentially avoids some of the grounding and antenna installation problems experienced when operating equipment in cold weather environments. 

G-5. It is difficult to establish a good electrical ground in extremely cold areas because of permafrost and deep snow. The conductivity of frozen ground is often too low to provide good ground wave propagation. To improve ground wave operation, radio operators use a counterpoise to offset the degrading effects of poor electrical ground conductivity. To prevent snow from covering an antenna, operators should install a counterpoise; and if possible, install the antenna high enough above the ground to prevent snow from covering the antenna. 

G-6. In general, antenna installation in arctic conditions presents no serious difficulties. Antenna installation may take longer due to adverse working conditions. Tips for installing antennas in extremely cold areas include— 

- Handle the mast sections and antenna cables carefully since they become brittle in very low temperatures. 

- Construct antennas overhead to prevent damage from heavy snow and frost. 

- Use nylon rope if available, in preference to cotton or hemp, because nylon ropes do not readily absorb moisture, and are less likely to freeze and break. 

- Use extra wires, supports, and anchor stakes to strengthen antennas and to withstand heavy ice and wind loading. 

G-7. Some radios, generally older generation radios adjusted to a frequency in a relatively warm place, may drift off frequency when exposed to extreme cold. Low battery voltage can also cause frequency drift. When possible, operators should allow a radio to warm up for several minutes before placing it into operation. Extreme cold tends to lower the output voltage of a dry battery; operators can use body heat to elevate the battery temperature. This technique minimizes frequency drift. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**107** 

**Appendix G** 

G-8. Northern regions sometime experience flakes or pellets of electrically charged snow. When these particles strike the antenna, the resulting electrical discharge causes a high-pitched static roar that can affect frequencies. To overcome this static, operators cover antenna elements with polystyrene tape or shellac. 

##### **MAINTENANCE IN COLD WEATHER** 

G-9. The maintenance of radio equipment in extreme cold presents many difficulties. Snow potentially freezes dials and knobs and can get into the wiring, causing shorts and ground faults. Radio sets should be protected from blowing snow. Cold weather can damage or reduce cord and cable flexibility. Operators avoid additional damage by handling these items with care. Operators should winterize communications equipment and power units according to technical manual instructions. Batteries, power units, and microphones require routine maintenance for cold weather operations. Table G-1 is a list of winterization techniques. Refer to the equipment technical manual for additional instructions on winterization. 

**Table G-1. Winterization techniques** 

|**_Item_**|**_Technique_**|
|---|---|
|Power Units|Operators safeguard generators as much as possible<br>from the weather by using shields and protective<br>covering.|
|Batteries|Maintain moderate temperatures to extend battery life<br>duringcold temperatures.|
|Shock Damage|Check shock mounts frequently and change them as<br>required to avoid damage to radio equipment.|
|Winterization|Replace normal lubricants with the recommended arctic<br>lubricants and add a light coat of silicon compound on<br>antenna mast connections to prevent connective wiring<br>from freezingtogether and becominghard to dismantle.|
|Microphones|Prevent moisture from entering the perforated cover<br>plate of the microphone using standard covers, if<br>available, or improvise a suitable cover using rubber or<br>cellophane membranes,or rayon or nylon cloths.|
|Condensation<br>Management and<br>Corrosion Prevention|Wrap the equipment in a blanket or parka to ensure that<br>it warms gradually to reduce sweating before moving<br>cold equipment into a heated area. Equipment must be<br>thoroughly dry before taking it back into the cold air, or<br>the moisture freeze. Sweating occurs when cold<br>equipment suddenly encounters warm air and moisture<br>condenses on the equipmentparts.|
|Vehicle-Mounted<br>Radios|Observe proper starting procedures. Allow time for the<br>vehicle’s heater to warm the radio sufficiently to ensure<br>that any frost collected on or inside the radio has a<br>chance to thaw or stabilize (approximately 15 minutes).<br>Charge the batteries before increasing engine<br>revolutions per minute. Turn off radios to avoid an<br>excessive power surge. A vehicle operated at a low idle<br>with radios, heater, and lights on may drain the<br>batteries.|



#### **OPERATIONS IN JUNGLE AREAS** 

G-10. Limitations on radio communications in jungle areas stem from the climate and the density of jungle growth. The hot and humid climate increases the maintenance demands. Thick jungle growth acts as a vertically polarized absorbing screen for RF energy that reduces transmission range. Therefore, increased emphasis on maintenance and antenna site selection is inherently important when operating in jungle areas. 

G-11. Radio communications in jungle areas requires careful planning. Dense jungle growth, heavy rains, and hilly terrain significantly reduce the range of radio transmission. Trees and underbrush absorb VHF and UHF radio energy. In addition to the ordinary free space loss between transmitting and receiving antennas, a 

**108** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Communications in Adverse Conditions** 

radio wave passing through a forest undergoes additional loss. This extra loss increases rapidly as the transmission frequency increases. Near the ground, with antenna heights of less than 3 meters (9.8 feet), vertical polarization is preferred. If possible, operators should elevate the receiving and transmitting antennas as much as 10–20 meters (32.8–65.6 feet). Horizontal polarization is preferable to vertical polarization. Considerable reduction in total path loss results if either the transmitting or receiving antenna placement occurs above tree level. 

G-12. Planners should carefully consider the capabilities and limitations of tactical radios in a jungle environment. Their mobility and various configurations are primary advantages of tactical radios in jungle areas. 

##### **TECHNIQUES FOR BETTER COMMUNICATIONS IN JUNGLE AREAS** 

G-13. Antenna site selection is the main challenge while establishing radio communications in jungle areas. Techniques to improve communication in the jungle include— 

- Placing antennas as high as possible in clearings on the edge farthest from the distant station. 

- Keeping antenna cables and connectors off the ground to lessen the effects of moisture, fungus, and insects. This also applies to power and telephone cables. 

- Using complete antenna systems, such as broadband and dipoles. Complete antenna systems are more effective than fractional wavelength whip antennas. 

- Clearing vegetation from antenna sites. If an antenna touches any foliage, especially wet foliage, grounding of the signal occurs. 

- Using horizontally polarized antennas in preference to vertically polarized antennas because vegetation, particularly when wet, acts as a vertically polarized screen and absorbs much of a vertically polarized signal. 

##### **MAINTENANCE IN JUNGLE AREAS** 

G-14. Maintenance of radio sets in tropical climates is more difficult than in temperate climates due to excessive moisture and fungus. High relative humidity causes condensation to form on the equipment and encourages the growth of fungus. Radio operators and maintenance personnel should check equipment technical manuals for any special maintenance requirements. Techniques for improving maintenance in jungle areas include— 

- Keeping the equipment as dry as possible and in lighted areas to retard fungal growth. 

- Keeping air vents clear of obstructions to ensure that air can properly circulate for cooling and drying of the equipment. 

- Using moisture and fungus proofing paint, tape, or silicone grease to protect equipment after repairs, or to protect damaged or scratched painted surfaces. 

#### **OPERATIONS IN URBAN AREAS** 

G-15. Radio communications in urbanized terrain poses special challenges. During urban combat operations, the communications situation is considerably different from the situation faced by civil government or cell phone users. Military factors include— 

- Restriction of operation to the frequency range of common military radios (2–512 MHz). 

- Limits on the output power of military radio equipment. 

- Limited number of available repeater assets, if any. 

- Limited access to good repeater locations due to threat actions. 

- Need to communicate to both outside street locations and inside structures. 

- Lack of standard compact antenna systems useful for urban combat. 

- Severe restrictions on the movements of system users. 

- Lack of manpower required to cover multiple signal sites can easily exceed available resources. 

- Problems with buildings or other man-made structures blocking transmission paths. 

- Poor electrical conductivity of paved surfaces. 

- Commercial power line interference. 

- Distorted radio wave propagation in built-up areas. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**109** 

**Appendix G** 

G-16. VHF radios have their effectiveness reduced in urban areas. The operating frequencies and power output of these radios require line of sight between antennas. Line of sight at street level is not always possible in built-up areas. AM HF radios are less affected by the line of sight challenges because operating frequencies are lower, and power output is greater. HF radios are generally not organic to the small units that conduct urban clearing operations. Retransmission, if available, may overcome this limitation. 

##### **TECHNIQUES FOR BETTER COMMUNICATIONS IN URBAN AREAS** 

G-17. Depending on requirements, units should use organic retransmission teams to extend radio range in urban environments. When available, aerial retransmission platforms can provide effective radio communications in urban environments. Units should hide or blend radio antennas in with the surroundings to ensure they will not be landmarks for the threat to locate. Water towers, commercial antennas, and steeples can conceal military antennas. Signal elements executing operations in challenging urban environments leverage the barrage relay TSM technology of the ITN that offers reliable, redundant communications paths, and improves transmission completion rates. The technology simplifies network configurations and is particularly beneficial for on-the-move operations. 

G-18. Signal personnel install wire while friendly forces are in static positions and use existing telephone poles to raise wire lines above the streets. They use ditches, culverts, and tunnels to conceal the wire below the streets. If proper precautions are not taken, tracked and wheeled vehicles could sever lines and disrupt communications. 

G-19. Messengers provide security and flexibility. Planners should carefully select messenger routes to avoid pockets of enemy resistance. Units should vary routes and time schedules to avoid establishing a predictable pattern. Pyrotechnics, smoke, and marker panels are also excellent means for communicating, but they require coordination and understanding by air and ground forces. The noise of combat in built-up areas makes it difficult to use sound signals effectively. 

G-20. The possible seizure or retention of established communications facilities must be included in planning. Units must make every effort to prevent damage or destruction of these facilities. The local telephone system is already in place and tailored to the city or town. Forces can use local telephone systems to provide immediate access to wire communications with overhead and buried cable. This procedure helps overcome the challenges encountered with radios and provides a cable system less susceptible to combat damage. 

G-21. Local media such as newspapers, radio stations, and television stations, provide communication with the local populace after the level of combat declines. Intact police or taxi communications facilities are also possible radio systems, tailored to the city, with retransmission facilities already in place. 

G-22. Signal elements and leaders can park radio-equipped vehicles inside of buildings or garages for cover and concealment. It is important to dismount radio equipment, when possible, and install it inside the basements of buildings, if available. To increase noise absorption and provide concealment, operators should position generators against buildings or under sheds with adequate ventilation. 

G-23. Another important consideration for urban combat is raw power. The more power used, the more path loss and the deeper the signals penetrate structures. Common tactical VHF manpack radios like SINCGARS have a maximum output power of four watts. The AN/PRC-150 HF radio has a maximum output power of 20 watts. This means there is 7 decibels more signal power to overcome losses caused by the path, path obstructions, inefficient antennas, and other signal attenuating factors. The extra power improves the radio capabilities. The following are examples of power relationships— 

- 4 watts = 36 decibels above one milliwatt (dBm). 

- 20 watts = 43 dBm. 

- 50 watts = 47 dBm. 

- 150 watts = 52 dBm. 

- 400 watts = 56 dBm. 

G-24. The decibel is a logarithmic unit used to describe a ratio. The ratio may be power, voltage, intensity, or several other factors; but in this case, it is transmitter power in watts. Radio operators can calculate the difference of two power levels by taking a logarithm of log ten of their power ratio. For example, if the ratio of power is two—meaning one radio transmitter is double the power of the other—the difference is 3 decibels. Every 3 decibels gained through a more efficient antenna system or reduced transmission line loss is the 

**110** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Communications in Adverse Conditions** 

equivalent of doubling the transmitter power. The important point is that adjustments to antenna systems or operational frequencies to make an antenna more efficient can often produce far more effective signal power than simply increasing the transmitter power. More effective power may be necessary to overcome path loss for the near-vertical incidence skywave and ground wave systems. If the radio is already transmitting at maximum power, adjustments to the antenna systems or frequencies are the only way to compensate for path loss and improve signal penetration in an urban environment. 

**_Note._** It is important to remember that in many situations the power required to operate a radio may not need to be the maximum power. Operators should use only the power necessary to establish communication. 

G-25. Communication between two radio stations requires that the combined transmitter power, transmitting antenna gain, receiving antenna gain, and receiver performance overcome the path loss between stations. A base station typically uses a higher performance receiver and a more efficient antenna, and can compensate for a low power outstation radio, like the manpack radios and inefficient antennas used by forward troops. Communication between low-power outstations is much more difficult and may even require retransmission through a more efficient base station. 

G-26. In urban operations, small HF radios like the AN/PRC-150, are extremely portable, but are antennaand power-challenged based on location. Obtaining a high degree of portable near-vertical incidence skywave effect when needed can be done by physically reorienting standard vertical manpack or vehicle (whip) antennas to the horizontal plane. Direct (surface wave) signals are simpler to generate and use inside structures produced from the same antenna by leaving the antenna vertical. 

G-27. Because of their longer wavelengths, lower frequency HF signals (2–30 MHz) naturally penetrate urban structures deeper than signals on higher (shorter wavelength) frequencies. How deep the penetration depends on exact frequency, signal power level, antenna efficiency, and the makeup of the urban structures in the path. 

G-28. It is not uncommon for a small ground penetrating radar transmitter operating in the HF range to penetrate over 30.4 meters (100 feet) into common kinds of earth while the same power radar on a higher frequency penetrates much less. If the radio operator is using a common VHF military radio operating at 30 MHz (lowest frequency for SINCGARS) replaces it with a HF radio, such as the AN/PRC-150 operating at 5 MHz; the path loss drops by 20 decibels because of the way that longer wavelength lower frequency signals propagate. In this case, lowering the frequency is the equivalent to increasing the power of the transmitter by a factor of almost seven. 

##### **MAINTENANCE IN URBAN AREAS** 

G-29. The physical aspects of urban infrastructure and the need to protect communications systems presents unique operational challenges in urban areas. Units should be aware of potential for restrictions to communication ranges with non-vehicular systems due to line of sight limitations, urban structures, dispersion, and threat jamming. To alleviate electromagnetic challenges associated with urban environments, operators can implement redundant communications such as HF radios, satellite, and retransmission. To prevent personnel loss, equipment damage and degraded communications, operators should accomplish maintenance and equipment recovery expeditiously to secure themselves and their equipment. Techniques for improving maintenance in urban areas include— 

- Using camouflage and concealment to prevent signal detection, reduces communications footprint, and matches the local urban terrain. 

   - Park radio-equipped vehicles inside buildings for cover and concealment. 

   - Dismount radio equipment and install it inside buildings (in basement, if available). 

   - Place generators against buildings or under sheds to decrease noise and provide concealment. 

- Keeping equipment clear of obstructions that impede direct signal transmission to reduce interference. 

- Provide adequate ventilation to prevent heat buildup and subsequent failure of generators and communications equipment. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**111** 

**Appendix G** 

- Training and use of redundant platforms and systems to include retransmission and relay sites and equipment. 

- Remote radios by positioning antennas in upper stories or in adjacent buildings based on remote capabilities. 

#### **OPERATIONS IN DESERT AREAS** 

G-30. Radios are usually the primary means of communication in the desert. Radios employed in desert climate and terrain provide the highly mobile means of communications required for widely dispersed forces. However, desert terrain provides poor electrical ground, and counterpoises are needed to improve operation. 

G-31. Dust and extreme heat are two of the biggest problems involved in desert operations. Temperatures may vary from 58 degrees Celsius (136 degrees Fahrenheit) in the summer to -46 degrees Celsius (-51 degrees Fahrenheit) in the winter. Extreme heat can damage generators, wire, and communications equipment and increase personnel risk to serious heat-related illnesses or injuries. 

G-32. Dust and sand particles damage equipment. Some combat net radios have ventilating ports and channels that may clog with dust. Operators should check and clean these ports regularly to prevent overheating. 

G-33. In a desert environment, operators should ground equipment by burying ground plates in the sand and pouring salt solutions on the ground plates. Daily preventive maintenance checks and services should include cleaning generators and air filters to prevent equipment damage. 

##### **TECHNIQUES FOR BETTER COMMUNICATIONS IN DESERT AREAS** 

G-34. Operators should cut or adjust antennas to the length of the operating frequency and emplace antennas in the required direction. Approximate azimuth produced by guesswork is not sufficient. A whip antenna relies on the capacitor effect between itself and the ground for efficient propagation. The electrical ground may be very poor and degrade the antenna performance by as much as one-third if the surface soil lacks moisture, which is normally the case in the desert. 

G-35. If a ground-mounted antenna is not fitted with a counterpoise, operators should dampen the ground around it using any fluid available. See appendix H for more information on a counterpoise. Vehicle mounted antennas are more efficient if the main mass structure of the vehicle is forward of the antenna and is oriented toward the distant station. 

G-36. Operators should keep radios cool and clean in accordance with technical manual procedures. Units should operate radios in a shaded or ventilated area, and at low power whenever possible. A flat sheet of wood, cardboard, or a vehicle’s canvas top over the top of the radio can create man-made shade to keep radios cool. Leaving a space between the wood, cardboard, and the radio, helps to cool the radio by causing air to circulate in the shaded area between the radio and the wood. Carefully covering hot radios with a damp cloth without blocking ventilation outlets allows moisture evaporation from the cloth to cool the radio. 

G-37. Desert terrain can cause excessive signal attenuation, making planning ranges shorter. Desert operations require dispersion; yet the environment is likely to degrade the transmission range of radios, particularly secure VHF frequency modulation radios. The greatest degradation is most likely to occur during the hottest part of the day, from approximately 1200–1700. 

G-38. If, during the hottest time of day, radio stations begin to lose contact, units should exercise their PACE plan. Techniques may include— 

- Using retransmission capabilities—including airborne retransmission. Aerial retransmission aircraft should remain at least 4,000 meters behind the line of contact. Planners create retransmission plans to best support the scheme of maneuver. 

- Deploying an unemployed vehicle with a radio as a retransmission station. 

- Using alternative radio links, such as VHF multichannel telephones, a higher power level, or HF single-sideband voice. 

G-39. After dark, a rapid temperature drop creates heat inversion. This inversion can disrupt radio communication until the atmosphere stabilizes. 

**112** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Communications in Adverse Conditions** 

G-40. Wired connections are impractical during high-tempo military operations. Wire may be of some value in static defensive situations. When possible, installers and operators should bury wire and cables deep in the soft sand to prevent heat damage to cable insulation and prevent vehicle or foot traffic damage. 

G-41. Operators should protect computers and removable storage media from dust and sand. Covering computers and disks with plastic bags may reduce damage. Covering computers and radios for an extended period may cause condensation inside these components, resulting in equipment damage or data loss. Canned compressed air is a useful tool to clean keyboards and other components. 

##### **MAINTENANCE IN DESERT AREAS** 

G-42. Wind-blown sand and grit can damage electrical wire insulation over time. Tape can help protect cables from sand damage. Sand may also work into parts of items such as spaghetti cord plugs, either preventing electrical contact or making it impossible to join the plugs together. A soft brush, such as an old toothbrush, can clean cords and plugs before connection. 

G-43. Static electricity is prevalent in the desert. Many factors cause static electricity. One factor is windblown dust particles. Extremely low humidity contributes to static discharges between charged particles. Poor grounding practices exacerbate the problem. Operators should tape all sharp edges and tips of antennas to reduce wind-caused static discharges and the accompanying noise. When operating from a fixed position, operators should install a proper earth ground. Since static-caused noise diminishes with an increase in frequency, operators should use the highest frequency that is available and authorized. Refer to TC 6-02.6 for more information about grounding techniques. 

#### **OPERATIONS IN MOUNTAINOUS TERRAIN** 

G-44. Radio operation in mountainous terrain shares some of the same challenges as in cold weather areas. Mobility is difficult in mountainous terrain, and it can be difficult to find a level area to establish a communications site. 

##### **TECHNIQUES FOR BETTER COMMUNICATIONS IN MOUNTAINOUS TERRAIN** 

G-45. Line of sight paths are more difficult to establish in mountainous terrain. Operating in mountainous terrain may require additional retransmission assets. Signal plans should include the use of retransmission to improve communications range. Antenna positioning is critical in mountainous terrain; moving an antenna even a short distance can drastically affect reception. 

##### **MAINTENANCE IN MOUNTAINOUS TERRAIN** 

G-46. Generators and communications equipment need level ground for proper operation. It may be difficult to drive ground rods and guy wire stakes into rocky ground. An alternate grounding method may be necessary. Rocky soil provides poor ground conductivity. Adding salt solutions improves electrical flow. Refer to TC 6-02.6 for more information about grounding equipment in rocky soil. 

#### **OPERATIONS IN NUCLEAR ENVIRONMENTS** 

G-47. A nuclear environment adversely affects sensitive radio equipment and components. Operators should take measures to protect signal equipment and ensure equipment survivability and availability for future use. The ionization of the atmosphere by a nuclear explosion will degrade communications because of static and the disruption of the ionosphere. 

##### **TECHNIQUES FOR BETTER COMMUNICATION IN A NUCLEAR ENVIRONMENT** 

G-48. Electronic equipment is very susceptible to electromagnetic pulse. _Electromagnetic pulse_ is a strong burst of electromagnetic radiation caused by a nuclear explosion, energy weapon, or by natural phenomenon, that may couple with electrical or electronic systems to produce damaging current and voltage surges (JP 3- 85). Electromagnetic pulse radiates an intense electromagnetic field. This electromagnetic energy affects unprotected communications equipment, causing disruption and destruction of delicate circuitry and components. The residual ionized cloud also disrupts radio transmissions. The electromagnetic field from an 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**113** 

**Appendix G** 

electromagnetic pulse affects communications over all frequency ranges, but lower frequencies recover from the effects much sooner than the highest frequencies. 

G-49. A high-altitude electromagnetic pulse can disable electronic systems as far as 6,000 kilometers (3,720 miles) from the site of the detonation. An electromagnetic pulse can also cause severe disruption and sometimes damage when other weapon effects are absent. The enemy may explode a high-yield nuclear weapon above the atmosphere to knock out narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT without causing any other significant damage. The range of an electromagnetic pulse shortens if the weapon detonates at a lower altitude. 

G-50. A typical high-altitude electromagnetic pulse could be up to 1,000 times more intense than a radar beam. A radar beam has enough power to cause biological damage to the human body, including blindness or sterilization. The electromagnetic pulse is broad spectrum and extends from extremely low frequency into the extremely ultrahigh frequency band. A likely electromagnetic pulse effect would be temporary disruption of communications. This can occur even without permanent damage. This delay could give an enemy enough of an advantage to change the outcome of a battle. 

G-51. Narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT radios incorporate built-in features and techniques to counter electromagnetic pulse effects. Shielding can further reduce the effects of electromagnetic pulse; Whenever possible, operators should connect cable shields to grounding systems and keep cable length as short as possible. Units assess the probable direction of the nuclear blast, consider available shielding, then select unit and equipment locations to reduce exposure. 

G-52. Electronic equipment not in use should remain disconnected and stored in sealed shelters or other shielded enclosures to protect against electromagnetic pulse. This reduces the likelihood of the simultaneous damage to all equipment and provides a source of backup components to reinstall affected systems. If communications equipment cannot be disconnected, the unit should consider acquiring additional communications equipment to provide operational spares in the event of a nuclear hazard. 

##### **MAINTENANCE IN A NUCLEAR ENVIRONMENT** 

G-53. Installers and operators should shield and properly ground wire and cable and keep cable length as short as possible. Operators should connect cable shields to the grounding system, whenever possible. Effective grounding can reduce electromagnetic pulse effects. Most tactical radios with fully-enclosed metal cases provide adequate protection if external connectors are disconnected. Placing radios in vehicles, vans, and underground shelters may enhance protection. 

**114** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

##### **Appendix H** 

### **Antenna Selection and Techniques** 

The antenna is the key component in establishing reliable communications to support operations. This chapter addresses antenna techniques, concepts, terms, ground effects, antenna length, types of antennas, and examples of antenna field repairs. 

##### **SECTION I – OVERVIEW** 

H-1. All radios, whether transmitting or receiving, require an antenna. The antenna is a key component in establishing reliable communications. The selection, proper installation, and positioning of antennas around command posts is critical. The G-6 or S-6 staff ensures that antennas are more than twice the distance of their height from infrastructure such as power lines. For safety, units avoid placing antennas near sleep areas, tents, and vehicle parking areas. Selection factors to consider include the operational environment, means of employment, planning range, and asset availability. 

H-2. When selecting an antenna, the operator should consider— 

- Radio type. 

- Required coverage. 

- Take-off angle versus distance. 

- Frequency range or band. 

H-3. Identifying the specific conditions in the operational environment provides additional information necessary for determining what type of communications system and antennas to install. Tactical radio networks require antennas that are— 

- Reliable. 

- Robust. 

- Capable of interconnection between networks of the same type. 

- Capable of interconnection between networks that are dissimilar. 

H-4. Antennas can be a significant hazard. Planners and installers must assess and manage antenna assembly, emplacement, electrical discharge, and physical hazards to maintain safe conditions. Radio planners should assess the following when establishing a tactical radio network: 

- Antenna location. 

- Antenna selection. 

- Environment and terrain conditions. 

- Mode of transmission. 

- Threat situation. 

- Frequency band. 

- Antenna masking. 

- Electromagnetic warfare system deconfliction. 

- Signals intelligence system deconfliction. 

#### **ANTENNA FUNDAMENTALS** 

H-5. Antennas transmit and receive electromagnetic waves, referred to as radio waves. An understanding of antenna concepts and terms is critical to selecting the right antenna. Planners must have a fundamental knowledge of propagation, transmission, and waveform properties to develop reliable communications systems that meet the commander’s requirements. 

##### **FORMING A RADIO WAVE** 

H-6. Alternating current flows through a conductor. If the length of the conductor is short in comparison to the wavelength, the electric and magnetic fields generally die out within one or two wavelengths. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**115** 

**Appendix H** 

Lengthening the conductor increases the intensity of the field. Thus, an ever-increasing amount of energy escapes into space. 

##### **RADIATION** 

H-7. The alternating flow of electrons impressed on the bottom end of a wire creates electromagnetic energy. A wire connected to a transmitter and properly grounded oscillates electrically, causing the wave to convert the transmitter power into a radio wave. The electrons travel upward on the wire to the top, where they have no place to go, and bounce back toward the lower end. As the electrons reach the lower end in phase, they are in step with the radio energy applied by the transmitter. 

H-8. The energy of their motion is strongly reinforced as the electrons travel upward along the wire. This regenerative process sustains the oscillation. The wire is resonant at the frequency at which the source of energy is alternating. The energy stored at any location along the wire is equal to the product of the voltage and the current at that point. The electric current reaches its maximum near the bottom end of the wire. 

##### **RADIATION FIELDS** 

H-9. RF power delivered to an antenna creates two fields: an induction field, which associates with the stored energy; and a radiation field oriented at a right angle. At the antenna, the intensities of these fields are large and are proportional to the RF power delivered to the antenna. 

H-10. The electric and magnetic field components radiating from an antenna form an electromagnetic field that transmits electromagnetic energy through free space. A radio wave is a moving electromagnetic field that has velocity in the direction of travel. Figure H-1 is a visual representation of the components of electromagnetic waves. 



**Figure H-1. Components of electromagnetic waves** 

##### **RADIATION PATTERNS** 

H-11. The radiation pattern is a graphical depiction of the relative field strength transmitted from or received by an antenna. The full- or solid-radiation pattern appears as a three-dimensional figure that looks somewhat like a doughnut with a transmitting antenna in the center. Figure H-2 on page 117 is an example of solid antenna radiation patterns. The top figure shows the radiation pattern of a quarter-wave vertical antenna; the middle figure shows the radiation pattern of a half-wave horizontal antenna located one-half wavelength above the ground; and the bottom figure shows the radiation pattern of a vertical half-rhombic antenna. 

**116** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 



**Figure H-2. Solid radiation patterns** 

##### **POLARIZATION** 

H-12. The direction of the lines of force making up the electric field determines the polarization of a radio wave. Polarization can be vertical, horizontal, or elliptical. When a single-wire antenna is used to extract energy from a passing radio wave, maximum pickup result when the antenna is oriented in the same direction as the electric field. Correct polarization ensures the antenna lies in the same direction as the electric field component. 

H-13. Horizontal or vertical polarization is satisfactory for VHF or UHF signals. The signal maintains the original polarization produced at the transmitting antenna maintains as the wave travels to the receiving antenna. 

###### **Vertical Polarization** 

H-14. In a vertically polarized wave, the lines of electric force are at right angles to the surface of the Earth. Figure H-3 on page 118 illustrates a vertically polarized wave. Operators should use a vertical antenna for the efficient reception of vertically polarized waves. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**117** 

**Appendix H** 



**Figure H-3. Vertically polarized wave** 

H-15. Vertical polarization is necessary for ground wave transmission at medium and low frequencies. Vertical lines of force are perpendicular to the ground, and the radio wave can travel a considerable distance along the ground with minimal signal loss. 

H-16. Vertical polarization provides a stronger received signal at frequencies up to approximately 50 MHz when antenna heights are limited to 3.05 meters (10 feet) over land, as in a vehicular installation. 

H-17. Reflections from aircraft flying over the transmission path have a lesser effect on vertically polarized radiation. This factor is advantageous in areas with heavy aircraft traffic. 

H-18. Using vertical polarization results in a lesser production and reception of electromagnetic interference from the strong VHF and UHF transmissions used in television and commercial radio broadcasts. This factor is important when locating an antenna in an urban area that has television or radio broadcast stations. 

###### **Horizontal Polarization** 

H-19. In a horizontally polarized wave, the lines of electric force are parallel to the surface of the Earth. It is essential to use a horizontal antenna to receive horizontally polarized waves. Figure H-4 on page 119 is an example of a horizontal polarized wave. 

**118** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 



**Figure H-4. Horizontally polarized wave** 

###### **_Skywave Transmission_** 

H-20. With skywave transmission at higher frequencies, it makes little difference whether horizontal or vertical polarization is used. The skywave, after reflection by the ionosphere, arrives at the receiving antenna elliptically polarized. Operators should mount the transmitting and receiving antennas either horizontally or vertically. Horizontal antennas are preferred since they radiate at high angles and have inherently directional properties. A simple horizontal half-wave antenna is bidirectional. This characteristic is useful when minimizing electromagnetic interference from specific directions and masking signals from the threat. 

###### **_Advantages_** 

H-21. Although either horizontally or vertically polarized antennas can be used for HF communications, horizontally polarized antennas are preferred because of certain advantages. One advantage is that vertically polarized interference signals, such as those produced by automobile ignition systems and electrical appliances, are minimized by horizontal polarization. In addition, less absorption of radiated energy by buildings or wiring occurs when these antennas are used. Another advantage is that support structures for these antennas are of a more convenient size than those for vertically polarized antennas. 

H-22. Horizontal antennas are less likely to pick up man-made interference. Horizontally polarized waves, especially at frequencies above 100 MHz, suffer lower losses when antennas are located near dense forests. Small changes in antenna location do not cause significant variations in the field intensity of horizontally polarized waves. 

###### **Elliptical Polarization** 

H-23. With elliptical polarization, the electromagnetic field rotates as the radio waves travel through space. Satellites and SATCOM ground terminals use a form of elliptical polarization called circular polarization. Circular polarization describes a wave where the plane of polarization rotates through 360 degrees as it progresses forward. Depending on the phase relationship between the electrical and magnetic fields, this causes rotation either in one direction or the other. Figure H-5 on page 120 is an example of circular 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**119** 

**Appendix H** 

polarization. The rotation can be clockwise (right-hand circular polarization) or counterclockwise (left hand circular polarization). Circular polarization occurs when equal magnitudes of vertically and horizontally polarized waves combine with a phase difference of 90 degrees. 



**Figure H-5. Circular polarization** 

##### **DIRECTIONALITY** 

H-24. Vertical transmitting antennas radiate equally in horizontal directions; vertical receiving antennas accept radio signals equally from all horizontal directions. Stations operating on the same or nearby frequencies may interfere with the desired signal, making reception difficult or impossible. To improve the reception of the desired signal, operators use directional antennas. 

H-25. Horizontal half-wave antennas accept radio signals from all directions. The most robust reception occurs from a direction perpendicular to the antenna, while the weakest reception occurs from the direction of the ends of the antenna. When eliminating or reducing interfering signals, it is beneficial to change the antenna installation to ensure that the end of the antenna points directly at the interfering station. 

##### **RESONANCE** 

H-26. Resonance occurs when an object vibrates (or resonates) at its natural frequency. Resonance can also be achieved by either physically matching the length of the antenna to the wavelength or electrically matching the length of the antenna to the wavelength. 

H-27. In a resonant antenna, all radio signals feed to the antenna that radiates. When different frequencies are produced, the sound is louder at its resonant frequency than at all other frequencies. A non-resonant antenna effectively radiates a broad range of frequencies with less efficiency. When using a resonant antenna for a radio circuit, it is important to build a separate antenna for each frequency used. 

##### **RECEPTION** 

H-28. Electrons in the path of radio waves influence other electrons in the path of radio waves. For example, as a high frequency wave enters the ionosphere, it reflects or refracts back to the Earth by the action of free electrons. When the radio wave encounters the wire or metallic conductors of the receiving antenna, the radio wave’s electric field causes the electrons in the antenna to oscillate in step with the wave. The movement of these electrons within the antenna generates a small alternating electrical current, which the radio receiver detects. 

H-29. When radio waves encounter electrons that are free to move under the influence of the wave’s electric field, the free electrons oscillate in sympathy with the wave. This generates the electric current, which then creates waves of its own called reflected or scattered waves. This process is electromagnetic scattering. Materials that are good electrical conductors reflect or scatter RF energy. Since a receiving antenna is a good 

**120** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 

conductor, it too scatters energy. Only a portion of the energy that contacts the antenna converts into received electrical power; the wire reradiates a sizable portion of the total power. 

H-30. If an antenna is in a congested urban environment or inside a building, many objects may scatter or reradiate the energy and interfere with reception. For example, the electrical wiring inside a building can strongly reradiate RF energy. If a receiving antenna is positioned near electrical wiring, the reflected energy can cancel the incoming RF signal. When this condition exists, it is beneficial to move the receiving antenna away from electrical wiring where the reflected and direct signals may reinforce rather than cancel each other. Refer to TC 9-64 for more information about radio wave propagation. 

##### **RECIPROCITY** 

H-31. Reciprocity refers to the various properties of an antenna that apply equally, regardless of whether the antenna is used for transmitting or receiving. For example, the more efficient an antenna is for transmitting, the more efficient it is for receiving the same frequency. The directive properties of a given antenna are the same whether used for transmission or reception. 

H-32. If using the transmitting antenna as a receiving antenna; the antenna receives best in the same directions in which it produces maximum radiation, at right angles to the axis of the antenna. The minimum signal is received from a transmitter located in line with the antenna wire. 

##### **IMPEDANCE** 

H-33. How much power an antenna radiates depends on the amount of current applied to it. Maximum power emits when there is maximum current flowing. Maximum current flows when the impedance is minimized or when the antenna is resonated so its impedance is pure resistance. The impedance of an antenna is equal to the ratio of the voltage to the current at the point on the antenna where the feed points connect. If the feed point is located at a maximum voltage point, the impedance is as much as 500–10,000 ohms. 

H-34. The input impedance of an antenna depends on the conductivity or impedance of the ground. For example, if the ground is a simple stake driven about 1 meter (3.2 feet) into soil of average conductivity, the impedance of the monopole may be 2–3 times the quoted values. Resistance occurs at a point on the antenna circuit where the current is high, and a significant amount of transmitter power dissipates as heat into the ground rather than radiated as intended. It is essential to use Earth, artificial ground, or a counterpoise connection when using a vertical whip or monopole antenna. 

##### **BANDWIDTH** 

H-35. Bandwidth describes how much data is transmitted over a connection in a specified amount of time. In the radio communications process, information changes from speech or writing to a low frequency signal used to modulate a much higher frequency radio signal. Natural laws govern and limit signal transmission. The more words per minute, the higher the modulation frequency needed; which generates a wider bandwidth signal. To transmit and receive the necessary information, the antenna bandwidth must be equal to or wider than the signal bandwidth; otherwise, it limits the signal frequencies causing voice and data to be unintelligible. If the bandwidth is too wide the signal may become degraded due to additional voices and signal to noise ratio. 

H-36. Antenna bandwidth describes the range of frequencies over which the antenna can adequately radiate or receive energy. The bandwidth of an antenna reflects the frequency range over which it performs within certain specified limits with respect to impedance matching, gain, and radiation pattern characteristics. 

##### **ANTENNA DIRECTIVITY AND GAIN** 

H-37. The gain of an antenna depends on its design. Transmitting antennas are designed for high efficiency in radiating energy, and receiving antennas are designed for high efficiency in receiving RF energy. 

###### **Directional Antennas** 

H-38. On many radio circuits, transmission is desired between a single transmitter and one receiving station. Directional antennas radiate most of their RF energy in one direction. Directional receiving antennas increase the effective gain in the favored direction and reduce the reception of unwanted noise in signals from the 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**121** 

**Appendix H** 

other directions. Transmitting and receiving antennas should have small energy losses and should be as efficient as radiators and receptors. 

###### **Omnidirectional Antennas** 

H-39. When employed in forward combat areas, omnidirectional antennas transmit and receive signals equally in all directions. This characteristic provides a similarly strong signal to intended receivers and threat electromagnetic sensors. 

#### **GROUND ANTENNA THEORY** 

H-40. The ground is an excellent conductor for medium and low frequencies and acts as a large mirror for the radiated energy. As a result, the ground reflects a significant amount of energy that radiates downward from an antenna mounted over it. It is important that the ground have as high conductivity as possible. This reduces ground loss and provides the best possible reflecting surface for the downward-radiated energy from the antenna. Thus, a quarter-wave antenna erected vertically, with its lower end connected electrically to the ground, behaves like a half-wave antenna. Under these conditions, the vertical antenna quarter wavelength and the ground create the half wavelength. The ground portrays the quarter wavelength of radiated energy that reflects on completing the half wavelength. At higher frequencies, artificial grounds constructed of large metal surfaces are common and provide better wave propagation. Figure H-6 is an example of a grounded quarter-wave antenna. 



**Figure H-6. Grounded quarter-wave antenna** 

##### **TYPES OF GROUND** 

H-41. Operators should establish the ground connection to introduce the least resistance to ground. At higher frequencies, artificial grounds constructed of large metal surfaces are standard. 

H-42. Ground connections take many forms, depending on the type of installation and acceptable signal loss. In many simple field installations, one or more metal rods driven into the soil make an adequate ground connection. Operators connect ground leads to existing grounded devices to compensate for unsatisfactory ground. Ground connections typically consist of metal structures or underground pipe systems. In an emergency, a ground connection can be created by forcing one or more metal rods or stakes into the soil. 

**122** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 

##### **SOIL CONDITIONS** 

H-43. Favorable, less favorable, or unfavorable are the categories used to describe soil ground conditions. When erecting an antenna on soil with low conductivity, the soil can be treated using a variety of grounding techniques to reduce resistance, depending on the specific category and corresponding soil condition. 

###### **Favorable Soil Conditions** 

H-44. For simple installations, operators can fabricate a single ground rod in the field from a pipe or conduit. It is essential to establish a low resistance connection between the ground wire and the ground rod. It is important to clean the rod. Cleaning can be done by scraping and sanding the ground rod at the desired connection point and installing a clean ground clamp. Operators should solder a ground wire to adjoin the clamp and cover the wire with tape to prevent an increase in resistance caused by oxidation. 

###### **Unfavorable Soil Conditions** 

H-45. Unfavorable soil conditions occur when there is a high resistance of the soil or a large, buried ground system is not practical. A counterpoise or a ground screen can replace the direct ground connection in unfavorable soil. 

###### **_Counterpoise_** 

H-46. A counterpoise is a device made of wire, erected a short distance above the ground and insulated from it. A counterpoise can replace the usual direct ground connection with high-resistant soil. The counterpoise should be equal in size to, or larger than, the size of the antenna. Figure H-7 is an example of a counterpoise. 



**Figure H-7. Wire counterpoise** 

###### **_Ground Screen_** 

H-47. A ground screen consists of a large area of metal mesh or screen laid on the surface of the ground under the antenna. There are two advantages to using ground screens. First, the ground screen reduces ground absorption losses that occur when an antenna is installed over an area with poor soil conductivity. Secondly, the height of the antenna can be set accurately. Thus, the radiation resistance of the antenna is determined more accurately. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**123** 

**Appendix H** 

##### **GROUND EFFECTS** 

H-48. The ground alters an antenna’s radiation pattern. The ground also affects the electrical characteristics of antennas, specifically those mounted relatively close to the ground wavelength. For example, medium and HF antennas have radiation patterns that are entirely different from free-space patterns when elevated above the ground by only a fraction of a wavelength. 

#### **TYPES OF ANTENNA** 

H-49. Tactical antenna design requires that the antennas be rugged and permit mobility with the least possible sacrifice of efficiency. Typically, antennas are mounted using either a single mast or suspended between sets of masts. All tactical antennas must be easy to install. Small antennas can be mounted on the helmet of the radio operator. Large antennas must be accessible to dismantle, pack, and transport. 

H-50. The best kinds of wire for antennas are copper and aluminum. In an emergency, operators use any available wire. The exact length of most antennas is critical. An expedient antenna should be the same length as the antenna it replaces. 

##### **HERTZ ANTENNA** 

H-51. A Hertz antenna—also known as a doublet, dipole, an ungrounded, or a half-wave antenna—mounts in a vertical, horizontal, or slanting position and is generally used at frequencies above 2 MHz. With Hertz antennas, the wavelength to which any wire electrically tunes depends directly upon its physical length. The Hertz antenna is center-fed, and its total wire length is equal to approximately half the wavelength of the transmitted signal. 

##### **MARCONI ANTENNA** 

H-52. A Marconi antenna is a quarter-wave antenna with one end usually grounded through the output of the transmitter or the coupling coil at the end of the feed line, which is required for the antenna to resonate. Positioned perpendicular to the Earth, a Marconi antenna is generally useful at lower frequencies. When used on vehicles or aircraft, Marconi antennas operate at high frequencies; and the vehicle or aircraft serves as the ground for the antenna. 

H-53. The main advantage of the Marconi antenna over the Hertz antenna is that, for any given frequency, the Marconi antenna is physically much shorter. This is particularly important in all field and vehicular radio installations. Typical Marconi antennas include the inverted L and the whip. 

#### **ANTENNA LENGTH** 

H-54. An antenna has a physical and electrical length; the two are never the same. The reduced velocity of a wave on the antenna, and a capacitive effect—known as end-effect—make the antenna effectively longer electrically than physically. Contributing factors include the ratio of the diameter of the antenna to its length and the capacitive effect of terminal equipment. 

H-55. To calculate the physical length of an antenna, operators apply a correction factor of 0.95 for frequencies between 3 and 50 MHz. Table H-1 on page 125 illustrates the antenna length calculation for a half-wave antenna. 

**124** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques**

#### **ANTENNA ORIENTATION AND LOCATION CONSIDERATIONS** 

H-56. The orientation of an antenna is critical. Determining the position of an antenna concerning the points of the compass can make the difference between a marginal and good radio circuit. If the azimuth of the radio path is not provided, the azimuth should be determined by the best available means. The accuracy required in determining the azimuth of the path depends on the radiation pattern of the directional antenna. 

H-57. If an antenna has a wide beamwidth is vast (for example, a 90-degree angle between half-power points) a 10-degree azimuth error is of little consequence. In transportable operation, the rhombic and V antennas may have such a narrow beam as to require great accuracy in azimuth determination. If a line of a known azimuth is not available, the antenna with the correct azimuth should be erected at the site. A magnetic compass determines the best direction of the path. Figure H-8 on page 126 is an example of a beamwidth measured on relative field strength and corresponding power patterns. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**125** 

**Appendix H** 



**Figure H-8. Beamwidth** 

H-58. Figure H-9 is an example of a declination diagram. The diagram shows the relationship between the three-north points: magnetic north, grid north, and true north. These points are represented on the bottom margin of a topographic map by a declination diagram. It is important to understand the difference between the three north points and understand how to calculate the three north points. Magnetic azimuth is measured using magnetic instruments such as a lensatic compass or an M2 pocket transit, while a grid azimuth is plotted on a map between two points. The points are joined together by a straight line and a protractor is used to measure the angle between grid north and drawn line. 



**Figure H-9. Example of a declination diagram** 

**126** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 

##### **ULTRAHIGH FREQUENCY ANTENNA** 

H-59. UHF antennas are much shorter and need less space than HF antennas. HF antennas are not used, in general, with VHF and UHF since there are tradeoff considerations between antenna placement and radio performance. Whip antennas are parts of the radio system components. Examples of radio sets with the antennas included are the AN/PRC-119, AN/PRC-148, AN/PRC-152, AN/PRC-150, AN/PRC-158, AN/PRC-162, AN/PRC-163, and AN/PRC-117 series. HF radios may use whip antennas when in VHF (frequency modulation mode); however, the antennas used are usually larger antennas, except for those explained in the matrix shown at table E-2 on page 89. Base station and vehicular antennas for UHF ranges include OE-254, COM-201, COM-201B, OE-303, and vehicular whip (3 meters long), COM-202. 

##### **VERY HIGH FREQUENCY ANTENNAS** 

H-60. VHF propagation is line of sight in nature and is restricted to shorter-range communication. Atmospheric and man-made influences can adversely impact or limit signal transmission. Heavy vegetation and mountainous terrain are factors that can also reduce the expected signal range. Maximum line of sight distance depends on the height of an antenna above the ground. The higher the antenna, the greater the line of sight distance. 

H-61. Due to VHF propagation characteristics as signals travel through the air, obstructions—such as a mountain between the two antennas or dense tree lines—can block or reduce the signal and prevent effective communication. Heavy vegetation weakens radio signals considerably. Therefore, planners must understand the full spectrum of radio communications systems including the effect of antenna types on communication and select and locate antennas to obtain the maximum line of sight range. In vegetation, the antenna should be positioned away from trees that are in the direction of the signal and erected as high as possible. Specific propagation influences that affect VHF transmission and antenna selection include, direct, refracted, reflected, and diffracted rays. Other antenna properties that may improve VHF antenna radiation (omnidirectional, bidirectional, or directional) and efficiency include proper take-off angle, gain, ground screens, and ground planes. See detailed antenna descriptions and properties in section III of this appendix. 

H-62. A relationship exists between antenna length and the wavelength of the transmit frequency. This relationship is important when determining the appropriate antenna for a specific frequency or range of frequencies. The wavelength of a radio signal is the distance traveled in the time it takes to complete one wave cycle. 

##### **MULTIBAND ANTENNA** 

H-63. The Nextgen multiband antenna operates with advanced networking waveform and SINCGARS waveforms to provide transmission reliability for radio operations spanning from lower-lowerband, upperlowerband, and VHF frequencies. The Nextgen multiband antennas are rugged, and of high quality and durable construction with tri-band design and high isolation between ports for VHF, 30–88 MHz; UHF, 225– 450 MHz; and L-band ranges 1200–2110. The UHF dipole and L-band antenna elements are located high up in the whip for maximum range. A ground plane is required for the VHF element. Additional features include— 

- Designed for operation on all military platforms. 

- Suitable for operation on shelters, mounted on masts or in other permanent installations. 

- Includes North Atlantic Treaty Organization flange base. 

- Optional GPS installed in the antenna base for L1 and L2 configurations. 

#### **IMPROVEMENT OF MARGINAL COMMUNICATION** 

H-64. Under certain situations, it may not be possible to orient directional antennas to the correct azimuth of the desired radio path, possibly causing marginal communications. To improve marginal communications, operators should— 

- Check, tighten, and tape cable couplings and connections. 

- Check antenna adjustment for the proper operating frequency (if possible). 

- Change the antenna height. 

- Move the antenna a short distance away, and in different locations, from its original location. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**127** 

**Appendix H** 

- Separate transmitters from receiving equipment, if possible. 

- Separate transmitters from power fields. 

- Ensure transmission lines do not cross power lines. 

H-65. An improvised antenna may change the performance of a radio set. An operator can use a distant station to test whether an antenna is operating correctly. If the signal received from this station is strong, the antenna is operating satisfactorily. If the signal is weak, operators should adjust the height and length of the antenna and transmission line to receive the strongest signal at a given setting on the volume control of the receiver. 

H-66. Impedance matching a load to its source is an important consideration in transmission systems. If the load and source are mismatched, part of the power reflects along the transmission line toward the source. This prevents maximum power transfer and can be responsible for erroneous measurements of other parameters. It may also cause circuit damage in high-power applications. 

H-67. The power reflected from the load interferes with the forward incident power, creating standing waves of voltages and current to exist along the line. Standing wave maximum-to-minimum ratio directly relates to the impedance mismatch of the load. Therefore, the standing wave ratio provides the means of determining impedance mismatch. 

H-68. After site selection and proper antenna orientation, the signal level at the receiver is proportional to the strength of the transmitted signal. Operators can obtain a stronger signal using a high-gain antenna. Using a high quality transmission line as short as possible and adequately matched at both ends reduces signal loss between the antenna and the equipment. 

**_Note._** Excessive signal strength may result in threat interception and electromagnetic interference, or interference with adjacent frequencies. 

##### **SECTION II – PROPAGATION THEORY** 

H-69. The behavior and frequency of radio waves as they travel govern the level and quality of the signal received. Radio waves are also affected by the medium through which they travel and the objects in their path. This section describes three types of propagation—line of sight, ground wave, and skywave and lists additional factors affecting the way in which radio signals or radio waves propagate. 

#### **LINE OF SIGHT** 

H-70. When planning and establishing radio networks, what is between two antennas is an important consideration. Line of sight is the path between two antennas. Line of sight can have the following characteristics— 

- No obstacles reside between the two antennas. 

- Partial obstructions, such as trees between the two antennas. 

- Full obstructions exist between the two antennas. 

#### **GROUND WAVE** 

H-71. Ground wave propagation does not depend on waves that refract from the ionosphere skywaves. The electrical characteristics of the Earth and the amount of diffraction affect ground wave propagation. The strength of the ground wave at the receiver depends on the transmitter power output and frequency, the shape and conductivity of the Earth along the transmission path, and the local weather conditions. Figure H-10 on page 129 shows an example of possible routes for ground waves. 

**128** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 



**Figure H-10. Possible routes for ground waves** 

##### **DIRECT WAVE** 

H-72. The direct wave travels directly from the transmitting antenna to the receiving antenna. The direct part of the wave is limited to the line of sight distance between the transmitting and receiving antennas, and the small distance added by atmospheric refraction and diffraction of the wave around the curvature of the Earth. Increasing the height of the transmitting or receiving antenna, or both, can extend the antenna range. 

##### **GROUND-REFLECTED WAVE** 

H-73. The ground wave reaches the receiving antenna after reflecting from the surface of the Earth. Cancellation of the radio signal can occur when the ground reflected component and the direct wave component arrive at the receiving antenna at the same time and are 180 degrees out of phase. 

##### **SURFACE WAVE** 

H-74. The surface wave follows the Earth’s curvature. The Earth’s conductivity and dielectric constant affect the surface wave. 

##### **CHARACTERISTICS OF GROUND WAVES** 

H-75. Various frequencies determine which wave component prevails along a given signal path. For example, when the Earth’s conductivity is high, and the frequency of a radiated signal is low, the surface wave is the predominant component. For frequencies below 10 MHz, the surface wave is sometimes the predominant component. Above 10 MHz, losses sustained by the surface wave component are significant that the other components, direct and skywave become predominant. 

H-76. At frequencies of 30–300 kHz, ground losses are very small, so the surface wave component follows the Earth’s curvature. Radio operators use the Earth’s curvature for long-distance communication, provided the radio operator has enough power from the transmitter. Use 300 kHz–3 MHz frequencies for long distance communication over seawater and for medium-distance communication over land. 

H-77. At the HF range (3–30 MHz), ground conductivity is extremely important, especially above 10 MHz where the dielectric constant or conductivity of the Earth’s surface determines how much signal absorption occurs. In general, the signal is strongest at lower frequencies when the surface over which it travels has a high dielectric constant and conductivity. 

H-78. The dielectric constant or Earth’s surface conductivity determines how much of the surface wave signal energy absorbed or lost. The Earth’s surface conductivity is generally poor. Table H-2 on page 130 shows a comparison of the conductivity of varying surface conditions.

#### **SKYWAVE** 

H-79. Radio communications that use skywave propagation depend on the ionosphere to provide the signal path between the transmitting and receiving antennas. The ionosphere has four distinct layers. These regions are labeled D, E, F1, and F2, in the order of increasing heights and decreasing molecular densities. During the day, when the rays of the sun direct toward that portion of the atmosphere, all four regions may be present. During the night, the F1 and F2 regions seem to merge into a single F layer, while the D and E regions fade out. The actual number of regions, their height above the Earth, and their relative intensity of ionization vary continuously. Table H-3 provides a description of the ionosphere regions. Figure H-11 on page 131 shows the average layer distribution of the ionosphere. 

**Table H-3. Ionosphere layers** 

|**_Region_**|**_Description_**|
|---|---|
|D Region|Exists only during daylight hours and has little effect in bending the paths of HF radio waves.<br>The main effect of the D region is to attenuate HF waves when the transmission path is in<br>sunlit regions.|
|E Region|Used during the day for HF radio transmission over intermediate distances (less than 2,400<br>kilometers [1,500 miles]). At night, the intensity of the E region decreases, and it becomes<br>useless for radio transmission.|
|F Region|Exists at heights up to 380 kilometers (240 miles) above the Earth and is ionized all the time.<br>It has two well-defined layers (F1 and F2) during the day and one layer (F) during the night.<br>At night, the F region remains at the height of about 260 kilometers (170 miles) and is useful<br>for long-range radio communications (over 2,400 kilometers [1,500 miles]). The F2 layer is<br>the most useful of all layers for long-range radio communications, although its degree of<br>ionization varies appreciablyfrom dayto day.|
|**Legend:**<br>HF<br>hig|h frequency|

**Figure H-11. Average layer distribution of the ionosphere** 

H-80. The movement of the Earth around the sun and changes in the sun’s activity contribute to ionospheric variations. These variations are either regular, predictable, or irregular, which occurs from the abnormal behavior of the sun. Table H-4 shows the periodic variations of the ionosphere. 

**Table H-4. Regular variations of the ionosphere**

H-81. Planners should anticipate the status of the four regular variations. Irregular variations have a degrading effect, at times blanking out communications, and cannot be controlled or compensated for. Table H-5 on page 132 lists some irregular variations of the ionosphere.
