|**Legend:**<br>MHz<br>megah|ertz|



H-82. Sunspots generate bursts of radiation that cause high levels of ionization. More sunspots equate to greater ionization. During periods of low sunspot activity, frequencies above 20 MHz tend to be unusable because the E and F layer’s weak ionization reflects the signal back to Earth. At the peak of the sunspot cycle, it is unusual to have worldwide propagation on frequencies above 30 MHz. 

H-83. Primarily, the ionization density of each layer determines the range of long-distance radio transmissions, the higher the frequency, the greater the ionization density required to reflect radio waves back to Earth. The upper E and F regions reflect the higher frequencies because they are the most highly ionized. The D region, which is the least ionized, does not reflect frequencies above approximately 500 kHz. In each ionized region, there is an upper frequency limit known as critical frequency, which radio waves sent vertically upward reflect back to Earth. 

H-84. Radio waves directed vertically at frequencies higher than the critical frequency pass through the ionized layer of the Earth’s atmosphere into outer space. All radio waves directed vertically into the ionosphere at frequencies lower than the critical frequency reflect back to Earth. 

H-85. Generally, radio waves used in communications directed toward the ionosphere at some oblique angle called the angle of incidence. Radio waves at frequencies above the critical frequency reflect to Earth if transmitted at angles of incidence smaller than the critical angle, called the critical angle. At the critical angle, and at all angles larger than the critical angle, the radio waves pass through the ionosphere if the frequency is higher than the critical frequency. 

##### **TRANSMISSION PATHS** 

H-86. Skywave propagation refers to those types of radio transmissions that depend on the ionosphere to provide signal paths between transmitters and receivers. Skip distance refers to the distance from the transmitting antenna to the location, where the skywaves first return to Earth. The skip distance depends upon the angle of incidence, the operating frequency, and the height and density of the ionosphere. 

H-87. The antenna height and the operating frequency affect the angles at which transmitted radio waves strike and penetrate the ionosphere and then return to Earth. Control this angle of incidence to obtain the desired area of coverage. Lowering the antenna height increases the angle of transmission. This provides broad and even signal patterns in an area the size of a typical corps. Near-vertical incidence skywave uses near-vertical transmission paths. Raising the antenna height lowers the angle of incidence. Figure H-12 on page 133 is an example of skywave transmission paths. 

**132** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 



**Figure H-12. Skywave transmission path** 

H-88. Lowering the angle of incidence can produce a skip zone in which does not allow the receipt of a usable signal. This bounds the area by the outer edge of usable ground wave propagation and the point nearest the antenna at which the skywave returns to Earth. Low angles of incidence make long-distance communications possible. 

H-89. When a transmitted wave reflects back to the surface of the Earth, the Earth absorbs part of its energy. The remainder of its energy is reflected into the ionosphere and back to Earth. This means of transmission (alternately reflecting the radio wave between the ionosphere and the Earth) referred to as hops, enables the reception of radio waves at great distances from the point of origin. Figure H-13 is an example of skywave transmission hop paths. 



**Figure H-13. Skywave transmission hop path** 

###### **Fading** 

H-90. Fading is the periodic increase and decrease of received signal strength. Fading occurs from a radio signal received over a long distance path in the HF range. There is little common knowledge of how to reduce or eliminate fading effects. Fading associated with skywave paths is a significant detriment to reliable 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**133** 

**Appendix H** 

communication. Those responsible for communications circuits rely on raising the transmitter power or increasing antenna gain to overcome fading. Sections often do not work and seldom improve reliability. Only when the signal level fades below the background noise level for an appreciable fraction of time will an increase in transmitter power or antenna gain yield an overall circuit improvement. Choosing the correct frequency and using transmitting and receiving equipment properly ensure a reliable and robust receive signal, even when low power is used. 

###### **Maximum Usable Frequency and Lowest Usable Frequency** 

H-91. The maximum usable frequency is the maximum frequency at which a radio wave returns to Earth at a given distance when using a given ionized layer and a transmitting antenna with a fixed angle of radiation. The monthly median of the daily highest frequency predicted for skywave transmission over a path in a specific hour of the day. The maximum usable frequency is always higher than the critical frequency because the angle of incidence is less than 90 degrees. 

H-92. If the distance between the transmitter and the receiver increases, the maximum usable frequency also increases. Radio waves lose some of their energy through absorption by the D region, and a portion of the E region of the ionosphere, on specific transmission frequencies. The total absorption is less, and communications more satisfactory, when using higher frequencies up to the level of the maximum usable frequency. 

H-93. The absorption rate is most significant for frequencies ranging from approximately 500 kHz–2 MHz during the day. During the night, the absorption rate decreases for all frequencies. As the frequency of transmission over any skywave path increases from low to high, a frequency occurs at which the received signal overrides the level of atmospheric and other radio noise interference. This is the lowest usable frequency because frequencies lower than these are too weak for useful communications. The lowest usable frequency also depends on the power output of the transmitter and the transmission distance. When the lowest usable frequency is greater than the maximum usable frequency, no skywave transmission is possible. The spectrum manager uses Spectrum XXI to identify optimum frequency groupings. 

##### **OTHER FACTORS AFFECTING PROPAGATION** 

H-94. In the VHF and UHF ranges (30–300 MHz and beyond), the presence of an object may produce strong reflections that arrive at the receiving antenna in a way that cancels the desired signal and renders communication ineffective. 

H-95. Planners should choose receiver locations away from airfields due to possible adverse electromagnetic interference from signals bouncing off the aircraft. Planners and operators should avoid locating transmitters and receivers near airfields or near the midpoint of the propagation path of frequencies above 20 MHz. 

H-96. Many other factors may affect the propagation of a radio wave. Hills, mountains, buildings, water towers, tall fences, and even other antenna can affect the condition and reliability of a propagation path. The conductivity of the local ground or body of water can significantly alter the strength of the transmitted or received signal. Energy radiation from the Sun’s surface also dramatically affects conditions in the ionosphere and alters the characteristics of long-distance propagation at 2–30 MHz. 

###### **Path Loss** 

H-97. Radio waves become weaker as they travel outwards from the transmitter. Path loss refers to the ratio of the received power to the transmit power. Line of sight paths at VHF and UHF require relatively little power since the total path loss at the radio horizon is only about 25 decibels greater than the path loss over the same distance in free space (absence of ground). This additional loss results from some energy reflected from the ground, canceling part of the direct wave energy. This is unavoidable in almost every practical case. The total path loss for a line of sight path above average terrain varies with the following factors: 

- Total path loss between transmitting and receiving antenna terminals. 

- Frequency. 

- Distance. 

- Transmitting antenna gain. 

- Receiving antenna gain. 

**134** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 

###### • Reflected Waves. 

H-98. It is often possible to communicate beyond the normal line of sight distance by exploiting the reflection from a tall building, nearby mountain, or water tower. If the transmitting and receiving antennas can see the top portion of a structure or hill readily, it may be possible to achieve efficient communications by directing both antennas toward the point of maximum reflection. If the reflecting object is very large in terms of a wavelength, the path loss, including the reflection, can be very low. 

H-99. If a structure or hill exists adjacent to a line of sight path, reflected energy may either add to or subtract from the energy arriving from the direct path. If the reflected energy arrives at the receiving antenna with the same strength as the direct signal but has the opposite phase, the signals cancel, and communication is impossible. Under the same condition exists, if both signals arrive in phase they add and double the effective signal strength. These two conditions represent destructive and constructive combinations of the reflected and direct waves. 

H-100. Reflection from the ground at the common midpoint between the receiving and transmitting antennas may also arrive constructively or destructively. Generally, in the VHF and UHF range, the reflected wave is out of phase (destructive) concerning the direct wave at vertical angles less than a few degrees above the horizon. Since the ground is not a perfect conductor, the amplitude of the reflected wave seldom approaches that of the direct wave. Thus, even though the two arrive out of phase, complete cancellation does not occur. Improvements may result from using vertical polarization rather than horizontal polarization over line of sight paths because there tends to be less phase difference between direct and reflected waves. The difference is usually less than 10 decibels, in favor of vertical polarization. 

###### **Diffraction** 

H-101. Unlike a ship passing beyond the visual horizon, a radio wave does not fade out completely when it reaches the radio horizon. A small amount of radio energy travels beyond the radio horizon by a process called diffraction. Diffraction also occurs when a light source held near an opaque object, casts a shadow on a surface behind it. Near the edge of the shadow, a narrow band displays which is neither completely light nor dark. The transition from total light to total darkness does not occur abruptly but changes smoothly as the light diffracts. 

H-102. A radio wave passing over either the curved surface of the Earth or a mountain ridge behaves in much the same fashion as a light wave. For example, people living in a valley below a high, sharp, mountain ridge can often receive a television station located many miles below on the other side. Mountain ridges diffract and bend television station waves downward in the direction of the town. The energy decays very rapidly as the angle of propagation departs from the straight line of sight path. Typically, a diffracted signal may undergo a reduction of 30–40 decibels by a bend of only 1.5 meters (5 feet) by a mountain ridge. The actual amount of diffracted signal depends on the shape of the surface, the frequency, the diffraction angle, and many other factors. 

###### **Refraction** 

H-103. Refraction is the bending of a wave as it passes through air layers of different density. In semitropical regions, a layer of air 5–100 meters (16–328 feet) thick with distinctive characteristics may form close to the ground, usually the result of a temperature inversion. For example, on an unusually warm day after a period of rain, the Sun may heat up the ground and create a layer of warm, moist air. After sunset, the air a few meters above the ground cools very rapidly while the moisture in the air close to the ground serves as a blanket for the remaining heat. After a few hours, a significant difference in temperature may exist between the air near the ground and the air at the height of 10–20 meters (32.8–65.6 feet), resulting in a marked difference in air pressure. Thus, the air near the ground is considerably denser than the air higher up. This condition may exist over an area of several hundred square kilometers or an extended area of land near a seacoast. When an air mass forms, it usually remains stable until dawn, when the ground begins to cool, and the temperature inversion ends. 

H-104. When a VHF or UHF radio wave launches in air mass, it may bend or become trapped and forced to follow the inversion layer. This layer then acts as a duct between the transmitting antenna and a distant receiving site. The effects of ducting frequently occur during the year in locations where television or VHF 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**135** 

**Appendix H** 

modulation stations received over paths of several hundred kilometers. The total path loss within a duct is usually very low and may exceed the free space loss by only a few decibels. 

H-105. It is possible to communicate over long distances utilizing tropospheric scatter. At altitudes of a few kilometers, the air mass has a varying temperature, pressure, and moisture content. Small fluctuations in tropospheric characteristics at high altitude create blobs. In a blob, the temperature, pressure, and humidity are different from the surrounding air. If the difference is large enough, it may modify the refractive index at VHF and UHF. A random distribution of these blobs exists at various altitudes. If a high-power transmitter greater than 1 kilowatt and a high-gain (10 decibels or more) antenna are used, enough energy may be scattered from these blobs down to the receiver to make reliable communication possible over several hundred kilometers. Communications circuits employing this mode of propagation must use susceptible receivers and the form of diversity to reduce the effects of the rapid and deep fading. Scatter propagation is usually limited to path distances of less than about 500 kilometers (311 miles). 

###### **Noise** 

H-106. Noise consists of all undesired radio signals, man-made or natural. Noise masks and degrades useful information reception. The radio signal’s strength is of little importance if the signal power is greater than the received noise power. The signal to noise ratio is an important factor in a receiving system. Increasing receiver amplification cannot improve the signal to noise ratio since signal and noise amplifies and signal to noise ratio remains unchanged. Typically, receivers have more than enough amplification. 

H-107. Natural noise has two principal sources: thunderstorms (atmospheric noise) and stars (galactic noise). Both sources generate sharp pulses of electromagnetic energy over all frequencies. The pulses propagate according to the same laws as man-made signals and receiving systems must accept them and the desired signal. Atmospheric noise is dominant from 0–5 MHz; galactic noise is most important at higher frequencies. Low frequency transmitters must generate very strong signals to overcome noise. Strong signals and strong noise mean that the receiving antenna does not have to be large to collect a usable signal. A 1.5meter (4.9-foot) tuned whip antenna adequately delivers all the signals received at frequencies below 1 MHz. 

H-108. Man-made noise appears wherever electric power is used. Each source is small, but there are so many that together they can completely overwhelm a weak signal that would be above the natural noise in rural areas. Man-made noise is troublesome when the receiving antenna is near the source, but being near the source produces exploitable noise wave characteristics. Waves near a source tend to be vertically polarized. A horizontally polarized receiving antenna generally receives less noise than a vertically polarized antenna. 

H-109. Any conductors near the source, including the antenna, transmission line, and equipment cases, induce man-made noise currents. If the antenna and transmission lines should balance with respect to the ground, then the noise voltages will balance and cancel with respect to the receiver input terminals zero voltage across terminals and this noise will not receive. Near perfect balance is difficult to achieve, but any balance may help. 

H-110. Other ways to avoid man-made noise are to locate troublesome sources and turn them off or move the receiving system away from them. Moving at least one kilometer (.6 miles) away from a busy street or highway significantly reduces noise. Broadband receiving antennas are convenient because they do not require tuning to each working frequency, sometimes a narrowband antenna can make the difference between communicating and not communicating. The HF band is now so crowded with users that electromagnetic interference and noise, not signal strength, are the main reasons for poor communications. A narrowband antenna rejects strong interfering signals near the desired frequency and helps maintain good communication. 

##### **<mark>SECTION III – VERY HIGH FREQUENCY AND ULTRAHIGH FREQUENCY ANTENNA TECHNIQUES</mark>** 

##### **ANTENNA TYPE** 

H-111. Army forces employ a wide array of VHF and UHF antennas to expand communications over a more extended range and transmit communications signals to more users. Because VHF and UHF antennas are small, it is possible to use multiple radiating elements to form arrays, which provide a considerable gain in a 

**136** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 

direction(s). An array is an arrangement of antenna elements, usually, dipoles used to control the direction in which most of the antenna power radiates. 

##### **ANTENNA SELECTION** 

H-112. The VHF portion of the radio spectrum extends from 30–300 MHz and the UHF range reaches from 300–3,000 MHz (0–3 GHz). Both frequency ranges are extremely useful for short-range communications— less than 50 kilometers (31 miles). This includes point-to-point, mobile, air-to-ground, and general-purpose communications. Wavelengths at these frequency ranges are considerably shorter. 

H-113. In the VHF and UHF portion of the spectrum, there are sub-frequency bands for specific uses, such as VHF aircraft band, UHF aircraft band, and public communications. 

##### **ANTENNA SELECTION PROCEDURE** 

H-114. VHF and UHF antennas, such as the whip antenna, can be mounted on vehicles allowing units to extend communication over an extended range. Planners also consider weather during antenna selection and employment. High winds may adversely impact large antennas that comprise some signal systems degrading communication during a mission. When selecting VHF or UHF antennas, planners should consider— 

- Propagation. 

- Desired coverage and characteristics. 

- Construction material. 

- Site selection. 

- Power and power supply. 

- Polarization. 

- Gain. 

- Directivity. 

##### **POLARIZATION** 

H-115. In many countries, frequency modulation and television broadcast in the VHF range using horizontal polarization. One reason is that it reduces ignition interference, which is mainly vertically polarized. Mobile communications often use vertical polarization or two reasons. First, the vehicle antenna installation has physical limitations, and second, there is no interruption in the reception or transmission as the vehicle changes heading. 

H-116. Using directional antennas and horizontal polarization, when possible, reduces man-made noise interference in urban locations. Choose horizontal polarization only where an antenna height of many wavelengths is possible. Ground reflections tend to cancel horizontally polarized waves at low angles. Use only vertically polarized antennas when the antenna is less than 10 meters (33 feet) above the ground, or when desiring omnidirectional radiation or reception. 

##### **GAIN AND DIRECTIVITY** 

H-117. VHF and UHF above 30 MHz antenna gain are extremely important for several reasons. Assuming the same antenna gain and propagation path, the received signal strength drops as the frequency is increased. At VHF and UHF, more of the received signal is lost in the transmission line than is lost at HF. A 10–20 decibel loss is uncommon in a 30-meter (98.4 feet) length of the coaxial line at 450 MHz. 

H-118. At frequencies below 30 MHz, system sensitivity is usually limited by receive noise rather than by noise external to the antenna. Generally, wider modulation or signal bandwidths are employed in VHF and UHF transmissions rather than in the HF range. Since system noise power is directly proportional to bandwidth, additional antenna gain is necessary to preserve a usable signal to noise ratio. 

H-119. VHF and UHF antenna gain and directivity aids security by restricting how much power radiated in unwanted directions. Receiver sensitivity is generally poorer at VHF and UHF except for high quality stateof-the-art receivers. Obstructions buildings, trees, hills may seriously decrease the signal strength available to the receiving antenna because VHF and UHF signals travel a straight line of sight path. 

H-120. Obtaining communications reliability over difficult VHF and UHF propagation paths requires considerable attention to the design of high-gain directive antenna arrays. Unlike HF communications, the 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**137** 

**Appendix H** 

shorter VHF and UHF wavelengths support walkie-talkie transceivers and simple mobile transmissions units. Communicating or receiving with such devices over distances beyond 1 or 2 kilometers requires maximum antenna gain at the base station or fixed end of the link. 

H-121. An array provides directivity the ability to concentrate radiated energy into a beam directed toward the intended receiver. Arrays of resonant elements, half-wave dipoles, constructed of rigid metal rods or tubing or copper foil laid out or pasted on a flat non-conducting surface. Directing power helps to increase the range of the communications path and tends to decrease the likelihood of the interception of jamming from hostile radio stations. Highly directive antennas place an added burden on the radio operator to ensure that the antenna is properly oriented. 

##### **WHIP ANTENNA** 

H-122. Whip antennas for VHF tactical radio sets are usually 4.5 meters (15 feet) long. A HF vehicular whip antenna has a planning range of 400–4,000 kilometers (250–2,500 miles). 

H-123. Lightweight portable frequency modulation radios use two whip antennas: 0.9-meter (2.9-foot) semi-rigid steel tape antenna, and a 3-meter (10-foot) multi-section whip antenna. These antennas are shorter than a quarter wavelength to ensure they are a practical length. A quarter wavelength antenna for a 5.0 MHz radio would be over 14 meters (46 feet) long. An antenna tuning unit, either built into the radio set or supplied with it, compensates for the missing length of the antenna. The tuning unit varies the electrical length of the antenna to accommodate a range of frequencies. 

H-124. Whip antennas on tactical radio sets radiate equally in all directions on the horizontal plane. Since stations in a radio net lie in random directions and change their positions frequently, the radiation pattern is ideal for tactical communications. 

H-125. When a whip antenna mounts on a vehicle, the metal of the vehicle affects the operation of the antenna. Thus, the direction in which the vehicle is facing may also affect transmission and reception, particularly of distant or weak signals. Tactical whip antennas are electrically short, vertical, base loaded types, fed with a non-resonant coaxial cable of about 52 ohms impedance. Figure H-14 is an example of a whip antenna. 



**Figure H-14. Whip antenna** 

H-126. To attain efficiency comparable to that of a half-wave antenna, operators should position the height of the tactical whip antenna to a quarter wavelength antenna. This is not always possible; therefore, operators should use the loaded whip instead. The loading increases the electrical length of the vertical radiator to a quarter wavelength. The ground, counterpoise, or any conducting surface large enough, supplies the missing quarter-wavelength of the antenna. 

**138** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 

H-127. Figure H-15 shows the best direction for whip antennas mounted on vehicles. A vehicle with a whip antenna mounted on the left rear side of the vehicle transmits its strongest signal in a line running from the antenna through the right front side of the vehicle. Similarly, an antenna mounted on the right rear side of the vehicle radiates its strongest signal in a direction toward the left front side. In some cases, the best reception from signals traveling in the direction shown by the dashed arrows on the figure. In some cases, the best path for transmission can be determined by driving the vehicle in a small circle until the best position is located. Normally, the best direction for receiving from a distant station is also the best direction for transmitting to the same station. 



**Figure H-15. Whip antennas mounted on a vehicle** 

H-128. A whip antenna mounted on a vehicle may require leaving the antenna fully extended for instant use while the vehicle is in motion. The base-mounted insulator of the whip is fitted with a coil spring attached to a mounting bracket on the vehicle. The spring base allows horizontal tie down of the whip antenna when the vehicle is in motion, and when driving under low bridges or obstructions. Even in the vertical position, if the antenna hits an obstruction, the whip usually will not break because the spring base absorbs most of the shock. 

H-129. The energy leaving a whip antenna travels downward and the ground reflects the energy with little loss. Raising the whip antenna increases the transmitting and receiving range. When raised, the efficiency of a whip antenna decreases because it is further from the ground. Therefore, when using a whip antenna at the top of a mast, the operator should supply an elevated substitute for the ground plane. 

##### **DANGER** 

**When fully extended and in motion, drivers must avoid antenna** **<mark>contact with overhead power lines. Death or serious injury can result if a vehicular antenna strikes a high-voltage transmission line. When tied down, operators should ensure the antenna tip</mark> protector is in place.**

H-130. The OE-254 is a broadband omnidirectional antenna that contains a balanced to the unbalanced transformer and supports frequency hopping. Table H-6 on page 140 shows the planning ranges for the OE254 antenna. The OE-254 antenna— 

- Operates in the 30–88 MHz range without any physical adjustments.

##### **COM 201B ANTENNA** 

H-131. The COM 201B antenna is a commercial, VHF and UHF, vertically polarized, omnidirectional antenna. Its unique design supports quick employment to support different applications. The COM 201B antenna has a tripod leg structure that allows mounting the antenna directly on the ground or on a standard communications mast. The COM 201B tripod legs provide the ground wave radiation of the signal transmitted and are necessary for proper operations. The COM 201B supports quick assembly and disassembly for transport. This makes it ideal for situations where there is not enough time to erect an OE254 antenna. 

H-132. The COM 201B antenna’s ease of operation makes it ideal for use as a field expedient antenna or mounting to a vehicle when requiring more elevation. The eye fitting at the top of the antenna facilitates suspending it from buildings or trees when a mast is not available and when greater height is needed. The COM 201B antenna has the following characteristics and capabilities— 

- Provides range extending from 30–88 MHz. 

- Operates vertically polarized with no requirement for active tuning. 

- Uses input impedance of 50 ohms unbalanced with an average voltage standing wave ratio of 2.5:1, at RF power levels up to 200 watts. 

- Supports maximum power towards the horizon with a typical antenna gain of zero. 

- Uses minimal personnel to assemble and erect and can be assembled by one Soldier. 

- Uses compact assemblages that allow it to be stored in a space less than 36 inches long by 10 inches in diameter. 

##### **QUICK ERECT ANTENNA MAST** 

H-133. The quick erect antenna mast, AB 1386/U elevates tactical radio antennas to a maximum height of 10 meters (33 feet) for more reliable communication over extended ranges. The quick erect antenna mast uses the same antenna elements and RF cable as the OE-254 antenna. The mast can deploy and operate in a ground or vehicular (wheeled or tracked) configuration. The antenna erects in less than 8 minutes with 2 Soldiers and in 15 minutes with one Soldier. 

##### **VERY HIGH FREQUENCY HALF-RHOMBIC ANTENNA** 

H-134. The VHF half-rhombic antenna is a high gain, lightweight, directional antenna that operates over the frequency range of 30–88 MHz. The antenna and ancillary equipment, guy wires, stakes, tools, and mast sections fit in a carrying bag for manpack or vehicular transportation. The OE-303, VHF half-rhombic antenna is a vertically polarized antenna that, when used with VHF radios, extends the range of transmission considerably, and provides some electromagnetic protection. The half-rhombic antenna, when properly employed, decreases VHF radio susceptibility to jamming and enhances the communications ranges of radios. This effect occurs by directing the maximum signal strength in the direction of the desired friendly unit. 

**140**

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 

H-135. The planning range for the OE-303 is equivalent to the planning range of the OE-254. The OE-303 antenna is used with the AB-1244 mast assembly, consisting of 12 tubular mast sections—five lower-mast sections, one mast transition adapter, five upper-mast sections, and antenna adapter, a mast base assembly, and different ancillary equipment. Stabilization of the erected mast assembly uses a two-level, four-way guying system. Figure H-16 shows an example of the OE-303 VHF half-rhombic antenna.

- The OE-303 antenna handles RF power levels up to 200 watts. It matches nominal 50-ohm impedance with a voltage standing wave ratio of no more than 2:1, over the entire frequency range of the antenna. The antenna has the following characteristics and capabilities: 

   - Erectable in a space of 53.3 meters (175 feet) diameter, or less, depending upon the frequency. 

   - Mountable on any structure at least 15.2 meters (50 feet) high. 

   - Azimuth directional change within 1 minute. 

   - Transported by manpack or tactical vehicle when fitted into a package. 

   - Operation with the four-port frequency hopping multiplexer. 

H-136. The OE-303 antenna supports special applications and is a task assigned as required. Its primary use is on command and intelligence networks to a higher headquarters. Units that habitually operate over extended distances from parent units and special task units use the OE-303 half-rhombic antenna. 

##### **FIELD EXPEDIENT ANTENNAS** 

H-137. Field expedient antennas are generally used for HF communication. Field expedient antennas that can be used for VHF include the half-rhombic, half wave dipole, and improvised vertical half-wave. 

##### **DIRECTIONAL ANTENNAS** 

H-138. Operators should use directional antennas whenever possible to minimize the probability of detection by threat electromagnetic sensors. This may require the prefabrication of VHF directional antennas since these are not available in the current Army inventory. Operators can change antenna polarization on systems where distance is not an issue. A horizontally polarized ground wave has less signal loss than a vertically polarized ground wave if antenna heights exceed treetop levels or other horizontal energy absorbers. 

##### **MAST ASSEMBLIES** 

H-139. If possible, Operators should stack antennas in the null space of another vertical antenna. The radiation pattern of a vertical antenna has a deep energy void directly overhead 90 degrees. Figure H-17

shows possible antenna stacks. Operators should configure the mast assemblies to mount two OE-254 broadband antennas using vertical separation. 

H-140. Both dual-antenna mast assemblies must provide at least 12 decibels or greater antenna isolation (at 30 MHz) over that obtained using the same distance horizontal separation. Operators can take advantage of the lateral wave propagation of vertical antennas. Energy transference is negligible on a receiving antenna in this null space. Early fabrication of mounting devices may be required to achieve antenna stacking.

##### **GROUNDING** 

H-141. Operators must properly ground electronic equipment in the command post to electromagnetic interference-and reduce electrical shock hazards. Another option is to counterpoise the antenna. The wires used in the counterpoise should be either a half wavelength or a full wavelength long for best results. Greater directional gain can be achieved by orienting the counterpoise wires in the direction of the receiving antenna.

##### **ANTENNA SELECTION** 

H-147. An important consideration in addressing line of sight equipment is the antenna elevation with respect to the path terrain. Planners should choose sites that exploit natural elevations. H-148. An important consideration in siting over-the-horizon systems is the antenna horizon screening angles at the terminals. As the horizon angle increases, the transmission loss increases, resulting in a weaker signal. 

H-149. The effect of the horizon on transmission loss is significant. Except where the consideration of one or more other factors outweighs the effect of horizon angles, the site with the most negative angle should be the first choice. If no sites with negative angles exist, the site with the smallest positive angle should be the first choice. 

H-150. The horizon angle can be determined by using a transit to survey each site and sighting along the circuit path. The on-site survey determines the visual horizon angle. Though the radio horizon angle is slightly different from the visual horizon angle, the difference is generally insignificant. 

H-151. During the survey, planners measure the horizon angle between the tangent at the proposed location of the antenna and a direct line of sight to the horizon. The tangent line is a right angle to a plumb line at the antenna site. If the line of sight to the horizon is below the tangent line, the horizon angle is negative. 

H-152. Trees, buildings, hills, or the curvature of the Earth can partially block UHF signals (obstruction loss). To avoid signal loss due to obstruction requires a clearance between the direct line of sight and the terrain. Planners use path profile plots to determine if adequate clearance in line-of-sight systems. 

H-153. SATCOM sets weakening or producing distorted signals may result from operating near steel bridges, water towers, power lines, or power units. The presence of congested air-traffic conditions on the proximity of microwave equipment can result in significant signal fading. 

##### **SECTION V – FIELD EXPEDIENT ANTENNA REPAIR** 

H-154. Broken or damaged antennas may cause poor communications or communications failure. If a spare antenna is available, the operator should replace the damaged antenna. When a spare is not available, the user may have to construct an emergency field expedient antenna. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**143** 

**Appendix H** 

H-155. Knowing the structural makeup of an antenna aids in assessing its condition and determining what material is required to reconstruct faulty or malfunctioning parts. The following paragraphs provide examples of field repair techniques for tactical radio antennas. 

##### **WHIP ANTENNA** 

H-156. If a whip antenna is broken into two sections, an operator can temporarily repair the antenna by rejoining the sections. The operator must remove the paint at the join and clean the sections to ensure a good electrical connection. After placing the sections together, the operator secures them with a pole or branch, and then lashes them together with bare wire or tape above and below the break. Figure H-18 (example A) shows a field repair for a broken whip antenna using a pole or branch. 

H-157. Severely damaged whip antennas may be temporarily repaired with a length of field wire the same length as the original antenna. The operator removes the insulation from the lower end of the field wire, twist the conductors together, inserts them into the antenna base connector, and secures them with a wooden block. The operator should use either a pole or a tree branch the same length as the broken antenna to support the antenna wire. Figure H-18 (example B) shows a field repair for a broken whip antenna using field wire. 



**Figure H-18. Field repair of broken whip antennas** 

H-158. Emergency repair of a wire antenna may involve the repair or replacement of the wire used as the antenna or transmission line. It may also require the repair or replacement of the assembly used to support the antenna. When one or more antenna wires are broken, reconnecting the broken wires can repair the antenna. To do this, the operator lowers the antenna to the ground, cleans the ends of the wires, and twists the wires together. When possible, the operator should solder the connection and reassemble. 

H-159. Damaged antenna supports may require using a substitute for the damaged support and if properly insulated may consist of any material of adequate strength. Radiating elements not correctly insulated may short field antennas to ground and be ineffective. 

H-160. Operators may use many common items as expedient field insulators. Plastic or glass, including plastic spoons, buttons, bottlenecks, and plastic bags are the best insulator. Wood and rope also act as insulators, though they are less effective than plastic or glass. The antenna wire should only touch the antenna terminal and be electronically isolated from all other objects other than the supporting insulator. Figure H-19 on page 145 shows examples of field expedient antenna insulators. 

**144** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Antenna Selection and Techniques** 



**Figure H-19. Examples of field expedient antenna insulator** 

##### **ANTENNA GUYS** 

H-161. Antenna guys stabilize the supports for an antenna. They are usually made of wire, manila rope, or nylon rope. An operator can repair a broken guy rope by tying the broken ends together. If the rope is too short after tying, the operator should add another piece of rope or a piece of dry wood or cloth to lengthen it. Broken guy wire can replace another piece of wire. To ensure guy wires do not affect the operation of the antenna, the operator should cut the wire into several short lengths and connect the pieces with insulators. Figure H-20 on page 146 shows an example of antenna guy lines and mast repaired with wood.

H-162. If an antenna mast is broken, it may be replaced with another mast of the same length. When long poles are not available as replacements, an operator can overlap and lash shorter poles together with rope or wire to provide a pole of the required length.

##### **Appendix I** 

### **Tactical Radio Platforms** 

This appendix addresses the tactical radio platforms and associated waveforms forces employ at echelons corps and below across the range of military operations. 

##### **SECTION I – OVERVIEW** 

I-1. Army tactical radio platforms traditionally supported a triad of voice and data communications. This triad included SINCGARS, HF, and narrowband (single-channel) TACSAT. The advent of software-defined radios and technological advances such as networking waveforms allow tactical radios to perform multiple communications functions, enable data services down to the user level, and offer far greater flexibility and resilience on the battlefield. Emerging technology allows tactical network operations using a mix of legacy radios and software-defined radios as well as the integration of radios on multiple platforms and components. These radio platform components are a combination of the hardware, which includes antenna, batteries, enduser devices, and the software design inherent in the radio operating system. This diverse asset conglomerate extends communications options and provides greater operational flexibility to suit the commander’s requirements. 

#### **SINGLE-CHANNEL GROUND AND AIRBORNE RADIO SYSTEM** 

I-2. The SINCGARS remains the primary FM voice communications for dismounted and mounted operations as well as joint air-ground coordination. Since its initial fielding in 1986, SINCGARS continues to provide the predominant line of sight radio capability in the DOD, serving as a mainstay for command and control. SINCGARS operates in the 30–88 MHz range providing a frequency hopping capability extending over 2,000 separate channels. SINCGARS supports manpack, handheld, vehicle mounted, and aviation platform mounted systems. Improvements in second and emerging generations of SINCGARS waveforms on software-defined radios will ensure that SINCGARS will remain a significant communications capability. 

I-3. The SINCGARS system improvement program and ASIP increased the performance of the SINCGARS family of radios employing RT-1523. SINCGARS ASIP increases capabilities to support the tactical networking environment, specifically improved data capability, manpower and personnel integration requirement compliance, and flexible interfaces with other systems. SINCGARS ASIP incorporates waveform improvements, optimizations, and enhancements including— 

- ASIP waveform updates. 

- Noisy channel avoidance algorithm optimizations. 

- Time of day enhancement. 

- End of message enhancement. 

I-4. All ASIP radios are capable of remote operation controlled by another ASIP radio up to 4 kilometers (2.4 miles) away, using a twisted pair wire, typically WD-1 or WF-16. To remote a radio, operators use an external two-wire adapter to interface between the radio and the wires. Performance of the remote control feature occurs between the dismounted RT and the vehicular amplifier adapter, or between two dismounted RTs. Another host controller can control the ASIP radio using the external control interface when the ASIP integrates as part of a more extensive system. 

I-5. ASIP extends the functionality of the SINCGARS internet controller card and ancillary equipment. The internet controller card is in the right-hand side of the vehicular amplifier adapter and only needed when the SINCGARS system is operating in packet mode. 

I-6. The vehicular amplifier adapter mounted internet controller card is the predominant communications router for the tactical maneuver platforms participating in a SINCGARS enabled tactical networking environment. The internet controller card uses commercial Internet Protocol services to deliver unicast and multicast data packets. The internet controller card has improved microprocessor with increased memory buffer size and ethernet interface. This feature allows connection of multiple internet controller cards for sharing and dissemination of information in a local area network configuration. ASIP components include— 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**147** 

**Appendix I** 

- SINCGARS internet controller card. 

   - Supports data flow and permits a sufficient flow of information. 

   - Enables sharing of voice and data over the same network via the packet mode. 

   - Generates messages and Internet Protocol addresses of intended receiver from the host computer. 

- SINCGARS ancillary equipment. 

   - Allows intravehicular and external remote radio control. 

   - Provides securable remote control away from the radio. 

I-7. Remote control devices, data fill devices, variable storage transfer devices, and the vehicular intercommunications system are the main categories of ancillary equipment associated with SINCGARS. Remote control devices include— 

- Intravehicular remote-control unit. 

   - Operates with an integrated COMSEC radio. 

   - Operates with non-integrated COMSEC radio. 

- Securable remote-control unit. 

   - Remotes a single radio up to four kilometers (2.4 miles). 

   - Provides an intercommunication function from the radio to the terminal unit and vice versa. 

I-8. COMSEC and data adapter devices attach directly to the securable remote-control unit for secure communications over the transmission line and optimal interface with digital data terminals. The securable remote-control unit replaced the AN/GRA-39. 

#### **BEYOND LINE OF SIGHT AND SATELLITE-ENABLED PLATFORMS** 

I-9. Beyond line of sight and satellite capabilities facilitate secure, reliable information exchange and is an indispensable element for supporting dispersed troops that encounter infrastructure or environmental challenges. Along with familiarity with system features and requirements, Signal personnel and teams should understand key propagation concepts including environmental factors and their effect on transmission before employing beyond line of sight communications. These concepts have a profound impact on antenna choice and site selection and can severely degrade connectivity when not taken into consideration. Also essential is knowledge on specialized satellite terminals and communications equipment such as high-gain antennas, powerful transmitters, and sensitive receivers. 

I-10. To effectively employ space-based capabilities, signal planners must understand system capabilities and how to coordinate access. Users must also adhere to international and national regulatory policies for the use of RF and satellite communications. 

#### **HIGH FREQUENCY SINGLE-SIDEBAND** 

I-11. High frequency (3–30 MHz) radios predominantly support ground forces for both line of sight and beyond line of sight communications using radios with advanced single-sideband and ALE functionality. Army HF use is growing in part due to spectrum availability, updates in Army communications architectures, and improved signaling capability of modernized HF tactical radios. Some Army and joint aircraft still operate legacy HF systems, while the use of wideband HF continues to evolve for data networks and shows significant potential for Army use. HF single-sideband radios provide a terrestrial beyond line of sight tactical radio capability often employed as a backup system to counter jamming or overcome distance limitations without relying on SATCOM. HF radio wave propagation requires an understanding of the radio’s power capabilities, and the frequency used on the radio. The planning distances often dictates which type of antenna to use (whip, near-vertical incidence skywave, or doublet). 

#### **SATELLITE COMMUNICATIONS** 

I-12. U.S. dependence on SATCOM is well recognized by threats who continue to direct their training and assets against U.S. capabilities in efforts to degrade systems and mission objectives. Advanced communications capabilities are available through the combination of hardware, such as a receiver, with a software interface capability that allows satellite signal reception and processing to include real-time position, velocity, navigation, reporting, and timing information. 

**148** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Tactical Radio Platforms** 

I-13. Leaders and planners at all levels will realize even greater mission success as knowledge of satellitebased communications capabilities and the importance of their application are emphasized. This section describes enhanced capabilities in the Army’s inventory that aid in providing secure communications and situational awareness for tactical radio operations. 

#### **BLUE FORCE TRACKER** 

I-14. Blue Force Tracker is a GPS-enabled system that provides commanders and Soldiers with location information about friendly military forces. Using ground-based line of sight radios in operational environments with obstructive terrain can cause network limitations and hinder a commanders’ ability to communicate and provide and maintain situational awareness. To overcome these network limitations, units employ Blue Force Tracker. Typical employment of Blue Force Tracker systems consists of— 

- A computer to display location information. 

- A satellite terminal. 

- An antenna to transmit location information. 

- A GPS receiver to determine position. 

- Software to send and receive orders. 

- Mapping software, usually as a geographic information system that plots the Blue Force Tracker device on a map. 

I-15. The system displays the location of the host vehicle on the computer’s terrain-map display, the locations of other friendly platforms in blue, and the threat in red in their respective locations. Blue Force Tracker sends and receives text and imagery messages and has a mechanism for reporting the locations of threat forces and other battlefield conditions, for example, the location of minefields, battlefield obstacles, and damaged bridges. 

I-16. Additional capability in some Blue Force Tracker devices include route planning tools. By entering grid coordinates, the Blue Force Tracker becomes both the map and compass for motorized units. With proximity warnings enabled, the vehicle crew can maintain awareness as they approach critical or turn points. 

I-17. Blue Force Tracker monitors the location and progress of friendly and threat forces and sends those coordinates to a central location, typically command posts. There the data are consolidated into a common operational picture, and sent to headquarters element, other forces in theater, or back out to other military units for situational awareness. The system also allows users to input or update operational graphics, obstacles, engineer reconnaissance, and threat forces information. Once uploaded, this information is sent to higher headquarters or mailed to other subscribers of that user’s list, or other Blue Force Tracker users within the subscription system. 

##### **JOINT BATTLE COMMAND PLATFORM** 

I-18. The Joint Battle Command Platform is one of the Army’s friendly force tracking systems that equips Soldiers with a faster satellite network, secure data encryption and advanced logistics. Joint Battle Command Platform includes an intuitive interface with features like touch-to-zoom maps and drag-and-drop icons. Joint Battle Command Platform is interoperable with the Nett Warrior handheld device. Joint Battle Command Platform builds on Blue Force Tracker capabilities. 

#### **JOINT CAPABILITIES RELEASE** 

I-19. The Joint Capabilities Release is the next generation of battlefield situational awareness and command and control systems featuring enhanced capabilities. The Joint Capabilities Release tactical network has greater bandwidth, allowing the movement of more information to more users within seconds rather than in minutes. The Joint Capabilities Release system upgrade includes Blue Force Tracker 2, a high-tech, highspeed force-tracking SATCOM network. The Blue Force Tracker 2 is approximately ten times faster than the legacy Blue Force Tracker system. Employment of the Joint Capabilities Release system with the Blue Force Tracker 2 transceiver and network upgrade enables friendly position updates in seconds. 

**31 July 2025** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**149** 

**Appendix I** 

#### **NETWORK MODERNIZATION** 

I-20. Network modernization is a critical enabler to shaping future Army forces and underpins the Army’s modernization priorities. It aligns with core tenets of a multidomain-capable force by— 

- Establishing a unified network to enable multidomain operations. 

- Implementing security and survivability to allow commander’s freedom of action in cyberspace. 

- Reforming processes and policies to improve performance and affordability. 

- Providing network sustainment for a sustainable enterprise and tactical network infrastructure. 

I-21. As the Army evolves its operating concept to meet future requirements, central to its vision is the need for a unified network that extends the range and increases the speed of operations for armed conflicts involving the U.S. forces, its partners, and allies. The ITN enables readiness and will allow multidomaincapable forces to integrate and operate seamlessly and simultaneously across multiple domains and environments, geographics, and warfighting functions and to converge capabilities at the point of need. The ITN includes applications, devices, infrastructure, and network transport to support Army, joint, and coalition forces operating in congested and contested environments. 

##### **INTEGRATED TACTICAL NETWORK** 

I-22. Future U.S. engagements with peer threats require network mobility and continual connectivity. The ITN allows multiple voice and data transport pathways that provide network resiliency and alternate transport capability options while stationary or on-the-move. The network’s ability to provide automatic failover facilitates planners in implementing PACE plan without interaction from the user or manual switchover to select redundant communications means. 

I-23. The ITN is the tactical portion of the unified network, and combines existing tactical network capabilities with emerging commercial capabilities and systems, including non-developmental items. The ITN includes voice and data networks and gateways; tactical cross-domain solutions; small aperture satellite terminals; expeditionary servers; variable height antennas, commercial phone technology; and small satellite terminals with additional capabilities enabling transmission between different formats. ITN supports airground interoperability and enables future technology insertions. 

I-24. ITN contains a suite of high-capacity software-defined tactical radio voice and data communications systems that operate using advanced networking waveforms. Most often employed at brigade and below formations, these radios enable command and control between dispersed forces, offering line of sight and beyond line-of-sight communications over extended ranges. 

I-25. Tactical radios are critical to coordinate with combat enablers such as fires, joint effects, casualty evacuation, and air-ground coordination in real-time, in disparate conditions, at the time of need. 

**_Note._** Only Army formations with the modernized tactical radios (handheld, manpack and small form fit; GEN (generation) II Manpack; and Leader Radios) are scheduled to receive the ITN capability upgrades. The AN/PRC-117G, AN/PRC-152A, AN/PRC-154C, and GEN I Manpack (AN/PRC-155) legacy radios are not cryptographic compliant, nor are they scheduled for ITN modernization based on current fielding guidelines. 

##### **DEVICES, KITS, AND ANCILLARY EQUIPMENT** 

I-26. The integration of military and commercial capabilities that support multiple platforms creates nearcontinuous data sharing between higher and lower echelons providing leaders with a common operational picture that is both accurate and timely. These capabilities, along with additional supporting devices and kits, are critical to maneuver elements’ ability to communicate in a contested environment. 

###### **Nett Warrior End-User Device** 

I-27. The Nett Warrior end-user device is a handheld Android-based application device that provides users simple data services such as situational awareness, automatic position location information, and tactical chat. Nett Warrior allows Soldiers to transition from vehicular platforms to dismounted operations and provides leaders a data environment supporting platoon and company operations. The Nett Warrior end-user device— 

**150** 

**ATP 6-02.53** 

**31 July 2025** 

**Tactical Radio Platforms** 

- Supports National Security Agency-certified (type-1) security architectures and secure but secure but unclassified networks. 

- Allows data categorization in accordance with classification to enable greater information sharing with multinational mission partners. 

- Enables PACE communications in a contested or congested environment with secure data transmission to coalition forces in an unclassified environment. 

- Simplifies network training, set-up, and employment. 

- Enables use of alternate transport, including 4G/LTE. 

- Interoperates with legacy network systems to enable command and control. 

- Provides robust, reliable voice and data services immune to network volatility associated with highly dynamic networks. 

- Establishes connection for mobile wireless hotspot devices. 

- Interfaces with Ruggedized Applications Platform-Tactical Radio’s software for mission planning 

**_Note_ .** The AN/PRC-154A does not meet cryptographic modernization requirements as mandated by the DOD and the National Security Agency and is being replaced by the AN/PRC-148C/D or AN/PRC-163 Leader Radio. 

#### **LEGACY AND ENDURING RADIO PLATFORMS** 

I-28. Most legacy and enduring radio platform are non-digital and operate on an analog infrastructure. Legacy and enduring radio platforms offer consistency of design, reliability, availability, and maintainability at the expense of reduced performance over time and rigidity in the network design of each radio platform type. The primary value of the legacy and enduring radio platforms is immediate availability to meet mission needs and operators’ familiarity and level of training with existing radios. SINCGARS provides the predominant voice capability and offers an electromagnetic counter-countermeasures capability using frequency hopping on any of 2,320 frequencies from 30–88 MHz, with a 25 kHz channel separation. However, legacy SINCGARS lacks adequate electromagnetic counter-countermeasures to counter emerging threat techniques to defeat its first- and second-generation frequency hopping modes. Threats now have an increased ability to detect and intercept SINCGARS voice nets, break transmission security schemes, exploit SINCGARS signals, and profile network stations. Part of this problem exists from the fact that SINCGARS lacks multi-band operation and does not employ spectrum outside of its original 30–88 MHz range. Table I-1 on pages 152 through 164 provides a summary of tactical radios and radio specifications.
